# Body Contact and Battles

> **Rule set:** Written to NHL rules. Where IIHF, USA Hockey, Hockey Canada or typical rec-league
> rules differ in a way that changes how you play, it is flagged inline. This document contains more
> of those flags than any other in this guide, because **whether body checking is legal at all
> depends entirely on which league you are in.** **Playing in Britain? The IIHF flags are yours** —
> every level of UK hockey runs the IIHF book, amended locally; see
> [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md), and read **"If you play in Britain"** in §1
> before you play a shift.
>
> **Related:** [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md) · [Defending the Rush](../systems/defending_the_rush.md) · [Skating](skating.md) · [Puck Handling](puck_handling.md) · [Defender](../positions/defender.md) · [Risk Management](../hockey-iq/risk_management.md) · [Equipment](../off-the-ice/equipment.md)

## Overview

Most hockey games are decided not by pretty passing plays but by who wins the scrappy, three-second contests for a loose puck along the wall, in the corner and at the front of the net. This document is about winning those without getting hurt, ejected or penalised.

**Find out what your league allows before you play.** Body *contact* — two players leaning on each other while both chase the same puck — is legal everywhere. A body *check*, deliberately using your body to separate an opponent from the puck, is a different act that many leagues ban outright: younger age groups, most adult recreational leagues, and all girls' and women's play under USA Hockey and Hockey Canada — though the IIHF rulebook, which Britain plays under, permits a restricted form instead. **If you play in Britain, no rulebook answers this**, so ask your league and get it in writing. And "non-check" does not mean "non-contact" — USA Hockey says *"Non-check hockey does not mean no contact."*

**Nearly all of this is legal wherever you play.** *Angling* — steering an opponent by the route you skate rather than by hitting them — is legal everywhere. So is the *stick lift*: your blade under their stick, **below their bottom hand**, lifting their blade off the ice. Take it below the hand in every book, because the books diverge above it — the IIHF's says a stick against or near the opponent's hands is hooking and USA Hockey's hooking rule reaches a stick on the hand or arm. **The NHL is not the exception it looks like, and the slashing route to the hands is not the NHL's alone:** the NHL's *hooking* rule writes no hands sentence, but a forceful chop on or near the hands *"that, in the judgment of the Referee, is not an attempt to play the puck"* is **slashing** under NHL Rule 61.1 — and IIHF Rule 61.1 is that sentence word for word, while USA Hockey Rule 634 is a shortened form of it that drops the *"attempt to play the puck"* let-out entirely. A clean lift for the puck is not a chop and sits outside all three; a chop at the hands is inside all three — and a chop at the hands can reach a **major plus a game misconduct with nobody injured** in all four books (NHL 61.3 with 61.5, IIHF 61.3, USA Hockey 634(b), Hockey Canada 9.3(b)), where a hooking major needs an injury under the NHL (55.3) and Hockey Canada (8.2(b)) but not under the IIHF (55.3) or USA Hockey (623(b)), whose hooking rules reach that same tier for recklessly endangering an opponent. Only the section on delivering a check is restricted.

**Both sides of a collision have a rule that does not bend.** Never hit a player from behind, never hit one turned toward the wall, and never make contact with an opponent's head, face or neck. Checking from behind is a major penalty with automatic ejection and no lesser option, under both the NHL's rulebook and the IIHF's, and the onus is on the player delivering it — USA Hockey's Rule 608 and Hockey Canada's rule say so in those words, while the NHL's checking-from-behind rule states no onus at all. Where the NHL does write one down, in its **boarding** rule, it is not strict liability: that rule weighs the onus against whether the opponent made themself vulnerable and whether the check was avoidable at all — and **the same balancing clause is in the IIHF's boarding rule (41.1), which is the British reader's, and in Hockey Canada's (7.2), which carries no division scope**, while **USA Hockey's boarding rule (603) states its onus and attaches no balance to it at all**. **Play it as though it were absolute anyway** — those clauses are written for boarding, and the books that state an onus for checking from behind attach no "however" to it at all. (Hockey Canada does provide a minor for checking from behind, but never a bare one: Rule 7.5(a) assesses a minor **and** a game misconduct together.) **The head is not a matter of degree either, and a player facing you is not a licence to hit high** — IIHF Rule 48.1: *"There is no clean check to the head or neck… This rule supersedes all similar actions regarding hits to the head and neck."* USA Hockey Rule 620 reaches any contact to the head, face or neck with any part of the body, equipment or stick, and its Standard of Play puts the onus on the checker *"regardless as to size differential, to not make contact in the head/neck area of the opponent."* And never turn your back to the wall yourself, however protective that feels, and never duck. Head up, chin off your chest — and when the wall is coming and you cannot avoid it, meet it with your skates parallel to the boards and take the contact on your forearm and hip. **Those are two prohibitions against two different injuries.** A back turned makes the contact a hit from behind, and USA Hockey's warning on those is that *"even a light hit from behind could inflict a severe head or neck injury."* A chin tucked to the chest straightens the cervical spine, and it is the **injury** from that head-down, head-first collision with the boards or a goal post — not the turn — that USA Hockey says *"can occur at walking speed."* Turning into a hit deliberately to draw the call earns you nothing either: both books waive the checking-from-behind penalty when the turn was intentional, and the IIHF adds that other penalties may still be assessed.

**If a player may have hurt their head or neck, nobody moves and somebody phones.** Do not help them up.

---

## 1. Which League Are You Actually In?

Before you read another word, find out which category your league falls into. Getting this wrong is how good players end up in the penalty box, and how careless players hurt someone.

### The two categories

```facts
Key: Body contact and body checking are not the same thing — one is legal everywhere, the other is not
Rule: Hockey Canada defines body contact as incidental contact of two opposing players pursuing the puck in the same direction — no penalty
Rule: USA Hockey defines a body check as intentional physical contact by a skater to an opponent in control of the puck
Rule: Hockey Canada's mechanical test for a check — opposite-directional force, leaving your skating lane, or extending an arm, shoulder or hip after angling
Key: Body contact is legal in every league at every age; body checking is legal only in specific classifications
```

**Body contact** is incidental or positional contact between two players who are both playing the puck — leaning on each other, rubbing shoulders as you skate the same direction, boxing someone out at the net front, sealing a player off along the wall. Hockey Canada's rulebook defines body contact as "incidental contact of two opposing players in pursuit of the puck on the ice in the same direction" and states plainly that no penalty is assessed for it. **Body contact is legal in every league, at every age, in every division.**

**Body checking** is a deliberate act: you use your body to separate an opponent from the puck. USA Hockey defines a body check as "intentional physical contact, from the front, diagonally from the front or straight from the side, by a skater to an opponent who is in control of the puck." Hockey Canada adds the mechanical test — body checking occurs "when a player makes deliberate contact with an opposing player with opposite-directional force, when a player leaves their skating lane to make contact, or when a player extends an arm, shoulder, or hip after angling an opponent." **Body checking is legal only in specific classifications.**

### Where body checking is and is not permitted

```facts
Rule: Body checking is banned at 12U and below, in all girls'/women's classifications, and in all non-check adult classifications (USA Hockey Rule 604(a))
Rule: Hockey Canada bans body checking at U13 and below, throughout female hockey, and in "any other divisions approved by a Member of Hockey Canada" — so being above U13 does not settle it (Hockey Canada Rule 7.3)
Rule: When a checking team plays a non-checking team, body checking is prohibited outright (USA Hockey Rule 604(b))
Never: Assume your league permits checking — a local governing body can ban it in any classification, and adult leagues in checking countries very often are non-check
Rule: CARHA's book has no checking division at all — 49(a) makes a body check a minor or a major anywhere on the ice, for intentionally bodying, pushing or shoving an opponent, and a major where the contact injures (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: CARHA 49(c) assesses no penalty for accidental contact, so the line there is intent rather than touch — but 49(a) also reaches a player who "does not avert body contact with an opponent" (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: A CARHA major for a body check ends your night — Rule 30(a) rules it off the ice for the rest of the game and Rule 32(d) adds an automatic one-game suspension; both carve out the accidental high stick and nothing else (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Rule: CARHA Rule 32(a) ejects you from the game on three penalties of any kind in one game — in a book where every intentional bump is a minor, three of them is your night (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
Key: "Non-check" does not mean "non-contact" — USA Hockey says a Competitive Contact game can be very physical
```

| League / level | Body checking? | Source |
|---|---|---|
| **NHL** | Yes | NHL rulebook |
| **USA Hockey — 14U and older boys'/men's check classifications** | Yes | USA Hockey Rule 604(a) |
| **USA Hockey — 12U and below** | **No** | USA Hockey Rule 604(a) |
| **USA Hockey — all Girls'/Women's classifications** | **No** | USA Hockey Rule 604(a) |
| **USA Hockey — all non-check Adult classifications** | **No** | USA Hockey Rule 604(a) |
| **Hockey Canada — U13 and below** | **No** | Hockey Canada Rule 7.3 |
| **Hockey Canada — female hockey** | **No** | Hockey Canada Rule 7.3 |
| **Hockey Canada — any division a Member of Hockey Canada has approved** | **No** | Hockey Canada Rule 7.3 (the rulebook does not list which. Ask your league) |
| **Hockey Canada — U15 and above (male)** | Yes, **unless** your division is one of those Member-approved above | Hockey Canada Rule 7.3 |
| **IIHF women's international** | Restricted — see below | IIHF Rule 101.1 |
| **Women's hockey in Britain (EIH / SIHA / BUIHA)** | Restricted, same as IIHF | IIHF Rule 101.1, unamended by the In-House Rules |
| **PWHL** | Restricted — see below | PWHL Rule 52.1 |
| **British junior and men's/mixed hockey** | **Neither book states an age — see below** | IIHF Rule Book and In-House Rules are silent |
| **Most adult rec / beer leagues** | Usually **no**. Check your league | — |

Five things that catch people out:

- **Under Hockey Canada, being above U13 does not mean you are in a checking division.** Rule 7.3 has a third limb people miss: it applies "only in divisions of U13 and below, in female hockey, **and any other divisions approved by a Member of Hockey Canada**." The rulebook does not say which divisions those are, so a U15 or adult division can be non-check by approval and you will not find it in the playing rules. Ask your league.
- **A local governing body can ban checking in any classification.** USA Hockey's rule says so explicitly and encourages associations to offer non-check options "at all levels of recreational play." An adult men's league in a checking country is very often non-check. Do not assume.
- **When a checking team plays a non-checking team, nobody checks.** USA Hockey Rule 604(b) prohibits body checking outright in that game.
- **"Non-check" does not mean "non-contact."** USA Hockey states it in as many words: "Non-check hockey does not mean no contact and the Competitive Contact Category game can be very physical."
- **One published adult book answers the question for you, and its answer is no.** If your league runs the **CARHA Hockey** rule book — **CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only, and no reader in minor hockey** — you are not in a checking division, because that book has none. **Rule 49(a)** assesses *"[a] Minor or Major penalty, at the discretion of the Referee, based upon the intent and the violence of the impact with the opposing player… to any player who intentionally bodies, pushes, shoves, stands in front of an opponent for the purpose of making contact, and/or does not avert body contact with an opponent. When injury results from intentional body contact a Major penalty shall be assessed."* The book's own case situation prices a bodycheck at **centre ice** — nowhere near the boards, nobody hurt — at a minor or a major, and one **into the boards** at a double minor under **49(b)**, a major if the player is injured, and a match penalty if the referee judges the incident serious. Its referee's-signal page prints a single signal for *"Boarding and Body Checking"*, and **49(d)** adds a minor or a major for deliberate physical contact after the whistle. ⚠️ **What that book does not ban is contact as such** — **49(c)** provides that *"[w]here, in the opinion of the Referee, accidental contact has taken place, no penalty shall be assessed"*, so the line it draws is intent rather than touch, and battling for a loose puck is not what it is aimed at. But read 49(a)'s third limb before you decide how much room that leaves: it reaches a player who *"does not avert body contact with an opponent"*, which is a duty to get out of the way rather than merely a ban on initiating. *(That the third limb is a duty is a reading of the rule's grammar; the book does not gloss it, and 49(c) is what keeps genuinely accidental contact outside it.)* ⚠️ **And a major there is the end of your night, plus the next game:** **Rule 30(a)** rules any player assessed a major *"off the ice for the remainder of the game (Major penalty plus Game Misconduct)"* — its only exception in the whole book is an accidental high stick — and **Rule 32(d)** adds an automatic one-game suspension to that game misconduct. **Rule 32(a)** ejects you from the game on **three penalties of any kind**, so in a book where every intentional bump is a minor, three of them is your night. CARHA binds CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only and is not a general rec-hockey standard; it is set out here because it is a published answer to the question this section asks, in the kind of league many adult readers actually play in. Ask your league whether it is your book.

### If you play in Britain

```facts
Rule: Your book is the IIHF Rule Book, amended by the In-House Rules — the USA Hockey and Hockey Canada rows above are not yours
Rule: Neither the IIHF Rule Book nor the In-House Rules states an age at which body checking becomes legal — the age bandings above do not transfer
Never: Assume Britain mirrors USA Hockey's 12U or Hockey Canada's U13 — nothing in either British source says so
Action: Get the contact status of your division in writing from your league or club before your first shift
Rule: British junior categories are U19/U16/U14/U12/U10 by birth year and unisex, "not classified as 'Mens' within the UK" (In-House Rules, Rule 200)
Rule: In women's hockey under EIH, Adult runs from the 14th birthday and Under 16 from the 10th (In-House Rules, Rule 100.1)
Rule: In British women's hockey at every age, adult included, any major penalty carries an automatic game misconduct and ejection — In-House Rule 101's ejection bullet applies "in all cases" and states no age scope (In-House Rules, Rule 101)
Rule: The age-scoped half of In-House Rule 101 is its other bullet, which applies IIHF Rule 201.1 to non-adult women's categories — it does not narrow the ejection bullet beside it
Key: Read junior hockey the same way at every age, U19 to U10 — In-House Rule 201 scopes the IIHF junior rules to all junior groups without naming 201.1, so treat a major as ending the game
```

Every age banding for body checking just set out is North American — they are USA Hockey's and Hockey Canada's — and a British reader has no way to map those onto their own game. Of the levels listed, the IIHF one and the two British ones are written for you; the rest are not. Here is what maps and what does not.

**Your book is the IIHF Rule Book**, adopted as the foundation of the game in England and Scotland by England Ice Hockey, the SIHA and the BUIHA, with the **In-House Rules** sitting on top of it as amendments. So the IIHF rows and flags in this document — Rule 43 on checking from behind, Rule 42 on charging, Rule 48 on head contact, Rule 101.1 on women's hockey — are describing your game. The USA Hockey and Hockey Canada rows are not. See [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md) for the full amendment list.

> **⚠️ Neither book states an age at which body checking becomes legal in Britain, and this document will not invent one.**
>
> The **IIHF Rule Book** contains no body-checking age restriction at all. It defines a legal body check in its glossary — *"a Player checks an opponent who is in possession of the puck, by using their hip or body from the front, diagonally from the front or straight from the side… only with the trunk of the body (hips and shoulders) and must be above the opponent's knees and at or below the shoulders"* — and restricts checking in exactly one place: **Rule 101.1**, in women's hockey. Its junior section (**Rules 200–202**) covers age eligibility for the U20 and U18 World Championships, facial protection, and one playing rule — **201.1**, that any major penalty in men's junior also brings an automatic game misconduct. **There is nothing about contact.**
>
> The **In-House Rules** do not add one. Their junior section (**Rules 200–201**) sets the British age categories and then says, in its entirety, that the IIHF junior playing rules *"apply to all junior age groups as defined in Rule 200 of the in-house rules."* Their women's section (**Rules 100–102**) sets the age boundaries, applies IIHF Rule 201.1 to *"all non-adult age categories of Women's Hockey"*, ejects any player assessed a major *"in all cases"* — that one carries no age scope — and restricts half visors. **Neither section mentions body checking, body contact, or non-contact play — and nor does any other section of the document.**
>
> **So do not assume Britain mirrors USA Hockey's 12U line or Hockey Canada's U13 line.** It might in practice, and it might not; the point is that no source this guide has read says so, and guessing on this particular question is how someone gets hurt. **Where the answer actually lives is your competition's own regulations** — the In-House Rules note that EIH and SIHA *"may also issue Rule Bulletins from time to time"*, and each league sets its own competition regulations separately from the playing rules. Ask your club or league secretary, and get it in writing.

**The age bandings themselves are different, so the North American numbers do not even translate.** British junior categories are set by **birth year**, not by the North American banding used elsewhere in this document: **U19, U16, U14, U12 and U10** (In-House Rules, Rule 200). The In-House Rules add a note that matters for anyone reading a USA Hockey "boys'/men's check classification" row and looking for its British equivalent: *"Junior age categories are unisex and are not classified as 'Mens' within the UK."* There is no British "boys' checking division" to map onto. In women's hockey, **Adult runs from the player's 14th birthday** and Under 16 from the 10th to the 16th (Rule 100.1).

**One consequence to plan around, and it is not confined to the age groups:** in British **women's** hockey at **every** age, adult included, **any foul that draws a major penalty also draws an automatic game misconduct and ejection.** In-House Rule 101 carries that as a bullet of its own — *"Any player who commits a foul that is assessed a Major Penalty will, **in all cases**, also receive an additional Game Misconduct Penalty and be ejected from the game"* — and it states no age scope at all. The bullet above it in the same rule, which applies IIHF Rule 201.1 to *"all non-adult age categories of Women's Hockey"*, is a separate provision and does not narrow it: the two are siblings under a heading that reads *"Women's Hockey Specific Playing Rules"*, so the heading is the only scope the ejection bullet has. For **junior** hockey, In-House Rule 201 scopes the IIHF junior playing rules to *"all junior age groups"* without naming 201.1, so read the same consequence across U19 to U10 as an interpretation rather than the rule's own words. Either way, treat a major for charging, boarding or checking from behind as ending the game, and confirm with your league.

### Women's hockey — the nuance that is usually got wrong

```facts
Key: "Women's hockey has no hitting" is too blunt — the answer depends on which book you are under
Rule: USA Hockey and Hockey Canada prohibit body checking in all girls'/women's classifications
Rule: IIHF women's rules allow bodychecking only where there is clear intention of playing or gaining possession of the puck, and subject to the exception the rule itself then sets out (IIHF Rule 101.1)
Rule: Two players in pursuit of the puck may push and lean into each other only while "possession of the puck" remains "the sole object" of both — leaning on after that is an illegal hit (IIHF Rule 101.1)
Rule: The PWHL permits angling with the body to separate an opponent from the puck (PWHL Rule 52.1)
Never: Step or glide into an opponent, or move into a puck carrier trying to avoid you — that is an illegal hit
Rule: IIHF women's rules forbid using the boards to eliminate, push or pin an opponent (IIHF Rule 101.1)
Rule: A stationary player is entitled to their ice, and a puck carrier skating directly at her must avoid contact (IIHF Rule 101.1)
Rule: An illegal hit in women's hockey is a minor, or a major plus an automatic game misconduct (IIHF Rule 101.1)
Rule: In Britain IIHF Rule 101.1 is the operative bodychecking rule, unamended — but the 14th-birthday Adult threshold is not in it and not in IIHF Rule 100.1 either; it is a domestic amendment (In-House Rules, Rule 100.1)
Rule: IIHF Rule 100.1 sets women's Senior at eighteen by 31 December of the year the Championship Season ends, with a waiver for a 16th or 17th birthday that year, and Under 18 at fifteen to eighteen — four years above the British Adult line
```

The common statement "women's hockey has no hitting" is too blunt. What is accurate:

- **USA Hockey and Hockey Canada prohibit body checking in all girls'/women's classifications.** That part is simple and absolute.
- **IIHF women's rules permit a restricted form of bodychecking — and this is the rule women's hockey in Britain plays under.** IIHF Rule 101.1 states that in women's hockey, *"'bodychecking' is allowed when there is a clear intention of playing the puck or attempting to 'gain possession' of the puck"*, and subject to the exception the rule itself then sets out. Two players in pursuit of the puck *"are reasonably allowed to push and lean into each other provided that 'possession of the puck' remains the sole object of the two (2) Players."* What is banned is the *hit*: *"Any move by a Player to step or glide into an opposing Player will be assessed at least a minor penalty (2') for an 'illegal hit'"*, and the rule provides either a minor **or** a major plus an automatic game misconduct. Three further clauses are worth knowing exactly, because they decide most real contests:
  - **The boards.** Where two or more players are competing for possession, *"they are not allowed to use the boards to make contact with an opponent to eliminate her from the play, push her into the boards, or pin her along the boards."*
  - **Standing your ground is protected.** *"A Player, who is stationary, is entitled to that area of the ice. It is up to the opponent to avoid body contact with such a Player… the opponent is obliged to skate around the stationary Player."* And: *"Players are allowed to 'hold their ground' any time that they have established their position on the ice."* Everything in Section 4 of this document is available to you.
  - **The obligation runs both ways.** *"If a Player with the puck is skating directly at an opponent who is stationary, it is the obligation of the puck carrier to 'avoid contact'."* But if the carrier makes every effort to avoid contact and the stationary player moves into her, it is the *defender* who takes at least a minor.
- **The PWHL permits the same restricted form.** PWHL Rule 52.1: "In the PWHL, a form of Bodychecking is permitted. Players may angle their opponent by using their body in order to separate her opponent from the puck." Same-direction contact that dislodges the puck is legal; "opposite-directional force," leaving your skating lane to make contact, or extending an arm, shoulder or hip *after* angling are all illegal hits.

**In Britain, Rule 101.1 is your rule as written — the In-House Rules do not amend it.** What they do amend is who counts as an adult and what a major costs: **Adult women's hockey runs from the player's 14th birthday**, Under 16 from the 10th to the 16th, and — **at every age, adult included** — **any foul assessed a major penalty also carries an automatic game misconduct and ejection** (In-House Rules, Rules 100.1 and 101, whose ejection bullet applies *"in all cases"* and states no age scope). So an illegal hit serious enough to draw the major under 101.1 ends your game whatever your age. Do not read the Adult line as an exemption from it: what the 14th birthday changes is which age category you are in, not what a major costs you.

**That 14 is British, not international — do not cite it to the IIHF.** The IIHF's own Rule 100.1 defines its women's categories only by World Championship eligibility: "Senior" needs a player at least eighteen by 31 December of the year the Championship Season ends, with a waiver route for a 16th or 17th birthday in that year, and "Under 18" runs from fifteen to eighteen on the same date. Nothing in it addresses a fourteen-year-old in a domestic league, and Rule 101.1 — the bodychecking rule itself — carries no age at all. The threshold is the In-House Rules', and it sits four years below the IIHF's Senior line, which is why it is worth knowing whose rule it is. See [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md).

In practice, the women's-game standard is very close to the standard for **legal angling everywhere** — which is the subject of the next section.

### What this means for how you read the rest of this document

```facts
Key: Almost everything here applies in every league — angling, stick checks, body position, receiving a hit safely, battles, shot blocking
Priority: Two things need a full-checking league — Section 5, delivering a legal body check, and step 3 of the net-front box-out in Section 9, driving an opponent off their spot
Rule: Women's hockey is not a full-checking league — IIHF Rule 101.1 allows bodychecking only with a clear intention of playing the puck, and makes any move to step or glide into an opponent an illegal hit
Rule: A third thing is scoped, to the women's game — the pin along the boards in Sections 7 and 8, which IIHF Rule 101.1 bars; there you may pin the puck, not the player
Never: Treat anything here as a licence to hit, push or shove an opponent in a league that does not allow it
Key: Holding a position you have established is written as legal in three of the four books — moving an opponent off theirs is not
Rule: The NHL and the IIHF write it at Rule 56.1 and USA Hockey in the Note to 604(c); Hockey Canada's book has no such sentence — its only mention of body position, Rule 8.1, assumes it rather than granting it
```

Almost everything here applies in **every league, including yours** — angling, stick checks, body position, receiving a hit safely, board and corner and net-front battles, loose-puck races, shot blocking and staying out of the box. **Three things are not.** The first is the section on **delivering a legal body check** (§5), which applies only if your league permits **full** body checking — women's hockey does not, and its own restricted standard is the one set out in §1. The second is a single step inside the net-front box-out (§9): **driving an opponent off their spot — walking them out from under their arms — is a push, and it needs a full-checking league.** In the books that bar checking it is a minor — USA Hockey Rule 604(c), and Hockey Canada Rule 7.3(a) in *"divisions of U13 and below, in female hockey, and any other divisions approved by a Member of Hockey Canada"* — **and neither of those minors is the ceiling in its own book**: USA Hockey **604(d)** is a major plus a game misconduct for a player who *"recklessly endangers an opponent with a body check in a Competitive Contact category"* and **604(e)** a match penalty, while Hockey Canada **7.3(b)** reaches that same pair *"At the discretion of the Referee, based on the degree of violence of the impact"* — nobody has to be hurt — and makes it compulsory where the check injures, with **7.3(c)** a match penalty. **In women's hockey, where checking is restricted rather than barred, it is an illegal hit under IIHF Rule 101.1** — the walk-out is not an effort to play the puck, a stationary player *"is entitled to that area of the ice"*, and *"any move by a Player to step or glide into an opposing Player will be assessed at least a minor penalty (2') for an 'illegal hit'"*, with a major (5') plus an automatic game misconduct above it. **The third is scoped to the women's game rather than to checking:** pinning an opponent along the boards (§7, and the corner in §8, which is a board battle) is barred by IIHF Rule 101.1, which forbids two or more players competing for the puck from using the boards *"to make contact with an opponent to eliminate her from the play, push her into the boards, or pin her along the boards"* — there you may pin the puck, not the player. All three are flagged where they appear. The principle underneath is the one to carry: holding a position you have already established is not what these rules penalise; moving an opponent off theirs is. Nothing in this document is a licence to hit in a league that does not allow it.

---

## 2. Angling — Legal in Every League

**Angling is steering an opponent to where you want them by choosing your skating route, not by hitting them.** USA Hockey's glossary defines it as "a legal defensive skill used to direct/control the puck carrier to an area that closes the gap and creates an opening that is too small for the puck carrier."

It is legal everywhere. Hockey Canada's own interpretation of its no-checking rule says it outright: "A player may steer or direct an opposing player into the boards, without actually touching or body-checking them. This is acceptable." You are allowed to close off the boards and force a puck carrier to slow down or turn back inside — as long as you do not bump, push or shove them to do it.

This is how good defenders actually take the puck. Not by hitting people.

⚠️ **The picture below is borrowed from [Defending the Rush](../systems/defending_the_rush.md), and the distances in its caption belong there rather than here.** They are gap-control targets for a defender retreating on a one-on-one. This section sets no distance of its own: what carries across is the geometry, and nothing numeric.

![](diagram:rush-gap-and-angle)

It is the same *principle* rather than the same skill — that defender is travelling backward on a rush, where the four things below are written for closing on a carrier in open ice. What carries across is the geometry: the defender's body is between the carrier and the middle, the shaded ice is what the route takes away, and the only lane left open is the wall. What it does not show — because no still picture can — is that the defender was already at speed before that instant. **A gap cannot be closed from a standstill** — which is the same reason an angle has to start before you need it.

### The four things you are managing

```facts
Action: Skate at the ice they want, not at the player. Take away their good ice and force them to bad ice
Position: Approach on an angle that leaves your momentum heading back toward your own net, not sideways across the ice
Technique: Arrive under control — slow your feet as the gap shrinks, knees bent so you can still change direction
Never: Cross over as you close — a crossover commits your weight, and a good attacker cuts back against it
Technique: Stick on the ice in a sweeping manner, blade covering the inside lane while your body covers the outside
Goal: A gap too small for the puck carrier to cut inside
```

**1. Your route, not their route.** Skate at the ice they want, not at the player. If they are trying to get to the middle, your line takes the middle away and leaves them the wall. Coaches call the middle **good ice** and the outside **bad ice**; the whole aim is to make bad ice the only ice available. USA Hockey's own coaching write-up puts the objective as *"to take away an opponent's good ice and force them to bad ice"* — that sentence is the article's, not a quotation from anyone; the piece quotes Seth Appert, head coach of the U.S. National Under-18 Team at its National Team Development Program, separately and in his own words below.

**2. Momentum back toward your own net.** Approach on an angle that leaves you travelling *back* toward the goal you are defending, not sideways across the ice. Appert: "When you're skating to take away time and space from your opponent, one thing you need to have for the proper angle is momentum back toward your net." The reason is simple — if they get rid of the puck, you are already going the right way and you stay above them (between them and your net). If you angled flat across the ice and they beat you, you are now chasing.

**3. Closing speed.** Arrive under control. If you close at full speed, one inside cut beats you and you are 20 feet out of the play. Slow your feet as the gap shrinks and keep your knees bent so you can change direction. The gap you are aiming for is small enough that a cut inside is not available — that is what "creates an opening that is too small for the puck carrier" means.

- **Do not cross over as you close.** A crossover commits your weight to one direction, and a good attacker cuts back against a committed defender. Stay on an inside edge through the last few strides so you can still change direction. (Crossovers are for *building* the speed that gets you to the angle, not for arriving on it — see [Skating](skating.md).)

**4. Stick on the ice, blade in the passing lane.** Appert again: "you want your stick on the ice in a sweeping manner and protecting good ice." Your stick covers the inside — the lane back to the middle — while your body covers the outside. At the moment of contact, "if your opponent still has the puck, you want your stick on the puck."

### Where the angle ends

```facts
Action: In a non-checking league, end the angle with a seal — take away the last of their space and arrive stick-on-puck
Key: Both leaning, both playing the puck, both going the same direction is legal competitive contact
Action: In a checking league, the same angle ends with a rub-out or a check under the Section 5 rules
Priority: The angle comes first, the contact is the last step
Never: Throw a hit without an angle — a hit from bad position is where most penalties come from
```

In a **non-checking league**, the angle ends with a seal: your body position takes away the last of their space, you arrive stick-on-puck, and you either take it or force a dump. Contact happens — you are both leaning, both playing the puck, both going the same direction — and that is legal competitive contact.

In a **checking league**, the same angle ends with a rub-out or a check, delivered under the rules in Section 5. Note the order: **the angle comes first, the contact is the last step.** A hit thrown without an angle is a hit thrown from bad position, which is where most penalties come from.

**Angling is a skating skill before it is a defensive skill.** See [Skating](skating.md) for the crossovers, pivots and edge control it demands, and [Defending the Rush](../systems/defending_the_rush.md) for how angling combines with gap control on an oncoming attack.

---

## 3. Stick Checks — Legal, Effective, and Under-Used

A **stick check** is any attempt to win the puck with your stick rather than your body. All four below are legal skills in every league — but *where* you take them decides whether they stay legal, and the books do not draw that line in the same place. The rule most players are taught is this:

> **⚠️ Stick on the puck is legal. Stick on their hands or their body is a penalty.**

⚠️ **You will usually hear a third clause in the middle of that — "or stick on their stick" — and it is a two-book rule, not a universal one.** It comes from NHL Rule 55.1 (Hooking): "When a player is checking another in such a way that there is only stick-to-stick contact, such action is not to be penalized as hooking." The IIHF carries that sentence in the same words, bar a capitalised *"Player"* and quotation marks around *"hooking"*. 🇬🇧 **IIHF Rule 55.1 also carries a second sentence the NHL's does not, printed immediately before that one:** *"If the stick goes against the opponent's hands / or near the opponent's hands, it shall be penalized as 'hooking'."* It is in both current editions — 2025/26 Version 1.1 and 2026/27 — and it names the hands with no condition attached at all, so the book a British player is actually under is the plainest of the four on this point — though the stick-to-stick sentence beside it pulls the other way, and the IIHF reconciles neither, which is set out under [The stick lift](#the-stick-lift) below. Note the condition even there: it exempts a player **checking** an opponent, not stick-on-stick contact generally. **USA Hockey and Hockey Canada run the other way** — both name the opponent's stick as a hookable surface, "any part of an opponent's body or stick" (USA Hockey Rule 623's Note; Hockey Canada Rule 8.2). So under two of the four books "it was only stick-on-stick" is not a defence. **The stick lift survives all four, but on different footings.** Hockey Canada permits it expressly — Interpretation 1 to 8.2(a), a lift *"for the purpose of making a play on the puck"* *"will be allowed"*. **USA Hockey permits it too, in its Casebook, and on a broader footing than Hockey Canada's** — asked whether a player may lift an opponent's stick without being penalised under the Standard of Play, it answers *"Yes. A player may lift the opponent's stick at any time provided they do not impede their progress"*, and calls it *"a good defensive play"* (Standard of Play, Situation 2). ⚠️ **Read what each permission is conditioned on, because it is not the same thing:** Hockey Canada's turns on your *purpose* — a play on the puck — and USA Hockey's on the *effect*, that you do not impede their progress. **Neither is permission to take it at the hands**: Rule 623 still needs *"a pulling or tugging motion"* and still reaches a stick on the hand or arm, and the Casebook's Rule 623, Situation 1 opens by ruling that hooking an opponent's stick *is* a penalty before it allows the lift — and under the IIHF book it is the hands sentence above that catches you, on the location alone, with no pulling, tugging or effect to be shown. Take it low. And NHL Rule 61.1 (Slashing): "Any forceful or powerful chop with the stick on an opponent's body, the opponent's stick, or on or near the opponent's hands that, in the judgment of the Referee, is not an attempt to play the puck, shall be penalized as slashing."

Read that second one carefully. Chopping *at their stick* is still slashing if it is a chop rather than an attempt to play the puck. The difference is intent and violence, and referees are watching for it.

### The poke check

```facts
Technique: A short, straight jab of the blade at the puck, one hand or two, from a controlled distance
Read: Use it when you are square to a carrier with the puck out in front — receiving, looking up, or beginning a deke
Technique: Top hand on the knob, extend from the shoulder — do not lunge with your whole body
Action: Poke, recover, stay skating
Never: Poke from too far away — if your blade cannot reach the puck without lunging, you are too far away
```

**What it is:** A short, straight jab of the blade at the puck, one hand or two, from a controlled distance.

**When it works:** When you are square to the puck carrier and they have the puck out in front of them — receiving a pass, looking up, or beginning a deke. It is also the natural finish to an angle, when the carrier has run out of room.

**How to do it:** Keep your top hand on the knob and extend from the shoulder; do not lunge with your whole body. The moment you commit your feet, a good carrier steps around your stick and you have taken yourself out of the play. Poke, recover, stay skating.

**The mistake:** Poking from too far away, missing, and being beaten. If your blade cannot reach the puck without you lunging, you are too far away.

### The stick lift

```facts
Technique: Blade under their shaft low, below their bottom hand and a hand's width clear of the glove — lift crisply up, not sideways
Action: Step through as their blade comes off the ice and take the puck
Read: Lift late — as they are about to shoot, receive or move the puck, not while they are just carrying it
Read: A stick lift on an opponent bent low over the puck goes up at a face down at stick height — catching that face is a high stick however low your hands were. Take it under the shaft, or leave it alone
Position: Works from behind and beside an opponent, at the net front, in a board battle and on a backcheck
Never: Take the lift on a player who does not have the puck — what makes it legal is that it plays the puck; on anyone else it is restraint
Rule: Stick-to-stick is exempt from hooking only in the NHL and IIHF books, and only while checking (55.1) — USA Hockey 623 and Hockey Canada 8.2 name the opponent's stick as a hookable surface
Rule: IIHF 55.1 makes a stick against, or near, the opponent's hands hooking; its next sentence exempts stick-to-stick contact while checking, and the book reconciles neither. Below their bottom hand the two sentences never meet — no contact at the hands for the first to catch. Low is still the answer
Rule: The NHL's hooking rule writes no hands sentence but its slashing rule does — and so do IIHF 61.1, word for word, and USA Hockey 634, which drops the "attempt to play the puck" let-out: the hands are named in three of the four books, and Hockey Canada 9.3 reaches a chop there without naming them
Rule: Hockey Canada answers the missed stick lift directly, in junior and senior only: Interpretation 2 to Rule 9.5(a), item ii, makes an attempted lift that accidentally contacts the head or neck and causes injury a double minor. In minor and female hockey it is 7.6(a), a minor with no injury needed
```

**What it is:** You place your blade under the shaft of their stick, **below the bottom hand and with a clear gap above it**, and lift their blade off the ice — then take the puck out from underneath it.

**Why it is worth learning:**

- It takes the puck with **far less** penalty risk than a poke check (tripping) or a body check (a dozen possible penalties). **Not none — and no book here leaves the hands unprotected, the NHL included. But only three of the four reach a *lift* at the hands; the NHL's hands provision sits in its slashing rule and catches a chop.** 🇬🇧 IIHF Rule 55.1 carries a sentence the NHL's does not — *"If the stick goes against the opponent's hands / or near the opponent's hands, it shall be penalized as 'hooking'"* — and that is the book a British player is under. ⚠️ **Read 55.1's very next sentence too, because the book does not reconcile the two:** *"When a Player is checking another in such a way that there is only stick-to-stick contact, such action is not to be penalized as 'hooking'."* A lift taken under the shaft is stick-to-stick contact and nothing else, so the two sentences pull against each other, and **no IIHF interpretation reconciling them is held here.** Note the scope of the exemption even so — it covers a player **checking** an opponent, not stick-on-stick contact generally. Reading the hands sentence as the operative one is the cautious choice and the one this document makes; it may be stricter than the rule actually is. **That is not a reason to take the lift high.** An unresolved rule gets resolved on the night by the referee in front of you, and the reading that costs you nothing is the low one — below their bottom hand the two sentences never meet, because there is no contact at the hands for the first one to catch. **Low is still the answer.** **USA Hockey reaches the same place by a narrower route:** its Rule 623 lists among the actions considered hooking *"a stick placed on the hand/arm that takes away the ability for the opponent to pass or shoot the puck with a normal amount of force"* — a stick on the hands, but only where it has that effect, where the IIHF's sentence is flat and needs no effect at all. **And the NHL only looks like the exception, because its hands provision sits in a different section of the book.** Rule 55 Hooking is in Section 7, Restraining Infractions, and writes no hands sentence at all; Rule 61 Slashing, in Section 8, Stick Infractions, does — *"Any forceful or powerful chop with the stick on an opponent's body, the opponent's stick, or on or near the opponent's hands that, in the judgment of the Referee, is not an attempt to play the puck, shall be penalized as slashing."* **That slashing route is not the NHL's alone.** IIHF Rule 61.1 carries the NHL's sentence word for word, and USA Hockey Rule 634's Note is a shortened form of it — *"Any forceful chop with the stick on an opponent's body or opponent's stick, on or near the opponent's hands, shall be considered slashing"* — **with no "attempt to play the puck" let-out at all**, so under USA Hockey it is the force of the chop that decides and not the intent behind it. **Hockey Canada's Rule 9.3 is the one that enumerates nothing:** it defines slashing as *"the action of hitting an opponent with a stick"*, which reaches the hands without naming them, and its single mention of a hand — *"while holding the stick with one or both hands"* — is the slasher's own, not the opponent's. **Read the carve-out before you rely on it either way:** a clean lift for the puck **is** an attempt to play the puck and therefore sits outside 61.1, so the rule does not make the technique illegal — what it catches is the chop. Lift under the shaft, below their bottom hand, and you are outside the hands provision in each of the four books. Stick-on-stick is the other half of 55.1, and in the IIHF book the half that collides with its hands sentence: it is what NHL and IIHF Rule 55.1 protect, and only while checking — USA Hockey and Hockey Canada name the opponent's stick as hookable instead.
- It works from **behind and beside** an opponent, where you cannot legally do anything else. You cannot restrain a player from behind — NHL Rule 56.1 says a player behind an opponent "may not use his stick, body or free hand in order to restrain his opponent" — but lifting the stick of a player *who has the puck* is playing the puck, not restraining a player.
- It is the correct tool at the **net front**, in a **board battle**, and on a **backcheck** — the three places where amateurs instead reach, hook, or cross-check and go to the box.
- It leaves you **balanced**. You never leave your feet or lean past your centre of gravity, so if it fails you are still in the play.

**How to do it:** Get your blade under their shaft *low*, **below their bottom hand and a hand's width clear of the glove** — that is also where the leverage is, because the fulcrum is their top hand and a contact point further down the shaft has the longer lever arm. Lift crisply — up, not sideways. As their blade comes off the ice, step through and take the puck with your other hand still on your stick. Do it late: lift as they are about to shoot, receive, or move the puck, not while they are just carrying it.

#### Where the stick lift becomes a penalty

```facts
Never: Lift the stick of a player who does not have the puck. That is interference
Never: Catch their glove or forearm on the way up, or yank the stick down or across. That is slashing, roughing, or holding the stick
Rule: Hockey Canada allows the lift "for the purpose of making a play on the puck" (Interpretation 1 to Rule 8.2(a)), but makes it hooking where it contacts the hands and impedes their ability to "pass, shoot, receive, or otherwise propel the puck" — receive is the one that catches you
Rule: A lift that misses an opponent bent low is a high stick however low your own hands were — the reference is the struck player's waist (NHL 60.3; IIHF 60.3 in the 2026/27 book; IIHF Situation Handbook 2026/27, Situation 60.13). A minor, or a double minor where injury results
Rule: A missed lift catching a bent-over face is not capped at the double minor: IIHF 60.4, USA Hockey 621(b) and Hockey Canada 7.6(b)/7.7(b) reach a major plus a game misconduct with nobody injured; NHL 60.4's match penalty needs a deliberate or attempted injury
Rule: A fifth book is harsher — CARHA 62(b), CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only, is a major for contact above an opponent's shoulders, accidental or not, injury or not, with no minor rung there and no windup carve-out; its Note adds a game misconduct when intentional or when it injures
Rule: A chop at the hands reaches a major plus a game misconduct with nobody injured in all four books (NHL 61.3 with 61.5, IIHF 61.3, USAH 634(b), HC 9.3(b)) — a hooking major needs an injury under NHL 55.3 and HC 8.2(b), but not under IIHF 55.3 or USAH 623(b)
Rule: Three minor penalties for stick infractions in the same game end your game under Hockey Canada Rule 4.8(b)(i) — a Game Ejection. The rule defines stick infractions as Rules 9.1 to 9.5, so slashing counts and hooking does not — and 9.5 is Junior/Senior only
Rule: Hockey Canada's Interpretation 2 to Rule 4.8(b) counts a double minor as a single infraction towards its three-stick-minor Game Ejection, so a double minor for slashing puts one mark against you rather than two
Rule: A Hockey Canada Game Ejection is not a game misconduct and triggers no automatic suspension — Rule 4.8's Note 1 says so in terms, so a third stick minor costs the rest of that game and the book attaches nothing past the final whistle
Key: Penalties are counted as well as priced — three cheap stick minors end your game under Hockey Canada, and the other books run in-game counts of their own
```

Lifting a stick when the player does **not** have the puck is interference. Lifting at or near their hands is hooking under the IIHF book on the location alone — subject to the carve-out in 55.1's own next sentence, which the book does not reconcile with it and which is set out above — and under USA Hockey's Rule 623 where a stick placed on the hand or arm *"takes away the ability for the opponent to pass or shoot the puck with a normal amount of force"* — a condition the IIHF's sentence does not carry. **The NHL's *hooking* rule has no hands sentence, but do not read the book as silent on the hands:** NHL Rule 61.1 makes a forceful chop *"on or near the opponent's hands that, in the judgment of the Referee, is not an attempt to play the puck"* **slashing** — and three of the four books take that route, because IIHF Rule 61.1 is the NHL's sentence word for word and USA Hockey Rule 634 a shortened form of it with no *"attempt to play the puck"* let-out at all. A lift for the puck is an attempt to play the puck; a chop at the hands is not, in any of the three. **Hockey Canada reaches it by an interpretation written about this exact technique:** Interpretation 1 to Rule 8.2(a) allows a player to *"lift an opposing player's stick, for the purpose of making a play on the puck"* — but *"if this action contacts the opponent's hands and impedes their ability to pass, shoot, **receive**, or otherwise propel the puck, then a penalty for Hooking must be assessed."* Note *receive*: taking a pass is the commonest moment a lift lands on the hands. Catching a glove on the way up is slashing or roughing in any of them. Yanking the stick down or across is holding the stick.

**And a lift that misses an opponent who is bent low over the puck is a high stick, however low your own hands were.** The reference point is the player you hit, not you: the NHL's Rule 60.3 and the IIHF's 2026/27 Rule 60.3 both write *"above the height of the opponent's waist"*, and the **IIHF Situation Handbook 2026/27** answers this exact case at **Situation 60.13** — a stick-lift attempt on a player *"bent over digging the puck out of the corner"* which misses and hits them in the face while the striker's stick is *"only slightly above the height of their waist"* — with *"Yes, this is a minor (or double-minor) penalty for high-sticking."* **And the double minor is not the ceiling.** IIHF **60.4** lets the referee assess *"a major penalty and an automatic game misconduct penalty"* on a player who *"recklessly endangers their opponent by 'high-sticking'"* — wording identical in the 2025/26 and 2026/27 IIHF books, so this tier does not turn on which edition you are under — and **USA Hockey 621(b)** makes the same pair compulsory: *"A major plus a game misconduct penalty shall be assessed to any player who recklessly endangers an opponent as a result of high sticking."* **Neither waits for an injury**, and Hockey Canada reaches that tier through its head-contact rules instead, on the degree of violence rather than on endangerment — **7.6(b)** in minor and female hockey, *"At the discretion of the Referee, based on the degree of violence of the impact"*, and **7.7(b)** in junior and senior, *"for any Head Contact infraction with a high degree of violence"*. The NHL's **60.4** is a match penalty and needs more than a miss — it reaches a player who *"attempts to or deliberately injures an opponent with a high-stick"*. **Hockey Canada answers this same case directly, and its answer is division-scoped.** Interpretation 2 to Rule 9.5(a) lists the *"attempted restraining fouls"* that *"should be considered accidental high-sticks"*, and item ii is this one in terms: *"When an attempted lifting of the opponent's stick, which results in accidental contact to the head or neck area, causes injury, a double Minor will be assessed."* Item vi takes *"any aggressive, reckless, negligent or forceful action of the stick (e.g. pitchfork action in lifting the stick or hooking an opponent)"* that causes injury to a major plus a game misconduct. ⚠️ **Both are junior and senior only** — Rule 9.5 is titled *"High-Sticking – Junior/Senior"*, and items iv to vi are assessed under Rule 7.7, Head Contact – Junior/Senior. **Below that the case falls to Rule 7.6, and the trigger there is lower, not higher:** 7.6(a) assesses a minor *"to any player who accidentally contacts an opponent in the head, face, or neck, in minor and female hockey"*, with no injury required at all. So in Hockey Canada minor and female hockey a missed lift that merely brushes a bent-over face is already a penalty. ⚠️ **And a fifth book on disk is harsher than any of the four, in the kind of league many adult readers actually play in.** **CARHA Rule 62(b)** — binding **CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only**, and no reader in minor hockey — assesses **a major penalty** to a player who *"accidentally or intentionally contacts an opponent above the normal height of the opponent's shoulders with a high stick"*, *"whether or not injury results."* **Above the shoulders there is no minor rung at all.** CARHA's 62(a) is a minor for a **different act altogether** — checking or intimidating an opponent while carrying your stick above **their waist** — which requires no contact at all and is not a lower rung of 62(b). Nor is there a route back down through a head-contact rule: **CARHA Rule 50** is written for contact *"with any part of the player's body or equipment other than a stick"*, and its opening Note sends any stick contact back to Rule 62. 62(b)'s Note adds a game misconduct where the high stick was intentional, or where it was accidental and injures; **62(c)** is a match penalty. ⚠️ **And CARHA writes no windup or follow-through carve-out** — searched by concept as well as by phrase — so the accident that is a minor or a double minor under the four books is a five-minute major there. **Find out which book your league runs before you take a lift at a bent-over opponent.** **None of that is a reason to stop lifting sticks; it is the reason to take this particular one under the shaft or not at all.** Editions, rule numbers and the rest of the divergence are in [What actually gets called](#what-actually-gets-called).

**Which charge you take is the reason to lift rather than chop, even where you think you would get away with it.** Under the NHL and Hockey Canada a hooking major needs an **injury** — NHL 55.3 reaches a player who *"injures an opponent by 'hooking'"*, Hockey Canada 8.2(b) one who *"injures an opponent by a hooking or butt-end hooking infraction"* — where a slashing major needs only severity of contact (NHL 61.3) or *"the degree of violence of the impact"* (Hockey Canada 9.3(b)); and Hockey Canada 8.2(c) bars a match penalty for hooking outright — *"No Match penalty may be assessed for a hooking infraction"* — while 9.3(c) provides one for slashing. **Under the IIHF and USA Hockey the two rules run level, and both run high:** each reaches a major **plus a game misconduct** for recklessly endangering an opponent with nobody hurt — IIHF 55.3 and 61.3 at the referee's discretion, USA Hockey 623(b) and 634(b) where the major plus game misconduct *"shall be assessed"* — and each allows a match penalty above that (USA Hockey 623(c), 634(c)). **And in Hockey Canada a chop can end your game a third way, because the minors are counted as well as priced.** Rule 4.8(b)(i) assesses a **Game Ejection** *"[w]hen a player is assessed three Minor penalties for 'stick infractions' in the same game"*, and defines those in the same clause as *"Rule 9.1 — Butt-Ending, Rule 9.2 — Cross-Checking, Rule 9.3 — Slashing, Rule 9.4 — Spearing, and Rule 9.5 — High-sticking — Junior/Senior."* So a slashing minor counts toward it and a hooking minor, which lives in Section 8 with the restraining fouls, does not — Interpretation 1 to Rule 9.1(a) says exactly that about butt-end hooking. **Read the last item on that list with the scope printed in its own title, because it is not everybody's.** Rule 9.5 is headed *"High-Sticking – Junior/Senior"* and its penalty clause opens *"In Junior and Senior divisions"*, so in minor and female hockey only four of those five stick infractions reach you at all. That does not make a high stick free below Junior: there it is Head Contact under Rule 7.6, which carries a count of its own — three of those in the same game bring the same Game Ejection under Rule 4.8(b)(ii), on a separate tally. So a minor-division player has two counts running rather than one, and the high stick is on the other one. **Interpretation 2 to Rule 4.8(b) softens the count in one respect and one only:** *"For the purposes of accumulation towards a Game Ejection penalty, a double Minor penalty will be considered one infraction."* So a double minor for slashing is one mark against you, not two — and the third mark still ends your night, whichever way you got there. **What it does not do is follow you into the next game.** A Game Ejection is a lesser thing than a game misconduct, and the book says so: *"A Game Ejection is not equal to a Game Misconduct penalty and a player receiving a Game Ejection penalty does not trigger any automatic suspension."* (Rule 4.8's Note 1, printed after 4.8(c) without being tied to a sub-clause.) So the automatic suspension in [And what these fouls cost beyond tonight](#and-what-these-fouls-cost-beyond-tonight) is not triggered by this one; your league may still act, but the rulebook attaches nothing past the final whistle.

**The NHL, IIHF and USA Hockey books were each searched for a stick-specific count of their own, and none of them carries one. Read that negative narrowly.** It means those three books do not single stick minors out; it does **not** mean your penalties stop mattering once you have paid for each of them. Every one of the four escalates within a single game on a counter of some kind, on different triggers and different numbers, and this document does not set the other three out — [Rules Primer](../foundation/rules_primer.md) does. **Find your own book's count before you take a third cheap stick minor**, because the price of the first two is not what the third one costs.

### The sweep check

```facts
Technique: A low, wide arc — blade flat, shaft nearly parallel to the ice, sweeping through the puck or the passing lane
Read: Use it when beaten to the outside, defending a pass across the slot, or covering the pass on a 2-on-1
Technique: Bottom hand slides down the shaft, get low, sweep through the ice — not through the air
Never: Sweep at knee or waist height — that is a hook or a slash
Never: Lean your body after the sweep. Sweep with your stick instead and keep your feet under you
```

**What it is:** A low, wide arc of your stick across the ice — blade flat, shaft nearly parallel to the ice — sweeping through the puck or the passing lane.

**When it works:** When you are beaten to the outside and reaching across, when you are defending a pass across the slot, or when you are down low and want to take away a lane without committing your body. Defencemen use it on a 2-on-1 to cover the pass while the goalie takes the shooter.

**How to do it:** Bottom hand slides down the shaft, get low, sweep through the ice — not through the air. The blade must stay on the ice; a sweep at knee or waist height is a hook or a slash.

**The mistake:** Sweeping with your body leaning after it. Sweep with your stick and keep your feet under you.

### The press-down (stick press)

```facts
Technique: Lay your blade or shaft on top of their blade, pinning it to the ice so they cannot shoot, pass or move the puck
Read: Use it in tight — net front, board battles, faceoff scrambles — when you want the whistle or a teammate to arrive
Key: The lift takes their blade up so you can take the puck; the press takes it down so they cannot use it
Never: Press with force, chop down repeatedly, or press on their hands — a press is a hold-and-pin, not a chop
```

**What it is:** You lay your blade or shaft down on top of their stick blade, pinning it to the ice so they cannot shoot, pass, or move the puck.

**When it works:** In tight — net front, board battles, faceoff scrambles, and any time the puck is sitting between you and an opponent and you want either the whistle or a teammate to arrive. It is the opposite tool to the lift: the lift takes their blade *up* so you can take the puck; the press takes their blade *down* so they cannot use it.

**Where it becomes a penalty:** Pressing down with force, repeatedly chopping down, or pressing down on their hands. A press is a hold-and-pin, not a chop.

> **All four of these work in non-contact leagues.** If you play in a beer league and want to become a genuinely better defensive player this season, learn the stick lift.

---

## 4. Body Position as Defence

You can defend an entire shift without ever making a deliberate hit. The rulebook is on your side here, and the relevant passage is worth reading in full. NHL Rule 56.1 (Interference), under "Body Position":

> "A player is allowed the ice he is standing on (body position) and is not required to move in order to let an opponent proceed. A player may 'block' the path of an opponent provided he is in front of his opponent and moving in the same direction. ... A player is always entitled to use his body position to lengthen an opponent's path to the puck, provided his stick is not utilized (to make himself 'bigger'...); his free hand is not used and he does not take advantage of his body position to deliver an otherwise illegal check."

USA Hockey codifies the same idea for non-checking play, listing as legal competitive contact: a skater is entitled to the ice they occupy while maintaining skating speed and body position between an opponent and a loose puck; a skater may stand their ground and is not required to move; a skater may block an opponent when in front and moving in the same direction; and a skater may use body position while holding their skating lane to force an opponent onto a longer route to the puck.

### What that gives you

```facts
Action: Take away ice — standing in the place your opponent needs to be is a complete defensive act
Technique: Box out — inside shoulder ahead of theirs, hips between them and the puck, and hold
Action: Seal along the wall — occupy the lane out and let the boards do the rest
Rule: A player who has lost position must skate to gain or re-establish it in order to make a check (NHL Rule 56.1)
Never: Use your stick to make yourself wider, or your free hand to hold, pull, tug or grab
Rule: The free hand may fend off an opponent or their stick, but may not hold their stick or body — NHL and IIHF Rule 56.1; Hockey Canada 8.1 permits blocking a stick but not holding it
Rule: USA Hockey writes the fend-off permission in its Casebook, not its playing rules, and conditions it — control the puck (Standard of Play Situation 6) or be reaching for it (Situation 7), hold a position you "legally established", and never push off to create space
```

**Taking away ice.** Standing in the place your opponent needs to be is a complete defensive act. You do not have to touch them. Making a player skate 15 extra feet is often worth more than a hit, because it buys your teammates time to get back.

**Boxing out.** Get your inside shoulder ahead of theirs, put your hips between them and the puck, and hold. This is the basketball rebound principle and it is exactly how you defend the net front and how you win a loose puck in a crowd.

**Sealing.** Along the wall, put your body between the opponent and the ice they want, then let the boards do the rest. You are not pushing them into the boards — you are occupying the lane out.

**Skating to re-establish position.** The line you must not cross is in the same rule: a player behind an opponent who does not have the puck "must skate in order to gain or reestablish his proper position in order to make a check." If you have lost position, the only legal way to get it back is with your feet. Reaching with your stick is hooking; reaching with your free hand is holding.

**The two things that turn legal position into a penalty:** using your stick to make yourself wider, and using your free hand to hold, pull, tug or grab. NHL and IIHF Rule 56.1 permit the free hand to "fend off" an opponent or their stick, but "may not be used to hold an opponent's stick or body." **USA Hockey writes the permission too — in its Casebook rather than its playing rules — and hangs conditions on it the NHL's sentence does not.** Standard of Play Situation 6 asks whether a player may take a hand off the stick and use it as a shield, and answers that *"a player who is in control of the puck is allowed to use a free hand to maintain body position that they legally established"*, the example being *"the player with the puck uses the free arm to fend off the opponent and maintain their body position."* ⚠️ **Read every condition before you take a hand off your stick.** You must be **in control of the puck** — or, under Situation 7, **reaching for it** while physically engaged in a corner or in front of the goal; the position you are holding must be one you **legally established**; and the arm may hold what you have, never take more. Situation 6 ends *"if they grab the stick, sweater or arm of the opponent, or use the free arm to push off and create space, a penalty should be assessed"*, and Situation 5 allows a net-front defender to steer with the stick or forearm only *"as long as the arm(s) are not extended."* Hockey Canada 8.1 permits blocking an opponent's stick with the hands, arms or body but not holding it.

---

## 5. Delivering a Legal Body Check

> **⚠️ This section applies only in leagues that permit body checking.** If you play 12U or below, girls'/women's hockey under USA Hockey or Hockey Canada, U13 and below in Canada, any Canadian division a Member of Hockey Canada has approved as non-check, or a non-check adult league, everything below is a penalty, and it is not capped at a minor: USA Hockey 604 runs minor, major plus game misconduct, match, and Hockey Canada 7.3 the same shape. **And if your league runs the CARHA book, this section is not yours either** — that book has no checking division at all, its Rule 49(a) makes a body check a minor or a major anywhere on the ice and a major where it injures, and by Rule 30(a) that major rules you off the ice for the rest of the game (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only; see §1). Skip to Section 6 — **but read the last four sections of this one first: *The four that cause the injuries*, *Boarding, and the player who cannot see you coming*, *Checking from behind, and what the onus does and does not mean*, and *And what these fouls cost beyond tonight*.** Checking from behind, head contact, charging and boarding are penalties in *every* division: Hockey Canada scopes 7.3 and USA Hockey scopes 604, and neither scopes those four — though Hockey Canada does split head contact between 7.6 and 7.7, which between them cover every division. **All four can end your game in at least one book**, and they can cost you games beyond it. ⚠️ **But if CARHA is your book, read those four sections knowing that they price these fouls in the NHL, IIHF, USA Hockey and Hockey Canada books and never in CARHA's.** Every act they prohibit is prohibited under CARHA too — it is the penalty ladders that are not yours, and they do not run the same way. **Your four rules are CARHA 49 (Boarding and Body Contact), 50 (Head Contact), 52 (Charging) and 53 (Checking from Behind), and 53 is the one to read first.** CARHA's floor for checking from behind is *"[a] Minor penalty plus a Game Misconduct"*, assessed *"to any player who intentionally pushes, body checks, or hits an opposing player from behind, anywhere on the ice"* — **so the lowest rung in the CARHA book still ends your game, and the relief USA Hockey's 608(a) gives is not in it.** Under **CARHA 53(a)** a major plus a game misconduct is compulsory *"[i]f a player is injured"*, and **53(b)** is a **Match penalty** where a player is *"pushed, hit or propelled in any manner from behind into the boards or goal net, in such a way that the player is unable to protect or defend themselves"*, under a Note instructing referees *"not to substitute other penalties when a player is checked from behind in any manner."* **CARHA 52(a)** prices charging at a minor or a major, makes a major plus a game misconduct compulsory *"[i]f injury results"*, and adds a Note: *"If more than two steps or strides are taken, it shall be considered a charge."* **CARHA 50** covers contact to an opponent's head, face or neck *"with any part of the player's body or equipment other than a stick"* — a minor at 50(a) for contact that is *accidental*, a double minor or a major plus a game misconduct at 50(b) when it is intentional, and a match penalty at 50(c) for a deliberate attempt to injure; a stick that gets there goes to CARHA 62 instead, which §3 covers. **CARHA boarding is 49(b)**, and it works by conversion rather than by tier: where a player commits an infraction that causes an opponent to collide with the boards, the minor *"normally assessed under the rules will be waived and a Double Minor penalty shall be assessed"*, with the major or match applicable to that rule *"assessed in the normal manner."* **CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only; see §1.**
>
> **In Britain, you cannot answer that question from a rulebook** — neither the IIHF Rule Book nor the In-House Rules sets a body-checking age, so ask your league before you read on (see "If you play in Britain" in §1). **Women's hockey in Britain runs IIHF Rule 101.1**, so the restricted standard above is yours and this section is not.

### A body check is the last step of a progression, not the first thing you learn

```facts
Never: Deliver anything in this section in a league that does not permit checking — everything below is a penalty there
Rule: A body check under USA Hockey 604(c) at 12U and below, in all girls'/women's and all non-check adult classifications is not capped at a minor — 604(d) is a major plus a game misconduct where it recklessly endangers, and 604(e) a match
Rule: Hockey Canada prices a body check in divisions of U13 and below, in female hockey and in any other division a Member has approved — 7.3(a) a minor, 7.3(b) a major plus a game misconduct on the degree of violence with nobody hurt and mandatory on injury, 7.3(c) a match
Rule: Body checking is prohibited at 12U and below, in all girls'/women's classifications and in all non-check adult classifications (USA Hockey Rule 604(a))
Rule: Hockey Canada 7.3(a) bars body checking at U13 and below, throughout female hockey, and in any other division a Member has approved — so being above U13 does not settle it
Rule: In Britain neither the IIHF Rule Book nor the In-House Rules sets a body-checking age. Ask your league; women's hockey runs IIHF Rule 101.1
Key: League legality is a floor, not a readiness test
Technique: The progression is skating, angling, stick checks, contact confidence, body blocking, take out/rub out, solid body checks
Priority: If you cannot angle, lift a stick cleanly or stay balanced through contact, the missing skill is the step you skipped
Key: Body play should begin to be taught at 8-and-Under, years before checking is legal
Priority: A body check is the fourth line of defence after angling, stick checks and body blocks — not the first tool to reach for
```

League legality is a floor, not a readiness test. USA Hockey's *Introduction To Body Contact* sets out body play as an ordered progression, and a solid body check is step seven of seven:

1. **Skating**
2. **Angling**
3. **Stick checks**
4. **Contact confidence** — *"being comfortable with incidental body contact – contact that occurs when competing for a puck"*
5. **Body blocking**
6. **Take out / rub out checks**
7. **Solid body checks**

The manual's own justification for the order: *"It is important that coaches teach this progression of body play skills. This will allow players to enjoy the game more and will also make it a safer sport."* It also notes that body play skills should **begin** to be taught at 8-and-Under, years before checking is legal, precisely because the earlier steps are what make the last one safe.

Two things follow. **For a player:** if you cannot yet angle a puck carrier off the puck, lift a stick cleanly, or stay balanced through incidental contact, the missing skill is not the check — it is the step you skipped, and it is the reason the check will go wrong. Sections 2, 3 and 4 of this document are steps 1–5. **For a coach:** the first four levels of body play, in USA Hockey's own ordering, are angling, stick checks, body blocks and body checks — a body check is the fourth line of defence, not the first one to reach for.

### What a legal check is, precisely

```facts
Rule: Not legal where checking is barred — USA Hockey 604(a) at 12U and below, all girls'/women's and non-check adult; Hockey Canada 7.3 at U13 and below, in female hockey, and in any other division a Member of Hockey Canada has approved
Rule: IIHF women's play permits bodychecking only with clear intention of playing or gaining possession of the puck; any move to step or glide into an opponent is at least a minor for an illegal hit (Rule 101.1). Britain publishes no body-checking age at all. Ask your league
Rule: A body check is not legal in a CARHA-affiliated adult league either — that book has no checking division, 49(a) makes one a minor or a major anywhere on the ice and a major where it injures, and Rule 30(a) rules that major off the ice for the rest of the game
Goal: To gain possession of the puck — NOT to punish or intimidate an opponent
Targets: An opponent in control of the puck, hit from the front, diagonally from the front, or straight from the side
Technique: Trunk only — hips and shoulders. Hands, forearm, stick and elbow are all illegal
Position: Contact above their knees and at or below their shoulders
Technique: Start with your stick on the puck — the blade of the player delivering the check must be below the knees (USA Hockey)
Rule: Where checking is barred, an illegal body check is intentional contact using overt hip, shoulder, forearm or torso action — USA Hockey Rule 604 Note 1, scoped by its own words to "the purpose of this rule" and by 604(a) to the Competitive Contact classifications
Key: If your stick is up, you are about to commit a cross-check, a high stick or a head-contact penalty
```

USA Hockey's **Glossary** entry for *Body Checking* is the most explicit definition in the sport, so use it as your checklist — it is the Glossary that carries it, not Rule 604. Its mirror image is **USA Hockey Rule 604, Note 1**, which defines the illegal version — but mind the scope: 604 is titled *"Body Checking (Competitive Contact Categories)"* and its Note opens *"For the purpose of this rule"*, so it governs the classifications where checking is **barred**. **In a checking league the hip and shoulder action it names is the *legal* check — the forearm never is, in any classification** (the Glossary and the Declaration bar it book-wide). Where checking is barred, it reads: *"an illegal body check is defined as when a player makes intentional physical contact with the opponent using overt hip, shoulder, forearm or torso action. This includes physically forcing the opponent off the puck and with no effort to legally play the puck."* Everything below is what keeps you on the legal side of that line:

- **Purpose:** to gain possession of the puck — "NOT to punish or intimidate an opponent."
- **Target:** an opponent **in control of the puck**.
- **Direction:** from the front, diagonally from the front, or straight from the side.
- **Your contact surface:** the **trunk only** — hips and shoulders. Hands, forearm, stick and elbow are all illegal.
- **Their contact area:** **above the knees and at or below the shoulders.**
