- **Your stick:** "Proper body checking technique starts with stick on puck, therefore the stick blade of the player delivering the check must be below the knees."

**The IIHF book defines the same check in its own glossary**, and if you play in Britain this is the definition you are actually being officiated against: *"A legal body check is one in which a Player checks an opponent who is in possession of the puck, by using their hip or body from the front, diagonally from the front or straight from the side. Legitimate body checking must be done for the purpose of separating the opponent from the puck, only with the trunk of the body (hips and shoulders) and must be above the opponent's knees and at or below the shoulders."* Note what it does **not** contain: **no stick-below-the-knees clause.** That one is USA Hockey's coaching standard, and it is worth adopting anyway — but it is not a rule you are held to under the IIHF book.

**Two of the four books never define a legal check at all, and they are worth naming.** The
**NHL rulebook contains no positive definition of a body check** — the words appear in it
barely at all, and never as a definition of the legal act, which is why its glossary has no
entry for a check of any kind — and
**Hockey Canada** works the other way round, defining body-checking by directional force:
deliberate contact *"with opposite-directional force"*, leaving your skating lane, or extending
an arm, shoulder or hip after angling. That wording sits in **Rule 7.3**, which applies only
where checking is *banned* — so a Hockey Canada reader in a checking division gets no definition
from it at all. The book's **glossary** carries the same definition under *Body-Checking* with
no division scope, and adds the purpose 7.3 leaves out: the arm, shoulder or hip is extended
*"to maximize physical contact"*. Both arrive at the same place by prohibiting
rather than by describing. That is why the envelope above is worth playing to even where your
own book never writes it down: it is the most demanding of the four descriptions, so a check
that satisfies it does not offend the other three. That is a comparison drawn here between the
four definitions, not a rule any of them states.

That last point is the one that separates a coached player from an amateur. **The check starts with your stick on the puck.** If your stick is up, you are about to commit a cross-check, a high stick, or a head-contact penalty. If your stick is down on the puck, your hands are down, your elbows are down, and your shoulder arrives where it should.

### Delivering a Legal Body Check: Technique

```facts
Rule: Not legal where checking is barred — USA Hockey 604(a) at 12U and below, all girls'/women's and non-check adult; Hockey Canada 7.3 at U13 and below, in female hockey, and in any other division a Member of Hockey Canada has approved
Rule: IIHF women's play permits bodychecking only with clear intention of playing or gaining possession of the puck; any move to step or glide into an opponent is at least a minor for an illegal hit (Rule 101.1). Britain publishes no body-checking age at all. Ask your league
Rule: A body check is not legal in a CARHA-affiliated adult league either — that book has no checking division, 49(a) makes one a minor or a major anywhere on the ice and a major where it injures, and Rule 30(a) rules that major off the ice for the rest of the game
Technique: Play the body, not the head — the point of contact is the chest or upper body, square
Never: Extend the body upward or outward to reach — NHL Rule 48.1 counts that as evidence head contact was avoidable
Technique: Elbows down, hands down — an extended elbow is elbowing (NHL Rule 45.1) whether or not it injures
Action: Hit through, not at. Skate through the contact with your feet moving so you finish standing over the puck
Rule: Charging is judged on distance travelled (NHL Rule 42.1); IIHF Rule 42.1 penalises building speed by "multiple strides", USA Hockey more than two, Hockey Canada two or more
Rule: Never leave your feet to check — jumping is charging in its own right, whatever the stride count (NHL Rule 42.1, a player who "skates, jumps into or charges an opponent in any manner"; IIHF Rule 42.1, one who "jumps to check an opponent")
Technique: For an open-ice check the opponent must be one stride away — drive the shoulder into the chest, do not lunge forward
Never: Hit a player who is turned toward the boards
```

**Play the body, not the head.** The point of contact is the chest or the upper body, square. Do not extend upward or outward to reach — NHL Rule 48.1 lists "unnecessary extension of the body upward or outward" as evidence that head contact was avoidable, and IIHF Rule 48.1 is blunter still: "There is no clean check to the head or neck."

**Elbows down, hands down.** An extended elbow is elbowing (NHL Rule 45.1) regardless of whether it causes injury. Hands and forearms into a check are roughing or head contact.

**Hit through, not at.** Skate through the contact with your legs, keeping your feet moving. Stopping at the point of contact and swinging your upper body is how you end up off balance and reaching. Driving *through* on your skates keeps you upright and — the actual point — leaves you standing over the puck when they are not.

**Time it, don't chase it.** Charging is defined by distance travelled and speed built up. NHL Rule 42.1: "Charging shall mean the actions of a player who, as a result of distance traveled, shall violently check an opponent in any manner." USA Hockey's enforcement standard puts a number on it — the **Standard of Play and Rule Emphasis – Body Checking** (CHARGING): "A player cannot take more than two strides or travel an excessive distance to accelerate through a body check for the purpose of punishing the opponent." **Rule 607 (Charging)** carries the same test in its own words. **Hockey Canada writes the two-stride test straight into the definition** — Rule 7.4 lists charging as, among other things, when a player *"builds up speed by taking two or more strides immediately prior to making contact"* or *"travels an excessive distance with the sole purpose of delivering a hit"* (wording identical in the 2024-2026 and 2026-2028 Playing Rules). **IIHF Rule 42.1 carries a stride element too, without a number on it:** charging is *"the actions of a Player who either jumps to check an opponent, builds up speed by taking multiple strides immediately prior to making contact and / or travels an excessive distance with the sole purpose of delivering such a hit and / or violently checks an opponent in any manner."* That is the test a British player is officiated against — closer to Hockey Canada's construction than to the NHL's, but with "multiple" left to the referee rather than a stride count. **If you are winding up from across the ice, you have already committed the penalty.**

**And never leave your feet.** The stride count is not the whole of the rule, and a player who learns charging as a distance test can miss the other half of it. Both books put jumping in the opening sentence: the NHL penalises "a player who **skates, jumps into** or charges an opponent in any manner" (Rule 42.1), and the IIHF definition quoted above begins with a player who *"either **jumps to check an opponent**"* (Rule 42.1). Leaving your feet is charging in its own right — it does not matter how short your approach was, and it does not become legal because you took only one stride.

**For an open-ice check, the working constraint is tighter than either rule.** *Introduction To Body Contact* calls the open-ice shoulder check a *"high risk check, possibility of only partial or no body contact with the puck carrier"* and sets the condition for attempting one at all: *"Opponent must be close enough so that only one stride forward is required to make contact (gap control)."* One stride, not two. If you need more than one stride to get there, you do not have the gap. Close it with your feet first, or angle instead. The same entry adds: *"drive shoulder into opponent's chest, do not lunge forward."*

**Never hit a player who is turned toward the boards.** This is not a technique point, it is the line between hockey and a spinal injury. See below.

### The late hit — the check you are not allowed to finish

```facts
Rule: Not legal where checking is barred — USA Hockey 604(a) at 12U and below, all girls'/women's and non-check adult; Hockey Canada 7.3 at U13 and below, in female hockey, and in any other division a Member of Hockey Canada has approved
Rule: IIHF women's play permits bodychecking only with clear intention of playing or gaining possession of the puck; any move to step or glide into an opponent is at least a minor for an illegal hit (Rule 101.1). Britain publishes no body-checking age at all. Ask your league
Action: Check the puck carrier, and check them now — the moment they have passed or shot, the check is late
Rule: The NHL allows a check "rendered immediately following his loss of possession" — immediately, not a beat later; USA Hockey's Standard of Play penalises the same act under ROUGHING and calls it "finishing" the check
Rule: The IIHF writes the NHL's condition and adds a second on top of it — the check must also fall inside an "objectively reasonable window of opportunity" (IIHF 56.1), so a British reader's window is the narrower of the two
Rule: A late hit is not capped at a minor — IIHF 56.1 makes it reckless endangerment and 56.5 a major plus a game misconduct with nobody hurt; Hockey Canada 8.3(b) reaches the same tier on the degree of violence of the impact, and mandatorily where it injures
Rule: USA Hockey 640(b) makes a minor of every avoidable check on a player who has released a shot or pass — but it is written "(except Adult Male Classifications)", and there 640 Note 2 directs a roughing penalty instead, with no classification exception
Rule: Four conditions let you finish a check already under way and you need all four — an effort to gain possession first, an arm's length plus stick length away when the puck went, the check already being delivered, and minimised force (USA Hockey Casebook, Standard of Play, Situation 10)
Never: Skate one or two more strides after the puck is gone and then decide to deliver the check — USA Hockey's Casebook rules that out in those words
Rule: USA Hockey's escalations reach a late hit only through 640(b) — 640(g) a major plus a game misconduct, 640(h) a match penalty. Whether they reach adult men's hockey, where 640(b) is not assessed, the book does not say and this document does not know
Rule: USA Hockey Rule 602(a) is limited by no classification and tied to no named foul at all — a match penalty for anyone who "recklessly endangers or attempts to injure any opposing player"
```

**Check the puck carrier, and check them now.** A check delivered after the player has passed or shot is a late hit. USA Hockey's **Standard of Play and Rule Emphasis – Body Checking** penalises, under ROUGHING, "delivering a late check to a player who has already relinquished control of the puck by a pass or shot. This is oftentimes referred to as 'finishing' the check." The NHL allows a check "rendered immediately following his loss of possession" — immediately, not a beat later. 🇬🇧 **The IIHF writes that condition and then adds a second on top of it**, so a British reader's window is the narrower of the two: a player in possession *"may be checked legally, provided the check is rendered immediately following them 'loss of control' **and** their opponent still finds themself within an 'objectively reasonable window of opportunity' to deliver such a check"* (56.1). ⚠️ **And the late hit is not capped at a minor.** IIHF 56.1 says a late hit *"constitutes reckless endangerment"* and **56.5 reaches a major plus a game misconduct with nobody hurt**; Hockey Canada 8.3(b) reaches the same tier on *"the degree of violence of the impact"* with nobody hurt, and makes it mandatory where it injures. **And USA Hockey's window is the narrowest of the four — not the absence of one.** 640(b) makes a minor of every *avoidable* check on a player who has released a shot or pass — the rule says they *"are not eligible to be body checked"*, and the Declaration of Player Safety calls *"finishing the check"* an *"unacceptable action"* because it is *"meant to intimidate or punish the opponent with no intent to gain possession of the puck"* — wording the book's Glossary repeats under *"Late Body Check"* and widens, to *"no intent, or possibility, to gain possession"*. **Its separate Casebook is what says when contact already under way is not a late hit** — and, like Hockey Canada's 8.3, it measures that as a distance: a check may be finished only where the defending player *"first makes an effort to gain possession of the puck and is an arm's length plus stick length away from the opponent when the puck was dumped"*, the check *"was in the process of being delivered when control of the puck was relinquished"*, and the checker is *"expected to minimize the force used"* (Standard of Play, Situation 10). ⚠️ **That is four conditions and you need all of them, and lateness is an express test there rather than merely evidence of avoidability:** *"It is not acceptable to skate one or two more strides after the puck is gone and then decide to deliver the check."* That named minor is written *"except Adult Male Classifications"* — but it is not a free hit there either: 640 Note 2 directs a roughing penalty for *"an avoidable body check to an opponent … who is no longer in control of the puck"* with no classification exception. ⚠️ **And the minor is not USA Hockey's ceiling either — the tier is written into Rule 640 itself, and it needs no argument from anywhere else.** **640(g)**: *"A major plus a game misconduct penalty shall be assessed to any player who recklessly endangers an opponent as a result of any actions falling under Rule 640(b, c, d, e or f)."* **640(h)**: *"A match penalty for reckless endangerment may also be assessed under sub-sections (b), (c), (d), (e) or (f) of this rule."* ⚠️ **Note which sub-sections that reaches.** Both get to the late hit through **(b)** — the one written *"(except Adult Male Classifications)"* — while (d), a check delivered *"with no effort to gain possession of the puck"* with the stick blade above the knees, and (e), a check on an opponent already *"physically engaged for possession of the puck"*, carry no such limit. **Whether 640(g) reaches a late hit in adult men's hockey, where (b) is not assessed, the book does not say, and this document does not know.** **Rule 602(a) does not depend on the answer**: *"A match penalty shall be assessed to any player or team official who recklessly endangers or attempts to injure any opposing player or team official"* — tied to no named foul at all. ⚠️ **And a 640(g) major does not end with the game.** Rule 640 is on Rule 411(a)'s aggressive-infractions list, so that major counts on the season tally: a third costs three further games, a fourth five, and a fifth a suspension until a hearing. The whole ladder is set out under [And what these fouls cost beyond tonight](#and-what-these-fouls-cost-beyond-tonight), below.

### The four that cause the injuries

```facts
Rule: Boarding, charging, checking from behind and head contact bind every division, yours included — boarding (7.2, 603), charging (7.4, 607) and checking from behind (7.5, 608) carry no division clause; Hockey Canada splits head contact into 7.6 and 7.7, which between them cover everyone
Never: Make contact in an opponent's head, face or neck with any part of the body, equipment or stick — the onus is on the checker (USA Hockey Rule 620)
Never: Deliver a hit where the head was the main point of contact and it was avoidable — the NHL and IIHF write that test in the same words (both 48.1)
Never: Build speed into a check to punish rather than to win the puck — IIHF "multiple strides", USA Hockey more than two, Hockey Canada two or more
Rule: Charging is building speed to punish, or a running or jumping approach, and is not capped at a minor in any book — NHL 42.2 minor, 42.3 major, 42.4 match; USA Hockey 607(a) writes no bare minor and 607(e) is a match penalty
Rule: Hockey Canada prices charging at 7.4(a) a minor, 7.4(b) a major plus a game misconduct on the degree of violence with nobody hurt and mandatory on injury, and 7.4(c) a match
Rule: The IIHF writes no match penalty anywhere, so a British reader's charging ceiling is a major plus a game misconduct — and unlike head contact at 48.3, Rule 42.4 does not make that game misconduct automatic
Rule: Head contact is not capped at a minor in any book — USA Hockey 620(a) writes no bare minor and 620(c) is a match; Hockey Canada reaches a match at 7.6(c) and 7.7(c); the NHL rules out a major and a game misconduct (48.3, 48.4) but 48.5 is a match penalty
Rule: USA Hockey's rule says a head-contact match penalty "may also be assessed" (620(c)); its Casebook makes it mandatory — a check using excessive force to intimidate or punish that contacts the head "must be assessed as a match penalty" (Rule 620, Situation 4)
Rule: Hockey Canada ejects for head contact with nobody hurt — 7.6(b) gives the referee a major plus a game misconduct on the degree of violence in minor and female hockey, and 7.7(b) assesses that pair flatly for high violence in Junior and Senior
Rule: NHL 48.5 needs intent — a match only where the player "attempted to or deliberately injured" the opponent — and the IIHF, Britain's book, writes no match penalty anywhere, so its head-contact ceiling is a major plus an automatic game misconduct (48.3)
```

These are not ordinary penalties. Treat them as things you simply do not do.

**All four bind every division, including yours if you play non-check.** Hockey Canada scopes only 7.3 — the rule that bars body checking — and USA Hockey only 604; boarding (7.2, 603), charging (7.4, 607) and checking from behind (7.5, 608) carry no division or classification clause at all, and Hockey Canada's head-contact rule opens *"There is no type of legal contact to the head, face, or neck"* and binds *"at all times"*, splitting into 7.6 and 7.7 only so that Junior and Senior are covered separately.

**Checking to the head.** **The IIHF states the principle outright — IIHF Rule 48.1: *"There is no clean check to the head or neck."* The other books get there by describing the illegal hit instead.** NHL Rule 48.1 prohibits a hit where the head "was the main point of contact and such contact to the head was avoidable." USA Hockey Rule 620 goes further: any contact to the head, face or neck with any part of the body, equipment or stick, with the onus on the checker — Rule 620's own Note puts it as avoiding "placing a vulnerable or defenseless opponent in danger of potential injury", and the *"regardless as to size differential"* wording is the Standard of Play's, not the rule's. The **Standard of Play and Rule Emphasis – Body Checking** (HEAD CONTACT) spells out how far that onus runs: "The onus is on the player delivering the check, regardless as to size differential, to not make contact in the head/neck area of the opponent." **And the tiers run further than the NHL's do.** Under the **NHL** an illegal check to the head is a bare minor and, short of intent, nothing else — **48.3** reads *"There is no provision for a major penalty for this rule"* and **48.4** the same for a game misconduct — with **48.5** a match penalty only where the player *"attempted to or deliberately injured"* the opponent. **USA Hockey 620(a)** writes no bare minor at all — a minor plus a misconduct, or a major plus a game misconduct — and **620(c)** reaches a match penalty. ⚠️ **"Reaches" understates that one, because the playing rule and the Casebook are worded differently.** The rule says a match penalty *"may also be assessed"* for head contact; the Casebook says *must*, for exactly the hit this section is about. **Rule 620, Situation 4** asks what degree of force a match penalty needs, and answers that *"[a]ny check using excessive force for the purpose of intimidating or punishing the opponent that makes direct contact with the head area of the opponent must be assessed as a match penalty."* **Situation 3 does the same one rung down**: *"[a]ny time the Referee deems the contact to the head area to be intentional or a reckless act, a major plus game misconduct or match penalty must be assessed."* **So the discretion the rule appears to leave is not discretion about the deliberate hit.** It is about everything below it. **Hockey Canada splits the rule by division:** **7.6** covers minor and female hockey, where 7.6(a) is a bare minor, and **7.7** covers Junior and Senior, where the floor is a minor *and* a misconduct. ⚠️ **Between those floors and those match penalties both rules reach a major plus a game misconduct with nobody hurt — and the two clauses are not shaped alike.** **7.6(b)** leaves it to the referee: that pair *"At the discretion of the Referee, based on the degree of violence of the impact"*, and then compulsory — *"must be assessed"* — where a head-contact infraction that would otherwise be a minor injures the player. **7.7(b)** gives the referee no discretion at all: in Junior and Senior *"a Major penalty and Game Misconduct penalty will be assessed for any Head Contact infraction with a high degree of violence"*, and a further limb assesses the same pair where a player is *"fouled or hit illegally"* and, as a result, hits their head on the glass, ice or boards *"in such a manner that results in a head injury."* **Both also carry the cross-check limb** — a major plus a game misconduct for striking an opponent above the shoulders with a cross-check, *"whether or not injury results"* (§9 sets that out). Only then do both reach a match penalty, at 7.6(c) and 7.7(c). **So under either Hockey Canada rule you can be ejected for head contact without anyone being hurt.** **And in minor and female hockey those minors are counted as well as priced:** three head-contact penalties in one game bring a Game Ejection under Rule 4.8(b)(ii), which is set out in [And what these fouls cost beyond tonight](#and-what-these-fouls-cost-beyond-tonight) below. **The IIHF** starts at a discretionary minor (48.2) and stops at a major plus an automatic game misconduct (48.3) — **no match penalty, because its book contains none anywhere.**

**Charging.** Building speed into a check to punish rather than to win the puck, or a running or jumping approach. **The written threshold differs by book and the difference is one stride:** USA Hockey penalises *more than* two strides of acceleration, Hockey Canada penalises *two or more*, the **IIHF** — and so Britain — sets no number either, defining charging as a player who *"builds up speed by taking multiple strides"* (Rule 42.1), and the NHL sets no number at all. Under Hockey Canada, two strides is already charging. **And charging is not capped at a minor
in any book:** the NHL runs 42.2 minor, 42.3 major, **42.4 a match penalty**; USA Hockey 607(a)
writes no bare minor at all and reaches **607(e)**, a match penalty for reckless endangerment;
Hockey Canada 7.4(a) is a mandatory bare minor, but 7.4(b) lets the referee assess a major plus a
game misconduct *"based on the degree of violence of the impact"* **with nobody hurt** — and makes
that pair mandatory where a charge that would otherwise be a minor injures the opponent; **7.4(c)**
is a match penalty. **The IIHF is the exception, and it is Britain's book** — it
writes no match penalty for charging because it writes none anywhere in the rulebook, so the
ceiling there is a major plus a game misconduct (Rule 42.4 — which, unlike its head-contact
equivalent at 48.3, does not make the game misconduct automatic).

### Boarding, and the player who cannot see you coming

```facts
Rule: NHL Rule 41.1 is boarding — checking or pushing a defenceless opponent so they hit the boards "violently or dangerously" — and the definition names the icing/offside case in terms
Rule: Under USA Hockey boarding is not only a hit — its Rule 603 Note reaches a player who "pushes, trips or body checks" an opponent dangerously into the boards, and its Casebook prefers the boarding call to tripping (Rule 603, Situation 3)
Rule: Boarding is not capped at two minutes — NHL 41.2 minor, 41.3 major, 41.4 match, and 41.5 adds a game misconduct whenever a major follows an injury to the face or head; USA Hockey 603(a) writes no bare minor at all and 603(c) is a match penalty
Rule: Hockey Canada 7.2(b) reaches a major plus a game misconduct on the degree of violence of the impact with nobody hurt, and makes that pair mandatory where a board injures; 7.2(c) is a match penalty
Rule: Under the IIHF — Britain's book — boarding reaches a major plus a game misconduct with nobody hurt (41.4) and no match penalty exists; unlike checking from behind at 43.3, that game misconduct is not automatic
Never: Hit a player playing an obvious icing or offside puck into the boards — boarding in NHL 41.1, IIHF 41.1 and Hockey Canada 7.2 alike; USA Hockey reaches only the icing half, at 624(d)
Never: Arrive as the third man and hit someone already engaged in a battle — USA Hockey deems both players defenceless
Rule: Under IIHF, a check on an opponent who no longer has the puck and is either unaware of the hit or unprepared for it is automatically reckless, however clean it would otherwise be (IIHF glossary, "Vulnerable Position")
```

**Boarding.** NHL Rule 41.1: checking or pushing "a defenseless opponent in such a manner that causes the opponent to hit or impact the boards violently or dangerously." ⚠️ **Under USA Hockey, though, boarding is not only a hit — and this is the half of the rule most players never meet.** Three of the four books define it by the check: *"checks or pushes"* in NHL 41.1, IIHF 41.1 and Hockey Canada 7.2 alike. **USA Hockey's Rule 603 Note names a third action, and it is a stick foul** — boarding is *"the action where a player pushes, trips or body checks an opponent causing them to go dangerously into the boards."* Its Casebook takes that literally: at **Rule 603, Situation 3** a trailing player *"trips the opponent and causes them to lose control and crash into the end boards"*, and the answer to whether boarding may be called is *"Yes… Even though a tripping penalty may also be appropriate, the boarding call is preferred to draw attention to the more aggressive infraction."* ⚠️ **So under USA Hockey a stick trip that sends somebody into the end boards is not a two-minute trip. It is boarding, the referee is told to prefer that call, and 603(a) writes no bare minor — so the floor is a minor plus a misconduct.** A trip near the end wall is not the cheap option. **Back to the NHL's rule, and the specific case it calls out:** "Any unnecessary contact with a player playing the puck on an obvious 'icing' or 'off-side' play which results in that player hitting or impacting the boards is 'boarding'." A player skating hard to touch up an icing is not looking for you — and that clause is in three books word for word: NHL 41.1, IIHF 41.1 and Hockey Canada 7.2 all print *"off-side"* hyphenated, and the only difference between them is the capitalised *"Player"* the IIHF and Hockey Canada use; USA Hockey reaches only the icing half of it, at 624(d), without the offside half and without naming boarding. ⚠️ **And boarding is not capped at two minutes.** The NHL runs 41.2 minor, 41.3 major, 41.4 match, and **41.5 adds a game misconduct whenever a major follows an injury to the face or head**. USA Hockey 603(a) has no bare minor at all; Hockey Canada 7.2(b) assesses a major plus a game misconduct *"At the discretion of the Referee, based on the degree of violence of the impact"* — **with nobody hurt** — and *then* makes that same pair mandatory where a board that would otherwise be a minor injures; and under the IIHF book — the British one — 41.4 reaches a major plus a game misconduct with nobody hurt. **And the cost can outlive the game, for most of these fouls and not just boarding** — see [And what these fouls cost beyond tonight](#and-what-these-fouls-cost-beyond-tonight) below. **So on an icing race, take the puck and let him go.** The four-book comparison is in [Rules Primer](../foundation/rules_primer.md). **And boarding reaches a match penalty in three of the four books:** NHL **41.4** where the player *"attempted to or deliberately injured his opponent by boarding"*, **USA Hockey 603(c)** — a match *"for reckless endangerment"*, above a 603(a) that writes no bare minor at all — and **Hockey Canada 7.2(c)** where a player *"deliberately attempts to or deliberately injures an opponent by boarding"*. **The IIHF stops at a major plus a game misconduct (41.4), and that game misconduct is not automatic** — unlike its checking-from-behind and head-contact equivalents at 43.3 and 48.3.

⚠️ **One narrow thing USA Hockey's boarding rule does permit — set out here because the rule sets it out, and not as anything to aim for.** The sentence immediately after 603(a) reads: *"'Rolling' an opponent along the boards where they are attempting to go through too small an opening is not considered boarding."* **Read carefully what that is and what it is not.** It is a limit on the word *boarding*, covering a player who tried to force a gap along the wall that was never there — the same idea as being entitled to the ice you are already standing on. **It is not a technique, it is not a licence to drive anybody into the wall, and it is the only book of the four that writes it**: no counterpart appears in NHL Rule 41, IIHF Rule 41 or Hockey Canada Rule 7.2. **And it limits that one word only.** It does nothing to checking from behind (608), head contact (620) or charging (607); it does not touch 603's own Note, which still reaches a push, a trip or a check that sends an opponent dangerously into the boards; and it does not reach 602(a)'s match penalty for recklessly endangering an opponent, which is tied to no named foul at all. **Nothing about knowing this sentence should change how you play the wall.**

**And one more that is easy to commit without realising:** USA Hockey states that "when two or more players are physically engaged for control of the puck along the boards, they are considered to be vulnerable or defenseless," and any body check delivered to a player already engaged with another skater "is considered dangerous, careless or reckless (unacceptable) and must be penalized accordingly." **Do not arrive as the third man and hit someone already in a battle.** Take the puck instead.

**The IIHF book gets to the same place by a different route, and a British reader should know which one binds them.** It has no board-battle clause, but it defines a **Vulnerable Position** in its glossary: *"A skater is considered to be in a vulnerable position when they are no longer in control or possession of the puck and they are either not aware of an impending hit or they are not prepared for the hit. A bodycheck to an opponent who is vulnerable is automatically considered reckless even if that bodycheck would be considered legal to a non-vulnerable opponent."* A player wrestling for a loose puck with someone else has neither possession nor any preparation for a third arrival, so that hit is reckless under the IIHF book too — arrived at through the state of the player rather than the fact of the battle. **Reckless matters, because recklessness is the trigger for the major-and-game-misconduct tier** in IIHF Rules 42.4, 43.3 and 48 — and in British women's hockey, at every age and adult play included, any major brings a game misconduct anyway (In-House Rule 101's ejection bullet applies *"in all cases"* and states no age scope), with junior hockey reading the same way on In-House Rule 201's scoping of the IIHF junior rules.

### Checking from behind, and what the onus does and does not mean

```facts
Never: Check from behind — major plus automatic game misconduct (NHL/IIHF 43); match penalties at NHL 43.4, USA Hockey 608(c) and Hockey Canada 7.5(c); floors 608(a) and 7.5(a), neither a bare minor
Rule: The onus for checking from behind is USA Hockey Rule 608's — "The onus is on the player delivering the check to not hit from behind" — and Hockey Canada's, which puts it on the checker "in all circumstances"; NHL Rule 43 states no onus at all
Rule: "He turned at the last second" is no defence unless the turn was deliberate — a player who intentionally turns to create contact with their back draws the checker no penalty for checking from behind (NHL Rule 43.1; IIHF Rule 43.1, which adds that other penalties may still be assessed)
Rule: That waiver is the NHL's and the IIHF's alone — USA Hockey and Hockey Canada write no such clause, and Hockey Canada's Interpretation 2 to Rule 7.5(a) gives the penalty to the checker anyway: "the responsibility is always on the player delivering the check to avoid making contact with the back"
Rule: An onus written into a boarding rule is not strict liability — NHL Rule 41.1, IIHF Rule 41.1 and Hockey Canada Rule 7.2 all let the referee weigh whether the opponent put themself in a vulnerable position, and whether the check was unavoidable
Rule: USA Hockey's boarding rule is the one that attaches no balance — "The onus is on the player delivering the check to avoid placing a vulnerable or defenseless opponent in danger", with no however-clause (USA Hockey Rule 603 Note; its Casebook weighs force against preparedness at 603 Situation 2)
Never: Rely on that balance — it is written into the boarding rules, the two books that state an onus for checking from behind attach no "however", and a spinal injury is not undone by a favourable call
Rule: A fifth book has no rung that leaves you on the ice — CARHA 53(a) is a minor plus a game misconduct at the floor, a major plus a game misconduct compulsory on injury, and 53(b) a flat match penalty for driving a player from behind into the boards or goal net (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only)
```

**Checking from behind.** NHL Rule 43.1 defines it as a check "delivered on a player who is not aware of the impending hit, therefore unable to protect or defend himself," with contact on the back part of the body. **There is no minor penalty for it** (NHL Rule 43.2) — it is a major, and a game misconduct is automatic whenever the major is called (43.5). IIHF **Rule 43** matches this: 43.2 states that "this infraction is considered serious and dangerous, therefore there is no option to award a minor penalty," and 43.3 provides a major plus an automatic game misconduct. **USA Hockey Rule 608** assesses minor-plus-misconduct or major-plus-game-misconduct (608(a)), a major plus game misconduct where an opponent is recklessly endangered or sent head first into the boards (608(b)), and a match penalty for reckless endangerment of a vulnerable opponent (608(c)). **Two of the four books here provide a minor for it, and neither writes a bare one.** USA Hockey's, above, arrives with a misconduct — the lowest floor of the four, and the only one that leaves you on the ice. Hockey Canada's Rule 7.5(a) assesses "a Minor penalty **and** a Game Misconduct penalty" for hitting an opponent from behind anywhere on the ice, 7.5(b) lets the referee assess a major and game misconduct instead, *"At the discretion of the Referee, based on the degree of violence of the impact"* and **with nobody hurt** — and where a checking-from-behind infraction that "would otherwise call for a Minor penalty" injures the player, that pair "must be assessed" — and **7.5(c) is a mandatory match penalty**, in its own words *"A Match penalty **will** be assessed to any player who pushes, body-checks, cross-checks, or hits an opposing player in any manner from behind into the boards or goal frame, in such a way that the player is unable to protect or defend themselves"*, with no violence threshold and no injury threshold at all. **The NHL has a match penalty here too** — 43.4, at the referee's discretion, where the player *"attempted to or deliberately injured his opponent by checking from behind."* So a Canadian reader should not infer that two minutes is on the table: under all four books it costs you more than two minutes, and under three of them your game. ⚠️ **A fifth book has no rung at all that leaves you on the ice.** **CARHA Rule 53(a)** — CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only, and no reader in minor hockey — assesses *"[a] Minor penalty plus a Game Misconduct or a Major penalty plus a Game Misconduct at the discretion of the Referee, based on the degree of violence of the impact… to any player who intentionally pushes, body checks, or hits an opposing player from behind, anywhere on the ice"*, makes the major and game misconduct compulsory *"[i]f a player is injured"*, and adds that *"[a] Match penalty could also be assessed under this rule."* **Rule 53(b)** is that match penalty written flat, for a player *"pushed, hit or propelled in any manner from behind into the boards or goal net, in such a way that the player is unable to protect or defend themselves"* — and its Note tells referees they are *"instructed not to substitute other penalties when a player is checked from behind in any manner. This rule must be strictly enforced."* So the lowest tier in that book ends the game, and the relief USA Hockey 608(a) gives is not in it. **The onus is on the checker — and it is the North American minor-hockey books that say so.** USA Hockey states it in those words, with no qualification, in **Rule 608** itself: "The onus is on the player delivering the check to not hit from behind." Hockey Canada states the same principle in its own checking-from-behind rule, which reads *"In all circumstances the responsibility is on the player delivering the check to avoid"* contact to an opposing player's back. (Hockey Canada does use "the onus is always", but in Rule 8.5, on goaltender interference, which is a different rule and not this one.) **The NHL's rule contains no onus clause at all** — Rule 43 gives the definition, the penalty tiers and the intentional-turn carve-out, and nothing else. The only place the NHL's rulebook puts an onus on a checker is **Rule 41.1, boarding**, quoted below — everywhere else the word appears it is about maintaining your equipment. Do not cite Rule 43 for the onus. "He turned at the last second" is not a defence unless the turn was deliberate — NHL Rule 43.1 waives the penalty only when a player "intentionally turns his body to create contact with his back," and IIHF Rule 43.1 has its own carve-out, in its own words, for a player who "intentionally turns their back towards an opponent and puts themself in a vulnerable position" — adding, as the NHL does not, that "other penalties may still be assessed." So under the IIHF book what the carve-out waives is the checking-from-behind penalty specifically, not the checker's whole liability. Note which way that carve-out cuts: **a player who turns his back deliberately to draw the call gets nothing.** It is not a way to win a power play, and it is the position that breaks necks. **And that waiver is the NHL's and the IIHF's alone.** USA Hockey and Hockey Canada write no such clause — and USA Hockey's separate Casebook writes none either: its Rule 608 situations set out when each tier must be assessed and attach no exception to a player who turned, requiring a major plus a game misconduct or a match penalty *"in every instance where a player forcefully checks an opponent who is standing along the boards (back toward the middle of the ice)"* — and **Hockey Canada's Interpretation 2 to Rule 7.5(a)** puts the question directly — *"What penalty is assessed when a player, who is about to be checked, turns and, as a result, creates contact with the back?"* — and answers it the other way: the penalty goes to the checker, because *"the responsibility is always on the player delivering the check to avoid making contact with the back."*

**Two things the word "onus" does not mean.** It is not strict liability — and the statement of that sits in the NHL's **boarding** rule, which is the one place the NHL's book puts an onus on a checker at all. Rule 41.1: "The onus is on the player applying the check to ensure his opponent is not in a defenseless position and if so, he must avoid or minimize contact. **However**, in determining whether such contact could have been avoided, the circumstances of the check, including whether the opponent put himself in a vulnerable position immediately prior to or simultaneously with the check or whether the check was unavoidable can be considered. This balance must be considered by the Referees when applying this rule." So a boarding call is judged on a balance, not on outcome alone. **That balance is not the NHL's alone, and it is not in every book either.** **IIHF Rule 41.1 — the British reader's boarding rule — writes the same passage almost word for word**, with *"The responsibility is on the Player applying the check"* where the NHL says "onus", and the same *"However, in determining whether such contact could have been avoided, the circumstances of the check, including whether the opponent put themself in a vulnerable position immediately prior to or simultaneously with the check or whether the check was unavoidable can be considered. This balance must be considered by the Referees when applying this rule."* **Hockey Canada Rule 7.2 writes it too** — *"The duty is on the Player applying the check to ensure their opponent is not in a defenseless position"*, followed by the same however-clause, ending *"may be considered"* rather than "can be considered" and without the NHL's and IIHF's closing sentence — and Hockey Canada's boarding rule carries no division scope, so that balance is available in minor and female hockey as much as anywhere else. **USA Hockey's is the outlier, and it runs against you as the checker:** Rule 603's Note states the onus flat — *"The onus is on the player delivering the check to avoid placing a vulnerable or defenseless opponent in danger"* — and attaches no balancing sentence to it at all. ⚠️ **Its Casebook is not silent, though, and what it writes is a different weighing.** Rule 603, Situation 2 restates the onus first, scoped — *"For USA Hockey youth games, the onus is on the player delivering the check to do so in a manner that does not place the opponent in danger"* — then asks *"was the check reckless and/or what was the vulnerability of the player being checked?"* and works it both ways: a penalty *"may be assessed when a player was not necessarily hit that hard, but was considered to be in a vulnerable or defenseless position when unnecessarily hit"*, and *"a penalty may not be warranted when a player is hit much harder, but their preparedness for the hit causes them to go only mildly into the boards."* **The Casebook prints that weighing twice, and the second printing is narrower.** Standard of Play, Situation 19 asks *"was the check **necessary** and/or what was the vulnerability of the player being checked?"* — *necessary* where Situation 2 says *reckless* — and its no-penalty example carries a condition Situation 2's does not: a penalty *"may not be warranted when a player is hit much harder as an opponent clearly attempted to gain possession of the puck, but their preparedness for the hit causes them to go only mildly into the boards."* **So even USA Hockey's own relief turns on your having been going for the puck.** **That is force weighed against the opponent's preparedness — not the vulnerability-and-unavoidability balance the other three books write**, so it is not the excuse those books offer, and it is not in the playing rule. **And note the limit of all of it.** Those balancing sentences are written into the boarding rules; the two books that state an onus for *checking from behind* — USA Hockey 608 and Hockey Canada's — attach no "however" to it at all, and Hockey Canada's says "in all circumstances". So the qualification you are relying on may not exist in your rulebook. **None of that is a licence to lean on it.** You cannot know in advance how a referee will weigh the balance, you are the only one of the two players who chose to arrive, and the reason to give a turned player the ice is that a spinal injury is not undone by a favourable call. Play it as though the onus were absolute; just do not tell a teammate the rulebook says it is.

### And what these fouls cost beyond tonight

```facts
Rule: A game misconduct can cost you the next game — USA Hockey 404(b) suspends you for the first one, and a second major in the same game costs two: the two games are 403(b)'s and are additional to any other suspension from the incident, and 404(b) calls them a minimum
Rule: USA Hockey 411(a) adds a three-game suspension on a third major of the season across its aggressive-infractions list — seventeen rules, boarding, charging, checking from behind and head contact among them, ending with 639 and Rule 640 Unnecessary Roughness (Roughing)
Rule: A 640(g) late-hit major is an aggressive infraction and counts on that tally, and USA Hockey 411(a) does not stop at three — a fourth major in the category is a further five-game suspension, a fifth a suspension until a hearing. Fighting is not on that list — 411's Note sends it to Rule 615(f)
Rule: NHL 23.6 suspends a second game misconduct in the Physical Infractions Category inside 41 consecutive regular-season games, and 23.5 does the same for stick fouls
Rule: Hockey Canada 4.8(c) suspends a game misconduct given in the last 10 minutes, in overtime or after the game — a minimum of the next game or seven days
Rule: The IIHF Rule Book writes no automatic next-game suspension for a player — 23.2 gives the Proper Authorities "full power to impose further disciplinary measures" and 28.1 lets them act "at their discretion"
Rule: The IIHF Rule Book is not the IIHF's whole discipline — Rules 23.3 to 23.6 name four disciplinary categories, including the Stick and Physical Infractions categories the NHL tariffs, and refer them to the separate IIHF Disciplinary Code, which is not held here and has not been checked
Rule: In Hockey Canada's Minor and Female divisions, three Minor or double Minor penalties for Rule 7.6 head contact in the same game bring a Game Ejection (Rule 4.8(b)(ii)) — and Rule 4.8's Note 1 adds that a Game Ejection is not a game misconduct and triggers no automatic suspension
Rule: A double Minor counts once towards a Hockey Canada Game Ejection — Interpretation 2 to Rule 4.8(b) reads "a double Minor penalty will be considered one infraction", so an intentional head-contact double Minor is one mark of the three, not two
Key: A game misconduct is never only tonight — every one of the four books lets it reach the next game or beyond, and your own league's supplementary discipline sits on top of all four
```

All four books let a game misconduct reach
beyond the game it was given in, each on its own trigger — three of them automatically, and
the IIHF's at the Proper Authorities' discretion. Under **USA Hockey Rule 404(b)** a player
incurring a game misconduct *"shall also be suspended for the team's next game"* — the
first one, not the second. **A game misconduct for a second major in the same game costs two
games, and two is a floor rather than a tariff.** **Rule 403(b)** sets the number: the game
misconduct for that second major suspends the player for their team's *"next two games"*, and
that *"two-game suspension is in addition to any other required suspensions incurred during the
same incident."* The word *"minimum"* belongs to 404(b) instead — the same sentence quoted just
above ends by saying that where the game misconduct is imposed under 403(b) *"there shall be a
minimum two-game suspension."*
**Rule 411(a)** adds a three-game suspension on a
player's third major of the season for any combination of its listed aggressive
infractions. That list runs to seventeen rules — boarding, charging, checking from
behind and head contact among them, and ending with **Rule 639**
(tripping, clipping, leg checking, slew footing) and **Rule 640 Unnecessary
Roughness (Roughing)**. The last entry is the one to notice, because it is the rule
the late hit lives in: a **640(g)** major is an aggressive infraction and counts on
that tally. **And the ladder does not stop at three.** A fourth major in the same
category is a further five-game suspension, and a fifth suspends the player *"until
a hearing is conducted by the proper authorities"* — with all of them *"in addition
to any other suspensions imposed through the official playing rules."* **Fighting is
not on that list**: Rule 411's own Note sends it to a separate progressive-suspension
rule at Rule 615(f).
The **NHL** counts by category rather than by season: two game misconducts in the
**Physical Infractions Category** before playing 41 consecutive regular-season games
without one brings an automatic suspension under **Rule 23.6**, and **Rule 23.5** does
the same for the Stick Infractions Category. **Hockey Canada Rule 4.8(c)** attaches to
*when* rather than to how many — a game misconduct *"in the last 10 minutes of regular
playing time, at any time in overtime, or after the conclusion of the game"* brings an
automatic suspension of *"a minimum of the next regular league/play-off game or seven
days."* The **IIHF** writes no automatic trigger at all. **Rule 23.2** sends a player incurring a game
misconduct to the Proper Authorities, *"which shall have full power to impose further
disciplinary measures"*, and **Rule 28.1** lets the Proper
Authorities investigate and assess additional discipline *"at their discretion"*,
*"whether or not such offense has been penalized by the Referee(s)"* — so a British
reader's exposure is discretionary rather than tariffed, which is not the same as
smaller. *(The book's only *"automatically suspended"* is **Rule 46.11**, and it is a different thing: a **Coach or other team personnel** drawn into an altercation is assessed a game misconduct *"and automatically suspended from the game, ordered to the dressing room"* — an ejection from the game in progress, not a tariff on the next one. The half of Rule 46.11 that covers **players** carries no suspension language at all.)* **That is true of the IIHF *Rule Book*, which is what was read here**; Rules 23.3
to 23.6 name four disciplinary categories — including the same Stick and Physical Infractions
categories the NHL tariffs — and refer them to the separate **IIHF Disciplinary Code**, which
is not held here and has not been checked. **Your own league's supplementary discipline is a further layer on top of all
of this, and none of these books governs it.**

**One Hockey Canada consequence runs the other way, and head contact is where it
bites.** Hockey Canada counts head-contact minors inside a single game. **Rule 4.8(b)**
assesses a **Game Ejection** *"in the following circumstances"*, and **4.8(b)(ii)** is one
of them: *"[w]hen a player, in Minor or Female divisions, is assessed three Minor or double
Minor penalties for Rule 7.6 – Head Contact in the same game."* The head-contact rule states
the same counter from its own end — **Rule 7.6(a)**: *"In minor and female hockey, any player
incurring three Head Contact penalties under Rule 7.6 (a) will be assessed a Game Ejection
penalty."* **Note the scope, because both places write it in:** minor and female hockey.
Junior and Senior head contact is **Rule 7.7**, and neither the counter nor 7.6(a) names it.
**A double minor counts once.** **Interpretation 2 to Rule 4.8(b)** reads *"For the purposes
of accumulation towards a Game Ejection penalty, a double Minor penalty will be considered one
infraction"* — so the intentional head contact 7.6(a) prices at a double minor puts one mark
against you rather than two, and 4.8(b)(ii)'s *"three Minor or double Minor penalties"* lists
which penalties count rather than counting minutes. **And a Game Ejection is not a game
misconduct.** **Rule 4.8's Note 1** says so in terms — *"A Game Ejection is not equal to a
Game Misconduct penalty and a player receiving a Game Ejection penalty does not trigger any
automatic suspension"* (printed after 4.8(c) and not tied to a sub-clause). So a third
head-contact minor ends your night, and unlike the game misconducts set out above, the
rulebook attaches nothing past the final whistle. Your own league's supplementary discipline
may still act on it.

---

## 6. Receiving a Hit Safely

**Read this section whatever league you play in** — unexpected contact happens in non-check hockey too, and the boards and the goal posts are just as hard.

### Keep your head up. Always. This one rule prevents most of it.

```facts
Never: Skate with your head down — not "look up sometimes", never
Key: You cannot brace for something you cannot see; a body prepared for contact absorbs it
Rule: Under the IIHF book only — a square check with no upward motion on a puck carrier skating head down toward the checker is not an illegal check to the head, the checker must still avoid your head, and the IIHF attaches no penalty to the player whose head was down (IIHF Rule 48.1)
Rule: The IIHF's head-down carve-out is a list of conditions and not a general licence — a head-down player hit from the side or the blind side, or hit without the puck, sits outside it entirely, and it reaches only a puck carrier skating toward the checker (IIHF Rule 48.1)
Rule: Hockey Canada gives a checker no Head Contact penalty when the opponent ducks into the hit — Junior and Senior divisions only, and never where the head is low as the result of a legitimate hockey play such as shooting or taking a pass (Interpretation 3 to Rule 7.7(a))
Rule: Below Junior and Senior that interpretation does not apply — Rule 7.6 governs in minor and female hockey, requiring head contact to be penalised regardless of the circumstances, so the checker is penalised too (Rule 7.6)
Rule: Hockey Canada Rule 8.7 bars crouching to avoid a bodycheck in every division, and the ducker's own penalty is not capped at a minor — see Rules Primer for the tiers (Rule 8.7)
Action: Learn to carry the puck without staring at it — it is a stickhandling problem, and it is solved off the ice
```

Not "look up sometimes." Never skate with your head down. Two separate reasons, both serious:

1. **You cannot brace for something you cannot see.** A body prepared for contact absorbs it. A body that is surprised does not.
2. **Under the IIHF book, the rules protect you less when your head is down.** IIHF Rule 48.1 states that a player who checks an opponent "who is skating with the puck with their head down in the direction of the Player and does not use an upward motion or drive their body up into the opponent, shall not be penalized for an 'illegal check to the head'." Even under the IIHF book the checker is still obliged not to target your head; what that book declines to penalise is only the square hit with no upward motion, on a player who has the puck and is skating **toward** the checker. **All four of those are conditions, not description.** A head-down skater hit from the side or the blind side, or hit without the puck, is not inside that carve-out at all — the sentence reaches the carrier coming at you and nothing else. No such exception was found in USA Hockey's Playing Rules, nor anywhere in Hockey Canada's rules, in any division — Rule 7.6 and Rule 7.7 both, with their Interpretations, and where Hockey Canada's book mentions a head-down player at all it runs the other way, in Rule 7.5, which tells referees to strictly enforce checking from behind on *"a player along the boards, with their head down looking for the puck or 'digging the puck out,'"* who is *"hit from behind and driven head-first into the boards."* **And under Hockey Canada the book can do worse than decline to protect you — the *checker's* amnesty is confined to junior and senior.** **Rule 8.7's prohibition on crouching to avoid a check carries no division scope**, so the ducker's own exposure reaches every division. Hockey Canada's Interpretation 3 to Rule 7.7(a) provides that in *"Junior and Senior divisions"* a player who *"ducks to avoid a check"* and is hit in the head area by *"an otherwise legal check"* draws no Head Contact penalty against the checker, and that the referee *"may assess a penalty to the player who ducks to avoid a check under Rule 8.7 — Clipping"* — a minor under Hockey Canada Rule 8.7(a), and **the minor is the floor rather than the ceiling**: 8.7(b) reaches a major plus a game misconduct at the referee's discretion on *"the degree of violence of impact (including the impact with the ice or boards)"*, **with nobody hurt**, and mandatorily where the duck injures the checker; and 8.7(c) is a match penalty for an attempt to injure or a deliberate one. That Hockey Canada interpretation *"does not apply to a player who is in a vulnerable position as the result of a legitimate hockey play (e.g. shooting, making or receiving a pass, etc.)"*, so a head that is low because you are shooting or taking a pass is not a duck. Full treatment in [Common Mistakes](#common-mistakes).

Learning to carry the puck without staring at it is a stickhandling problem, and it is solved off the ice. See [Puck Handling](puck_handling.md).

### Do not turn your back to an oncoming checker near the boards

```facts
Never: Turn your back to a checker near the boards. It gives them your spine, puts your head first into the wall, and makes it a hit from behind — even a light one could inflict a severe head or neck injury
Key: A chin tucked to the chest straightens the cervical spine; the head stops, the body keeps going, and vertebrae fracture
Risk: The spinal injury from a head-down, head-first collision with the boards or a goal post can occur at walking speed — no speed is slow enough to make one safe
Action: Case 1, best of all — arrive on an angle, not perpendicular
Action: Case 2, contact coming — skates parallel to the boards, forearm and upper thigh to the wall, knees bent, lean into the impact
Action: Case 3, already facing the wall — spread the load, stick and gloves up on the glass, arms out as a shock absorber
Never: Take the wall on the tip of your shoulder, or crouch or duck to avoid a check
Rule: Turning into a hit deliberately to draw a call earns nothing — a player who intentionally turns to create contact with their back draws the checker no penalty for checking from behind (NHL Rule 43.1; IIHF Rule 43.1, which adds that other penalties may still be assessed)
Key: Head up, chin off your chest — hit the boards or a post with an arm, a leg or anything but your head first
```

**Never turn your back to the wall, at any speed and in any league.** It is worth understanding why it is so tempting: when contact is coming and you are facing the wall, turning away feels like protecting yourself. It is the opposite. Turning gives the checker your back and your unprotected spine, and puts your head first into the boards. **It also converts the collision into a hit from behind**, which is the one USA Hockey addresses to the checker in the plainest terms it uses anywhere: *"Even a light hit from behind could inflict a severe head or neck injury."* You cannot see it coming and you cannot brace for it — and read that sentence for what it says: **the warning is written to cover the light hit**, so a slow checker is not the safe case. **Neither this nor the head-down collision described below is the lesser hazard.** No source cited here ranks the two against each other; both prohibitions are absolute, and USA Hockey's walking-speed finding below is stated of the head-down collision rather than of the turn.

**And if you were thinking of turning on purpose to draw the call, the rulebook has already closed that door.** NHL Rule 43.1: "When a player intentionally turns his body to create contact with his back, no penalty shall be assessed." IIHF Rule 43.1 says the same at more length, for a player who "intentionally turns their back towards an opponent and puts themself in a vulnerable position immediately before a bodycheck, to create a 'checking from behind' situation" — though it adds that "other penalties may still be assessed" against the checker on some other ground. You take the whole risk and you do not get the power play.

The standard teaching against it is the **"Heads Up, Don't Duck"** programme, begun in 1995 by Dr Alan Ashare and carried by the Hockey Equipment Certification Council and USA Hockey. Its whole content is one posture: head up, chin off your chest, whatever else is happening.

**Two facts from USA Hockey's *Heads Up Hockey* programme that make the hazard concrete.** First, the mechanism: the upper spinal column has a natural curve that gives the head and neck flexibility in a normal heads-up position, but *"when the head is flexed (chin toward the chest), this normal curve is removed, and the cervical spine becomes straight."* In that head-down position, hitting the boards or a post head-on stops the head while the body keeps moving, *"compressing the spine. This force can produce a shock greater than the neck's discs and muscles can cushion, resulting in a fracture or break of one [or] more vertebrae. And if one breaks, it can cause compression of the spinal cord, resulting in paralysis below the level of the fracture."* (The guide prints "one *of* more"; the bracket is this document's correction of a typo in the original, not a change to its meaning.) The finding beneath it is one USA Hockey attributes rather than claims: *"According to research done among a wide range of hockey players,"* it says, almost all on-ice cervical spine injuries have been due to the head being slightly flexed on head-on contact with the boards or goal post — and the research it prints underneath is **Charles H. Tator and colleagues, *Spinal Injuries Due To Hockey*, Canadian Journal of Neurological Sciences 11:34–41.** Second, and this is the part players get wrong: **_"A player doesn't have to be going at full speed for this to happen — it can occur at walking speed."_** There is no speed slow enough to make a head-down collision with the wall safe.

⚠️ **The paper USA Hockey credits for that "almost all" is a six-patient case series, and the paper itself does not carry the quantifier.** *Spinal Injuries Due To Hockey* is indexed as a case report. Its published abstract records six patients with cervical spinal injury due to hockey treated at a single Toronto unit — the Acute Spinal Cord Injury Unit, Sunnybrook Medical Centre — between 1974 and 1981, of whom *"four struck the boards with the neck flexed, and one struck another player with the neck flexed"*, and it closes *"[t]he reasons for the increase in spinal injuries in hockey are unknown."* So *"almost all"* and *"a wide range of hockey players"* are **USA Hockey's characterisation of the research**, not that paper's own finding. **The mechanism is not what is in doubt, and larger work by the same author points the same way.** A national survey of the 42 injured players reported to the Committee on Prevention of Spinal Injuries due to Hockey between 1976 and 1983 found that *"[s]trikes from behind and collisions with the boards were common mechanisms of injury"* and that *"[m]any of the players had suffered a burst fracture of the cervical spine following a blow to the top of the helmet when the neck was slightly flexed"* (Tator and Edmonds, *National survey of spinal injuries in hockey players*, **Canadian Medical Association Journal 1984;130:875–880**). And of 355 Canadian cases registered between 1943 and 2011, **78.9% were cervical-spine injuries** and **64.2% resulted from impact with the boards**, with a check or push from behind *"still the most common cause of injury"* at **36.0%** (Tator, Provvidenza and Cassidy, **Clinical Journal of Sport Medicine 2016;26:232–238**). ⚠️ **None of the three sets the head-down posture against every other posture**, so read *"almost all"* as USA Hockey's summary rather than as a measured proportion. **What none of this touches is the instruction.** Head up, chin off your chest, never duck and never turn your back — all three papers put the flexed neck and the boards at the centre of the injury, and nothing here gives a player a reason to arrive at the wall any other way. (The two 1984 papers and the 2016 series were read here in their published abstracts and bibliographic records; their full texts were not.)

Hockey Canada's checking-from-behind rule lists the two scenarios its officials are told to watch for, and both are about a player who has turned or bent: a player who "turns and, as a result, creates contact with the back," and "a player along the boards, with their head down looking for the puck or 'digging the puck out,'" who "is hit from behind and driven head-first into the boards."

**What to do instead — USA Hockey's decision rule, in three ranked cases.** The full rule is here. [Skating → Arriving at the boards](skating.md#arriving-at-the-boards--head-up-skates-parallel-take-it-along-your-side) teaches Case 1 only, because choosing your angle is a skating decision made several strides out, and its Common Mistakes and Key Takeaways summarise Case 2.

**Case 1 — best of all: arrive on an angle, not perpendicular.** *"Skating into the boards at an angle means better puck control and less risk of injury… When players skate into the corners at an angle, the risk of hitting the boards with their helmets first is greatly reduced. A leg or side or an arm will absorb most of the impact"* (USA Hockey, *Introduction To Body Contact*, "Angling In"). Even better: **take the puck off the wall before you get there** — see "Get off the wall" below.

**Case 2 — contact is coming and you can still choose how you meet the wall: skates parallel to the boards, and take it along your side.** The manual's teaching points for a player checked while skating parallel to the boards are *"press your forearm and upper thigh (hip area) against the boards"* and *"keep a low center of gravity with your feet apart and knees bent"*; the angling-in list adds *"lean into the impact"* and *"absorb the shock over the widest possible part of your body."* Your shoulder and hip turn toward the **checker**; your forearm and hip meet the **wall**.

- **Do not take the wall on the point of your shoulder.** USA Hockey says it twice: *"Avoid hitting the boards with the shoulder"* and *"Never hit the boards or glass with the tip of your shoulder – it can cause injury."*

**Case 3 — you are already facing the boards and cannot turn: spread the load across everything you have.** *"If checked while facing the boards, distribute the force of the impact over as wide a surface as possible, including putting your stick and gloves on the glass to cushion the blow"*; the listed common error is *"not getting your stick and gloves up and spread out on the glass."* The teaching drill has players *"make contact with the boards and glass with their extended arms, forearms, side of body and legs, but with no head contact at all"*, with the arms out forming a *"three-part 'shock absorber'."* **This is damage limitation, not the position you are aiming for** — the manual's own wording is to turn your skates parallel *"if at all possible."*

**In every case, and non-negotiable:** head up, chin off your chest, and *"hit the boards or goal posts with an arm, a leg or anything but your head first"* (Look Up Line, "Heads Up, Don't Duck"). Never your back to the wall, never duck. A tucked head is what turns a hit into a neck injury.

> **Three pieces of rink-side advice you will hear that the primary coaching material does not support.**
>
> **"Take the wall on your shoulder."** Players and coaches say this constantly. USA Hockey's own material, quoted above, says the opposite: *"never hit the boards or glass with the tip of your shoulder"*. So take it on the forearm and hip. (Your shoulder does face the *checker*; it is the boards it should not meet.)
>
> **"Get square to the boards."** No coaching material teaches chest-first squaring up as the orientation to aim for. It appears only as the Case 3 fallback for a player who is already facing the wall. The target orientation is **parallel**.
>
> **"Get your hands off the boards — forearms, not hands."** USA Hockey teaches the opposite: get your **stick and gloves up and spread out on the glass**, and it names failing to do so as the common error. The related warning that *"a locked, straight arm into the boards is how wrists and collarbones break"* is widely repeated but **has no hockey safety authority behind it.** Treat it as unverified coaching craft. *(The nearest real evidence is not about hockey at all. In a laboratory study of forward falls onto the hand, Chou and colleagues found that "the elbow mediolateral shear force was 68% larger" when the arm was extended rather than flexed at impact, and that elbow flexion "reduces the first peak impact force value and postpones the maximum peak value" — but they measured **no** significant difference in axial force at wrist, elbow or shoulder, and a vertical fall onto a floor is not a skater hitting a wall. **How small a fall matters here:** eleven volunteers were dropped onto force plates from **3 and 6 centimetres**, elbows extended at impact. That is a scale below anything you will meet on the ice. It is a reason to find the advice plausible, not a source for it.)* The reading that reconciles it with the manual is USA Hockey's own phrase: arms out as a **progressive shock absorber that bends and gives**, spreading load across two gloves and two forearms, rather than one locked strut taking your whole weight through one wrist.

Note the corresponding rule on the other side: you also cannot solve the problem by ducking. **How far that rule reaches depends on your book, and the difference is worth knowing.** IIHF Rule 44.1 (Clipping) is conditional — a player "may not crouch down near the boards to avoid being bodychecked **and as a result, causes an opponent to tumble over them**" — so under the IIHF book it is the location and the consequence that make it an offence. **Hockey Canada's Rule 8.7 (Clipping) states it without either condition:** "Players may not crouch down to avoid being bodychecked." Full stop, anywhere on the ice, whether or not anyone falls over you. Do not carry the IIHF's conditions into a Hockey Canada game. ⚠️ **And under Hockey Canada, ducking can cost you the protection as well as earning you the penalty.** Interpretation 3 to Rule 7.7(a) provides that *"In Junior and Senior divisions, if a player ducks to avoid a check and, as a result, is hit in the head area with an otherwise legal check, this will not be considered Head Contact and no penalty will be assessed"* — the checker walks away from it. **Read the limit the interpretation writes on itself:** it *"does not apply to a player who is in a vulnerable position as the result of a legitimate hockey play (e.g. shooting, making or receiving a pass, etc.)"*, and it is confined to junior and senior — below that Rule 7.6 governs and penalises head contact *"regardless of the circumstances"*, so the checker is penalised there whether you ducked or not. Do not duck in any book, because the reason not to is the one above, not the penalty.

**Being hit from behind is a different problem, and what is taught for it is thinner than for the other cases — but it is not nothing.** USA Hockey's *Heads Up Hockey* guide does give a fallback for contact you cannot prepare for: *"if they get in a situation where they can't skate through a check, players should keep their arms up, knees bent and, of course, their heads up"*, with the same checklist as the boards cases — skates parallel, *"arms/legs as shock absorbers"*, *"feet apart, knees bent, low center of gravity"*. No published treatment gives a technique for a hit with **genuinely zero warning**; every one of them assumes you have at least registered that contact is coming. Beyond that it is handled on the other side of the collision — checking from behind is a major penalty with an automatic game misconduct, set out in Section 5 above — and on your side by awareness: USA Hockey's only instruction is *"check over your shoulder to see where your opponent is."* Knowing pressure is coming is what converts the situation into Case 1 or Case 2. See [Scanning and Anticipation](../hockey-iq/scanning_and_anticipation.md).

### Bracing and absorbing

```facts
Position: Wide base, knees bent, weight low — an athletic stance rides contact, a stiff upright one gets knocked over
Action: Meet it, don't wait for it — momentum you generate is momentum that does not go into you
Never: Skate into an oncoming opponent to meet a hit — IIHF 101.1 makes any move to step or glide into an opponent an illegal hit, and the same rule lets you hold ground you have established rather than move
Position: Chest and shoulder first, hands and stick in front of your body, not out to the side
Technique: Absorb with your legs — let your knees and hips give rather than taking it all through a locked frame
```

- **Wide base, knees bent, weight low.** A stiff, upright player gets knocked over. A player in an athletic stance rides the contact.
- **Meet it, don't wait for it.** Skating into contact with your body prepared and your feet moving costs far less than standing still and being hit. Momentum you generate is momentum that does not go into you. ⚠️ **Meet it — do not go and get it.** Nothing here asks you to skate *at* an opponent, and one book penalises exactly that: IIHF Rule 101.1 makes *"any move by a Player to step or glide into an opposing Player"* at least a minor for an illegal hit. The same rule also tells you that you do not need to — *"Players are allowed to 'hold their ground' any time that they have established their position on the ice. No Player is required to move out of the way of an oncoming Player to avoid a collision."* Being braced and moving on your own line is the whole of the point.
- **Chest and shoulder first, hands in front.** Keep your hands and stick in front of your body, not out to the side. This protects you and keeps you able to play the puck the instant contact ends.
- **Absorb with your legs.** Let your knees and hips give a little rather than taking it all through a locked frame.

### Protecting yourself along the wall

```facts
Priority: Arrive first if you possibly can — the player who arrives first controls the geometry of the collision
Position: Get low and get wide — a low centre of gravity along the boards is very hard to move
Technique: Skates parallel to the wall, forearm and hip to it — not the point of your shoulder, and never your back. Head up, chin off your chest
Never: Stop and admire the puck — standing still, bent over, back to the play is the posture the checking-from-behind rules describe
```

When you go to the wall for a puck with a checker arriving:

- **Arrive first if you possibly can** — the player who arrives first controls the geometry of the collision.
- **Get low and get wide.** A low centre of gravity along the boards is very hard to move.
- **Get your skates parallel to the wall and put your forearm and hip to it — not the point of your shoulder, and never your back.** Head up, chin off your chest. (Case 2 above.)
- **Do not stop and admire the puck.** Standing still, bent over, back to the play is the exact posture the checking-from-behind rules describe.

### Get off the wall

```facts
Goal: The best protection is not being pinned. Avoid the collision entirely and keep possession
Options: Chip the puck along the boards, kick it to a teammate, or take it off the wall toward open ice before contact arrives
Action: Know what is around you before the puck arrives
Action: Have a plan for the puck before you get there — a chip, a rim, a reverse, or a spin off the wall
Key: Players who arrive at the wall with no plan get pinned; players who arrive with a plan get out
```

The best protection is not being pinned. If you can chip the puck along the boards, kick it to a teammate, or take it off the wall toward open ice before contact arrives, you avoid the collision entirely and keep possession.

Two habits make this possible: **know what is around you before the puck arrives** (see [Scanning and Anticipation](../hockey-iq/scanning_and_anticipation.md)), and **have a plan for the puck before you get there** — a chip, a rim, a reverse, or a spin off the wall. Players who arrive at the wall with no plan get pinned. Players who arrive with a plan get out.

### Talk

```facts
Action: Call loud, immediate and specific for a teammate going back with their head down and pressure coming
Convention: "Time!" means they have space; "Man on!" or their name plus "behind you!" means they do not
Key: The warning is what lets a teammate prepare for contact they would otherwise take blind
```

If a teammate is going back for a puck with their head down and pressure coming, the correct call is loud, immediate and specific. "Time!" means you have space. "Man on!" or their name plus "behind you!" means you do not. A player cannot brace for a hit they cannot see, and the call is what gives them the chance to. See [On-Ice Communication](../foundation/on_ice_communication.md).

### If it happens anyway — a suspected neck injury on the ice

```facts
Never: Help the player up — it is the most common and most damaging mistake after a spinal impact
Action: Call for immediate medical help first
Read: Symptoms after a spinal impact — pain at the injury, buzzing or tingling in arms or legs, loss of movement, radiating pain
Action: Ensure adequate airway and breathing, observing the ABCs — Airway, Breathing, Circulation
Action: Reassure the player, keep them warm, and instruct and immobilise them so they do not move — head and neck especially
Never: Take the helmet off — CRT6 writes that one flat, and unlike its instruction about moving the player it carries no exception
Never: Roll, lift or otherwise move the player unless you are trained to and it is required for airway support
Key: Do not wait for symptoms — CRT6 says assume a possible spinal cord injury in all cases of head injury
```

Everything above is prevention. This is what to do when prevention has failed, and it is short enough to memorise. **The most common and most damaging mistake is helping the player up.**

**Symptoms to look for after a spinal impact**, per USA Hockey: pain in the area of the injury; buzzing or tingling in the arms and/or legs; loss of movement in the arms or legs; radiating pain in the arms or legs.

**If you think a player may have sustained a spinal injury, your immediate role is to get help and keep the player calm and immobile.** After calling for immediate medical help, USA Hockey's instructions are:

- **Ensure adequate airway and breathing**, observing the ABCs — Airway, Breathing, Circulation.
- **Reassure the player.**
- **Do not allow the player to move. Instruct the player not to move and immobilize him or her to prevent motion, especially motion of the head and neck.**
- **Keep the player warm.**

⚠️ **That list is USA Hockey's and it ends there — it says nothing about the helmet, and the helmet instruction is not theirs.** It is **CRT6's**, from the Concussion Recognition Tool's "Remember" box, and the two instructions in that box are not written the same way. Moving the player is qualified twice: *"Do not attempt to move the athlete (other than required for airway support) unless trained to do so."* The helmet is written flat, with no exception attached to it at all: *"Do not remove helmet (if present) or other equipment."*
