Under the IIHF book **56.5 reaches a major plus a game misconduct with nobody hurt for a *late hit*, because 56.1 defines one as reckless endangerment; for a *pick* it needs reckless endangerment shown.** **USA Hockey is the exception and it matters:** Rule 625 caps interference at a minor and is not on Rule 411's list — but 602(a)'s match penalty for a player who *"recklessly endangers or attempts to injure any opposing player"* is tied to no named foul at all and sits outside 625, and a *late hit* there is not interference at all: it is 640(b), which 640(g) and 640(h) take to a major plus a game misconduct and a match penalty, both only through (b), the sub-section that excepts adult men. |
| **Cross-checking** | The shaft between two hands used forcefully to check an opponent (NHL Rule 59.1). No blade-on-ice element — that wording is in USA Hockey's playing rule (609 Note: "with no portion of the stick on the ice"), but absent from NHL 59.1 and IIHF 59.1. **And a minor is not the ceiling in any of the four books** — every one writes a major above it that needs no injury, and three write a match penalty above that. Full ladder in [The cross-check problem](#the-cross-check-problem). |
| **Roughing** | A punching or slamming motion, normally at the head or face; also, in USA Hockey, using hands, stick or extended arms to body check, and late checks on a player no longer controlling the puck. |
| **High-sticking** | A stick that **contacts an opponent above the shoulders, provided their shoulders are at waist level or higher** (NHL Rule 60.1, rewritten for 2025-26 — the NHL's own 2024-2025 book still read *"A 'high stick' is one which is carried above the height of the opponent's shoulders"*, so the test is now *contact*, not how you carry the stick; the **IIHF Official Rulebook 2026/27** carries the new sentence word for word at its own 60.1, and the **IIHF Official Rule Book 2025/26 (Version 1.1)** does not: its 60.1 still runs that superseded definition, word for word as the NHL had it — a carrying test, with no waist floor at all). **Do not plan a stick on a crouching opponent on the strength of that floor:** NHL 60.2, the clause that actually imposes the minor, reads *"Any contact made by a stick on an opponent above the shoulders is prohibited"* with no waist proviso at all. Accidental contact on a normal windup or follow-through is permitted **under the NHL and IIHF books only** — USA Hockey writes no such carve-out in its playing rules **or in its separate Casebook**, and Hockey Canada's Interpretation 1 to Rule 9.5(a) assesses a minor for a follow-through that hits an opponent above the shoulder. ⚠️ **And under USA Hockey the playing rule and the Casebook do not describe the same offence.** Its playing rule defines high sticking as carrying the stick above the opponent's shoulders *"and makes contact with the opponent"* (621 Note); its Casebook then answers "no" to whether contact must occur, says *"[i]t is not a requirement that a stick make contact above shoulder height for high sticking to be called"*, and requires the call wherever a player raises the stick above the shoulders *"in an attempt to intimidate an opponent or is careless in their actions"* (Rule 621, Situation 2). **Both of the Note's limbs fall away there — the contact, and the height at which it lands.** **Carelessness alone is enough — but one of those two states has to be there**, so this is not a bar on carrying the stick high. **Play the wider reading**, which is the only one safe under both. A wild swing at a bouncing puck is never permitted. |
| **Tripping** | Using a stick, knee, foot, arm, hand or elbow to cause an opponent to fall or lose their footing. **No fall is required under USA Hockey or Hockey Canada** — USA Hockey 639 Note 1 reads *"lose balance **or** fall"*, and its Casebook answers *"No"* to whether the fouled player must fall to the ice (Rule 639, Situation 1); Hockey Canada 8.6(a) penalises any player who *"trips an opposing player"*. **The NHL and IIHF books are unresolved**: 57.1 in each defines tripping as causing an opponent *"to trip **or** fall"*, while 57.2, the clause that actually imposes the minor, writes *"trip **and** fall"*. Play it as though the stumble is enough. ⚠️ **Playing the puck first does not save you the minor in the NHL or the IIHF** — it saves the penalty shot only (NHL 57.3, IIHF 24.8). USA Hockey and Hockey Canada write relief on different terms, and **neither reaches slew-footing** — USA Hockey's, the Note to 639(a), relieves only a player *"clearly hook-checking or poke-checking the puck"*, which is a **stick** check and not a leg through the skates; Hockey Canada's 8.6 says so in terms, *"This rule does not apply to actions defined under Rule 8.7 – Clipping or Rule 8.8 – Slew-Footing."* **This is the most-called penalty in the NHL** — 16.6% of all calls in the 400-game sample used in §9. |
| **Slew-footing** | A leg or foot used to take an opponent's feet from under them — classically while an arm or elbow pushes their upper body backward at the same time (NHL and IIHF 52.1; USA Hockey Rule 639 Note 4; Hockey Canada 8.8). ⚠️ **It is not a two-minute foul in any book, and it ejects you at the floor in three of the four.** **NHL 52.2 is a mandatory match penalty** — *"Any player who is guilty of slew-footing shall be assessed a match penalty"* — with no minor or major tier beneath it. **IIHF 52.2**, the British book, says the referee *"shall, at their discretion, assess"* a major plus an **automatic game misconduct**, and Rule 52 writes no lesser tier beneath it. **USA Hockey 639(a) excludes slew footing from the minor outright** and 639(b) sets the floor: *"The minimum penalty to be assessed for slew footing is a major plus game misconduct penalty"*, its Casebook adding at Rule 639, Situation 5 that *"[a] minor penalty is NOT an option for slew footing."* ⚠️ **Hockey Canada starts lowest, and that is a floor, not a ceiling** — 8.8(a) is a double minor and 8.8(b) bars a major, but **8.8(c) escalates past both to a match penalty**, at the referee's discretion on *"the degree of violence of impact (including the impact with the ice, goal, or boards)"* and **mandatory where the foul injures**. Full ladder [below](#slew-footing--the-foul-that-grows-out-of-a-legal-battle-position). |
| **Charging** | Distance travelled and speed built up into a check, or jumping into an opponent. Written thresholds differ: **IIHF Rule 42.1 "multiple strides"** (the British test), USA Hockey *more than* two strides, Hockey Canada *two or more*, NHL no stated number (judged on "distance traveled"). |

⚠️ **The waist floor runs through 60.1 and 60.3, and which book has it depends on which IIHF edition you are under.** The NHL book has it at both. So does the **IIHF Official Rulebook 2026/27**: its 60.1 reads *"A 'high stick' is one which contacts an opponent above the shoulders, provided their shoulders are at waist level or higher"* — the NHL's sentence word for word — and its 60.3 carries the parenthesis *"(above the height of the opponent's waist)"*. The **IIHF Official Rule Book 2025/26 (Version 1.1)** has it at neither: there 60.1 tests a stick *carried* above the opponent's shoulders, 60.3 a stick *carried or held* above the shoulders, and no waist wording appears anywhere in Rule 60. 🇬🇧 **Which of those is yours in Britain is worth asking**, because the IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27 name *"the latest IIHF Official Rule Book (Version 1.1, published July 2025)"* as their foundation — and Version 1.1 of July 2025 is the 2025/26 book, not the 2026/27 one. **USA Hockey 621 and Hockey Canada 9.5 write no waist floor in either case.**

**And the waist that counts is the waist of the player you hit, not yours.** The NHL's **60.3** and the IIHF's 2026/27 **60.3** say so in terms — *"above the height of the opponent's waist"* — and the **IIHF Situation Handbook 2026/27** puts the case at **Situation 60.13**: a player bent over digging the puck out of the corner, and an opponent's stick-lift attempt that misses and catches them in the face while the striker's stick is *"only slightly above the height of their waist."* The answer is *"Yes, this is a minor (or double-minor) penalty for high-sticking. The key here is that the opposing player was hit in the neck/face/head with the opponent's stick, at or above the height of the waist of the player being struck."* That Situation is in the **2026/27** Handbook and not in the 2025/26 one, and the Handbook ranks itself below the book — its Foreword reads *"in case of any contradictions, the valid IIHF Rulebook is always the basis."* Here it does not need to override anything: under the 2025/26 IIHF book a bent-over opponent is caught by Rule 60 more simply still, because Rule 60 there sets no floor at all — though that edition carries 60.1's carve-out for the centre bent over at a face-off in the same words as the 2026/27 one, which is set out below. **So a stick below your own waist is a defence in no book.** The practical consequence is for the stick lift in [§3](#the-stick-lift): against an opponent bent low over the puck, the shaft you are lifting under is nearer their face than it looks, and a lift that misses rides up into it. Take it under the shaft and low, and accept that you will not get every one of them.

**60.2 is word-for-word identical in the NHL book and in both IIHF editions** — *"Any contact made by a stick on an opponent above the shoulders is prohibited and a minor penalty shall be imposed"* — and it is the clause that actually imposes the minor, which is why the floor above it is no basis for planning a stick on a crouching opponent. Under USA Hockey and Hockey Canada, and under the 2025/26 IIHF book, no waist floor stands between a crouching opponent and the high-sticking rule at all. ⚠️ **That is not the same as absolute protection, and the exception is printed inside the definition itself.** IIHF 60.1 permits *"accidental contact"* committed as a normal *"windup or follow through"* of a *"shooting motion"*, and permits *"accidental contact on the opposing center who is bent over during the course of a 'face-off'"* — a crouching opponent, named and excused. That sentence is identical in both IIHF editions and word for word the NHL's 60.1, and it is confined to the centre at a faceoff and to accidents; it is not a general licence over a bent-over player. **The other two books do not write it, and one of them runs the other way:** USA Hockey's playing rule at 621 carries no equivalent sentence, and Hockey Canada's Interpretation 1 to Rule 9.5(a) assesses *"a Minor penalty for High-sticking"* where a junior or senior player high-sticks the puck and *"then, on the follow through, hits an opposing player above the shoulder and no injury has resulted"*, adding that it *"would also apply on a follow through of shooting the puck."* ⚠️ **But the absence of a waist floor is not full protection under those two books either.** Neither writes a bare *"above the shoulders"* test: USA Hockey's 621 Note measures against *"the normal height of the opponent's shoulders"*, and Hockey Canada's 9.5 and 9.5(a) against *"the normal height of the shoulder[s]"*. ⚠️ **Whether "normal" means the shoulders where they actually are or those of a player standing upright is a question neither book answers for these rules, so do not read it either way.** Hockey Canada defines the phrase exactly once in the whole book — Rule 6.9(a) Note 1, *"The 'normal' height of the shoulders refers to when a player is standing fully upright"* — but 6.9 is high-sticking **the puck**, and the book nowhere says that definition travels to 9.5 — its own 6.9(c) sends a puck high-stick, or an attempted one, that contacts an opponent to **Rule 7.6 Head Contact** instead, and only in minor and female divisions; USA Hockey defines *"normal"* nowhere at all, in its playing rules or in its Casebook. ⚠️ **And USA Hockey's playing rule and its own Casebook do not describe the same offence.** 621's Note is two-limbed — high sticking is where a player *"carries the stick above the normal height of the opponent's shoulders **and** makes contact with the opponent"* — but the Casebook asks *"Must contact with an opponent occur in order for the Referee to assess a penalty for high sticking?"*, answers *"No."*, and adds that *"[i]t is not a requirement that a stick make contact above shoulder height for high sticking to be called"* and that *"[w]henever a player raises their stick above their shoulders in an attempt to intimidate an opponent or is careless in their actions, high sticking must be called"* (Rule 621, Situation 2). **Both of the Note's limbs fall away there — the contact, and the height at which it lands.** The two texts sit in one printed volume, the *Official Rules and Casebook of Ice Hockey 2025-29*, which carries the playing rule word for word and then reads it wider in its situations; **this document records that rather than picking a side.** ⚠️ **Play the wider reading, because it is the only one that is safe under both:** a stick raised above your shoulders can be a minor with no contact at all, and a stick that catches an opponent *below* their shoulders can still be high-sticking. Carelessness alone satisfies the Casebook's trigger, so no intent to intimidate is needed — but one of those two states does have to be there, so this is not a bar on carrying a stick high in open ice. **A routing point sits in the same Casebook, and it raises the price rather than lowering it:** where the stick reaches the head *"during the process of delivering a check"*, the call is not high-sticking at all but Rule 620 Head Contact, whose floor is a minor **plus a misconduct** (Rule 621, Situation 1). ⚠️ **What reaches a crouching opponent in those two books is head-contact law, and it does not turn on stick height at all.** USA Hockey Rule 620 makes head contact *"the action of a player contacting an opponent in the head, face or neck with any part of the player's body, equipment or stick"*, puts the onus *"on the player delivering the check"*, and starts at a minor **plus a misconduct** (620(a)). Hockey Canada Rule 7.6 opens *"There is no type of legal contact to the head, face, or neck"* and requires that *"[r]egardless of the circumstances, players must be penalized for contact with an opponent's head, face, or neck at all times."* **And do not read Hockey Canada 9.5 as its general high-sticking rule** — it is titled "High-Sticking – Junior/Senior". In Hockey Canada minor and female hockey, contact above the shoulders falls to Rule 7.6 Head Contact, which is harsher than a minor. **And a high stick is not capped at a double minor:** IIHF 60.4 reaches a major plus an automatic game misconduct for reckless endangerment and USA Hockey 621(b) a major plus a game misconduct, USA Hockey 621(c) and NHL 60.4 reach a match penalty, and Hockey Canada reaches both tiers by a different route. ⚠️ **And a fifth book on disk never reaches the double minor, because it starts above it.** **CARHA Rule 62(b)** — **CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only** — makes contact with an opponent above the normal height of their shoulders a **major** from the first instance, accidental or intentional, *"whether or not injury results"*, with no minor rung beneath it and no route back down: **CARHA Rule 50** covers head contact *"with any part of the player's body or equipment other than a stick"*, and its opening Note sends stick contact to Rule 62. Its Note adds a game misconduct where the contact was intentional, or accidental and injuring, and 62(c) is a match penalty. ⚠️ **Back under Hockey Canada now — CARHA is an adult book and has no junior or senior divisions at all.** In Hockey Canada junior and senior, 9.5(b) and 9.5(c) bar the major and match labels under high-sticking outright — *"No Major penalty may be assessed for high-sticking"* — and send them to 7.7(b) and 7.7(c). In Hockey Canada minor and female hockey there is no high-sticking rule at all: 7.6 Head Contact *"supersedes any other rules"* bar fighting, so the same contact is 7.6(b), a major plus a game misconduct, or 7.6(c), a match penalty. ⚠️ **And neither of those majors waits for an injury.** **7.6(b)** opens *"At the discretion of the Referee, based on the degree of violence of the impact, the Referee may assess a Major penalty and Game Misconduct penalty for Head Contact"* and only then makes the same pair compulsory where *"a player is injured, as the result of a Head Contact infraction that would otherwise call for a Minor penalty"*; **7.7(b)** assesses it *"for any Head Contact infraction with a high degree of violence"*, and adds a third route the injury reading hides — a player fouled or hit illegally who, as a result, hits their head on the glass, ice or boards. **7.7(b) reaches the boards case only where a head injury results; its high-degree-of-violence limb does not.** Both (c) clauses are match penalties for hitting a player in the head *"in such a way that the player is unable to protect or defend themselves"*, or for a deliberate or attempted injury by head contact.

### Slew-footing — the foul that grows out of a legal battle position

```facts
Rule: Slew-footing is a leg or foot used to take an opponent's feet from under them, classically while an arm or elbow pushes their upper body backward at the same time — NHL and IIHF 52.1, USA Hockey 639 Note 4, Hockey Canada 8.8
Never: Put a leg behind an opponent in a battle. Keep your leg in front of you instead, and win the puck with your hips and your skates
Rule: NHL Rule 52.2 is a mandatory match penalty with no minor or major tier beneath it — "Any player who is guilty of slew-footing shall be assessed a match penalty"
Rule: IIHF Rule 52.2 — the British book — reads "The Referee shall, at their discretion, assess a major penalty and an automatic game misconduct penalty"; Rule 52 writes no lesser tier, and the IIHF writes no match penalty anywhere
Rule: USA Hockey 639(a) excludes slew footing from the minor and 639(b) sets the floor at a major plus a game misconduct; its Casebook at Rule 639, Situation 5 says "[a] minor penalty is NOT an option for slew footing"
Rule: USA Hockey's floor is not its ceiling either — 639(c) allows a match penalty for reckless endangerment, and the same Casebook Situation 5 closes that "a match penalty must be considered" any time one of these actions is deemed to recklessly endanger the opponent
Rule: Hockey Canada 8.8(a) is a double minor and 8.8(b) bars a major — but that is a floor, not a ceiling: 8.8(c) reaches a match penalty on the degree of violence, and mandatorily where the foul injures
Rule: Hockey Canada defines it widest, reaching a leg that knocks, drags or sweeps the feet away (8.8), and its Interpretation 1 to 8.8(a) urges "serious consideration for a Match penalty" where the offender is in motion
Rule: "I played the puck first" is a defence to slew-footing in no book — every puck-first relief sits inside a tripping rule: USA Hockey 639(a) is written "except slew footing", Hockey Canada 8.6 excludes 8.8 in terms, and NHL and IIHF Rule 52 write none
Never: Move a player who has gone down backward and is not getting up, and never take their helmet off. That is the spinal-injury section above
```

**This is the one that starts from something legal.** [§4](#4-body-position-as-defence) is about getting your body between an opponent and the puck, and the free arm that fends them off is permitted on conditions. Slew-footing is what that becomes when the arm pushes their upper body backward **and a leg goes through their skates at the same time**. NHL and IIHF Rule 52.1 write it as two forms — the leg alone, *or* the leg with that arm push — and **both forms count**; each definition ends on a player made to *"fall violently to the ice"*. USA Hockey's Rule 639 Note 4 describes the same two limbs, and Hockey Canada's Rule 8.8 goes wider still, reaching a leg or foot used to *"knock, drag, or sweep an opposing player's feet from under them"*.

**Why it is priced where it is.** A player whose feet leave in front of them goes down backward with nothing to break the fall — *that is this guide's reading, not a sentence any rulebook writes.* What the books do write is the *"fall violently to the ice"* above, and four tariffs that agree on one thing: **none of them prices this at a bare minor.**

- **NHL Rule 52.2 — a mandatory match penalty**, no discretion, no lesser tier: *"Any player who is guilty of slew-footing shall be assessed a match penalty."*
- **🇬🇧 IIHF Rule 52.2 — your book in Britain** — *"The Referee **shall**, at their discretion, assess a major penalty and an automatic game misconduct penalty if a Player is guilty of 'slew-footing' on an opposing Player."* Note the frame — *shall assess*, where the IIHF writes *may assess* in its neighbouring rules — and Rule 52 writes no lesser tier beneath it: 52.1 defines the foul and 52.2 is its only penalty clause. That is still an ejection; the IIHF book writes no match penalty for anything.
- **USA Hockey Rule 639** takes it out of the minor in terms — 639(a) covers the tripping family *"except slew footing"* — and 639(b) sets the floor at *"a major plus game misconduct penalty"*, with 639(c) reaching a match penalty for reckless endangerment. Its Casebook is blunter, at Rule 639, Situation 5: *"[a] minor penalty is NOT an option for slew footing."* ⚠️ **And that Situation does not stop there — its last sentence is stricter than the rule it interprets.** Where 639(c) says a match penalty *"may also be assessed"*, the Casebook directs it: *"Any time one of these actions has been deemed to recklessly endanger the opponent, a match penalty must be considered."*
- ⚠️ **Hockey Canada is the one book that starts small, and the double minor is a floor rather than a ceiling.** 8.8(a) assesses *"a double Minor penalty"* and 8.8(b) says *"[n]o Major penalty may be assessed for slew-footing"* — but **8.8(c) goes straight past the major to a match penalty**, at the referee's discretion on *"the degree of violence of impact (including the impact with the ice, goal, or boards)"*, and **mandatory** where the foul injures. Its Interpretation 1 to 8.8(a) adds that *"[s]erious consideration for a Match penalty should be paid when an offending player is 'in motion' (skating) while committing this foul"*, so skating into it is an aggravating factor. **Read the double minor as the bottom of a ladder, not as the price.**

**So: keep your leg in front of you.** Win the battle with your hips, your base and your skates on the puck — [§7](#7-board-battles) is the whole method — and if you have lost position, the only legal way to get it back is with your feet. ⚠️ **And "I played the puck first" is a defence to none of it.** Every puck-first relief the four books write sits inside a *tripping* rule and stops there: USA Hockey's is the Note to **639(a)**, and 639(a) itself is written *"except slew footing"*; **Hockey Canada's Rule 8.6** says so in terms — *"This rule does not apply to actions defined under Rule 8.7 – Clipping or Rule 8.8 – Slew-Footing"* — so its Interpretations 1 and 2 to 8.6(a), which excuse a player who touches the puck first, do not reach 8.8; and **NHL and IIHF Rule 52 write no relief of any kind.** [Risk Management](../hockey-iq/risk_management.md#slew-footing-the-one-the-carve-outs-never-reach) covers this from the other side. ⚠️ **And if someone goes down backward and is not getting up, nobody moves them and nobody takes the helmet off** — that is [the spinal-injury section above](#if-it-happens-anyway--a-suspected-neck-injury-on-the-ice).

### The habits that keep you out of the box

```facts
Action: Blade on the puck, never on the player — USA Hockey penalises a stick in front of the legs to impede them "even if on the ice", with no effort to legally play the puck
Action: Move your feet instead of reaching — a stick that has to reach is a stick that hooks, holds or trips
Priority: Take the puck, not the player — if your first thought is the body, your stick is somewhere it should not be
Never: Stay in a scrum. Play the whistle and then stop instead; retaliating turns their penalty into two
Never: Take a penalty out of frustration at a referee — it is the cheapest goal you will ever give up
Action: Know your league's standard — watch how the first five minutes are being called and adjust
```

- **Blade on the puck, never on the player.** Almost every stick penalty starts with the blade off the ice — but a blade *on* the ice is no safe harbour either: USA Hockey's Standard of Play penalises placing the stick in front of an opponent's legs to impede them "even if on the ice", where there is "no effort to legally play the puck".
- **Move your feet instead of reaching.** A stick that has to reach is a stick that hooks, holds or trips. Coaches name reaching as the biggest driver of penalty minutes in amateur hockey — **coaching emphasis, not a counted ranking**.
- **Take the puck, not the player.** In a battle, aim for the puck. If your first thought is the body, your stick is somewhere it should not be.
- **Play the whistle, then stop.** Nothing good happens in a scrum. Both the instigator and the retaliator can be penalised — and USA Hockey's standard actually directs the *extra* penalty at the instigator, with Rule 615(a) protecting a player who does not retaliate. The common claim that the retaliator is called more often is unsourced; what is not in doubt is that retaliating turns their penalty into two.
- **Never take a penalty out of frustration at a referee.** It is the cheapest goal you will ever give up.
- **Know your league's standard.** Beer leagues, youth associations and international play call the same rulebook very differently. Watch how the first five minutes are being called and adjust.

### Fighting — and why the amateur rules are much harsher than the NHL's

```facts
Rule: Fighting in the NHL is a five-minute major (Rule 46.14); an instigator takes that major plus an instigating minor and a ten-minute misconduct (46.11); an aggressor a major plus a game misconduct (46.2)
Rule: The third man into an altercation faces a game misconduct at the referee's discretion — except where a match penalty is being imposed in the original altercation (NHL Rule 46.16)
Rule: USA Hockey counts an attempted punch, contact or not, as fighting — a major plus a game misconduct (Rule 615)
Rule: A player who does not retaliate after being struck shall not be penalised for fighting under USA Hockey Rule 615(a) — neither current IIHF edition writes that protection, and under both, punching back can reach a major for fighting (2025/26 Rule 46.6; 2026/27 Rules 46.1 and 46.12)
Never: Drop your stick or remove your gloves in an altercation you were not part of — a minor under USA Hockey 615(a), plus a game misconduct if you are judged the instigator of a subsequent altercation
Rule: Under IIHF Rule 46 an instigator takes 2+5+game misconduct and the third player in a game misconduct — the numbers moved between editions (2025/26: 46.3, 46.7; 2026/27: 46.10, 46.14). Which edition binds a British player is unresolved, and 2026/27's narrower third-man exception is the safe reading
Rule: In NIHL (all leagues), SNL and BUIHA Varsity, fighting is a five-minute major — and in NIHL, NIHL1 and NIHL2 a fighting major in the last five minutes of regulation or in overtime also carries an automatic game misconduct (In-House Rules, Section 6)
Rule: In British women's hockey at every age, adult included, any major brings an automatic game misconduct — a fighting major ends your game (In-House Rule 101's ejection bullet applies "in all cases" and states no age scope)
Key: Junior hockey at every age reads the same way, on In-House Rule 201's scoping of the IIHF junior rules — an inference, since 201 does not name 201.1, so treat a fighting major as ending your game
Key: Rec and beer leagues are commonly harsher again — a general observation about how such leagues are run, not a sourced survey; read your own league's sheet, the only version that binds you
Action: If a scrum starts, hands down, gloves on — skate away and let the officials sort it out
```

This document is about legal contact, and fighting is not it. But it belongs here, because the moment a battle turns into a shove-fest the rules that apply are ones most players know only from watching the NHL — and **they are not the rules you play under.**

- **NHL (Rule 46).** Fighting is a **five-minute major** — 46.14 reads simply, *"A major penalty shall be imposed on any player who fights."* A player judged to have started it takes more than an extra minor: an **instigator** *"shall be assessed an instigating minor penalty, a major penalty for fighting **and a ten-minute misconduct**"* (46.11) — and it escalates, because a second instigator penalty in the same game replaces that ten-minute misconduct with a **game misconduct**, and a third in one regular season brings an automatic game misconduct of its own. An **aggressor** — one who *"continues throwing and landing punches"* at an opponent *"no longer in a position to defend himself"* — *"shall be assessed a major penalty for fighting and a game misconduct"* (46.2). And the **third man into an altercation** faces a game misconduct *"at the discretion of the Referee"* (46.16), which also catches *"subsequent players who elect to intervene"*. ⚠️ **That last one carries an exception, and it is written into the same sentence:** the game misconduct applies *"except when a match penalty is being imposed in the original altercation."* Do not plan around it — you cannot know from outside a fight whether a match penalty is coming — but do not read the rule as a flat ban on ever stepping in either. It is still the trap: stepping in to help a teammate can end your night.
- **USA Hockey (Rule 615) is far stricter, and the threshold is much lower.** Its Note defines fighting as *"a punch, **or an attempted punch**, thrown by any player in the direction of an opponent, **regardless as to whether contact is made**."* You do not have to connect. And 615(a) assesses *"a major plus a game misconduct penalty… to any player who engages in fighting"* — so under USA Hockey a thrown punch is not five minutes, it is **an ejection**, plus a further minor if you instigated it, plus the progressive suspensions that follow a game misconduct. There is also a minor for any player who *"drops their stick and/or removes their glove(s) during an altercation and is not a participant in the original altercation"* — i.e. for the third man arriving hot. ⚠️ **That minor is not the ceiling either, and the next sentence of the same rule says so:** *"A game misconduct penalty shall be added if, in the judgment of the Referee, such player is deemed to be the instigator of a subsequent altercation."* So arriving hot to somebody else's scrum can end your game without your ever having been in the first one.
- **615(a) also protects you for doing nothing:** *"a player who does not retaliate after being struck shall not be assessed a penalty for fighting under this rule."* That clause is worth knowing by heart, because it means the disciplined response is also the one the rulebook rewards.
- **IIHF (Rule 46) — the British base, and the one fighting rule here that has just been rewritten.** The book opens by saying that *"'Fighting' is not part of international ice hockey's DNA."* **The tiers are the same in the 2025/26 and 2026/27 editions; the numbers are not**, so quote a number with its edition or not at all. An **instigator** takes a minor plus a major for fighting plus an automatic game misconduct — 2'+5'+GMP — at **46.3** in 2025/26 and **46.10** in 2026/27; an **aggressor** the same, at **46.4** and **46.2**; the **third player in** a game misconduct at the referee's discretion, catching *"subsequent Players who elect to intervene"*, at **46.7** and **46.14**.
  - ⚠️ **The third-man exception narrowed, and that one is about you rather than about the fighters.** The 2025/26 book waives the game misconduct outright *"except when a game misconduct penalty is being imposed in the original altercation"* (46.7). The 2026/27 book waives it only *"if an aggressor penalty is being imposed in the original altercation"* **and** the referee judges the third man in *"is doing so with the purpose of intervening to stop the ongoing altercation with an 'unwilling combatant' and not to start a secondary altercation"* (46.14). **Under the current book, skating in to break it up is more exposed, not less.**
  - **Neither edition writes USA Hockey's flat protection for the player who does not retaliate.** What the 2025/26 book writes is the opposite case, at **46.6** — *"Defender – Unwilling Combatant"* — covering the player who *does* punch back: *"A Player who retaliates to being punched will be assessed at least a minor penalty ('Roughing') or a major penalty ('Fighting', without a game misconduct penalty), **if the criteria for 'fighting back' with a few punches is not fulfilled**."* Read that against the same rule's opening paragraph, which does shelter a player who *"'defends' themself with a 'few punches'"* as an unwilling combatant — so the minor waits for a retaliation that goes past a few punches rather than for the first one.
  - **The 2026/27 book deletes 46.6 entirely** — 46.6 there is *Helmets* — and keeps only a note, repeated under 46.2, 46.8, 46.10 and 46.12, that a player who *"'defends' themselves against an aggressor or an instigator may be considered as an 'unwilling combatant' and the automatic game misconduct penalty may be waived at the discretion of the referee."* **Note what that waives and what it does not: the game misconduct, not the fighting major.** The sentence about retaliating to a punch is not in that book at all — so what a retaliation costs there falls back on **46.1**, which deems a fight to have occurred once a player *"punches or attempts to punch an opponent repeatedly"*, and **46.12**, a major plus an automatic game misconduct for any player who fights. **Neither edition makes punching back safe. The 2025/26 book prices it at a minor or a major; the 2026/27 book leaves the major and only lets a referee lift the ejection.**
  - ⚠️ **Which edition binds a British player is not answerable from the British document.** The IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27 adopt *"the latest IIHF Official Rule Book (Version 1.1, published July 2025)"* — that is the **2025/26** book — while their own cover says *"the Current IIHF Rule Book"*. Until that is resolved, take the harsher reading of each point: do not count on a non-retaliation protection, and do not count on the third-man exception.
- **The IHUK In-House Rules amend it.** In **NIHL (all leagues), SNL and BUIHA Varsity**, *"fighting shall be penalised with a 5-minute Major Penalty."* And in **NIHL, NIHL1 and NIHL2** there is an additional provision: *"Any fighting major assessed in the last 5 minutes of regulation time (including overtime) will also carry an automatic Game Misconduct."* A further British consequence follows from elsewhere in the In-House Rules, and it is not limited to the age groups: in **women's hockey at every age, adult included**, any major penalty already brings an automatic game misconduct and ejection — Rule 101 carries that as a bullet of its own, applying *"in all cases"* with no age scope stated — so there, a fighting major *is* an ejection. **Read junior hockey the same way at every age group**, on In-House Rule 201's scoping of the IIHF junior playing rules to *"all junior age groups"* — though note that Rule 201 does not name 201.1, so that reading is an inference rather than the rule's own words. See [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md).
- **Rec and beer leagues are commonly harsher again** — many eject and suspend for any fight regardless of who started it. *(That is a general observation about how such leagues are run rather than a sourced survey; there is no league-wide census of rec-league fighting policy. Read your own league's sheet, which is the only version that binds you.)*

**The practical version:** in every book that governs amateur hockey, throwing a punch — or even winding up to — ends your game. If a scrum starts, your hands stay down and your gloves stay on. Skate away and let the officials sort it out; that is not passivity, it is the only outcome that leaves you on the ice.

A team that plays hard, wins battles and takes no penalties is far more physically dominant than a team that hits everything and spends nine minutes a game shorthanded. See [Risk Management](../hockey-iq/risk_management.md).

### Leaving the bench or the box during an altercation

```facts
Rule: USA Hockey 629(a) is a major plus a game misconduct to any player who leaves either bench "during an altercation", "in addition to any other penalties" — and a USA Hockey major is served with "no on-ice substitute" permitted (403(a)), so the team kills it short-handed
Rule: USA Hockey's Casebook puts that on every player who leaves, "not just the first player to do so" (Rule 629, Situation 2) — so "only the first man off the bench gets it" is not USA Hockey's rule
Rule: In USA Hockey Casebook 629, Situation 1 a player whose penalty had expired left the penalty bench during an altercation, stayed out of it and skated to his own bench — "Each player would be assessed a major plus game misconduct penalty"
Rule: The IIHF rules that pattern the same way — a player with 0:45 left who leaves the box during a fight and "just skates around the altercation" takes "an additional minor penalty plus a game misconduct" (Situation Handbook 70.1, on Rule 70.4)
Rule: The prohibition is in every book, in wording close but not identical — NHL 70.1 bars leaving "the players' or penalty bench at any time during an altercation or for the purpose of starting an altercation"; IIHF 70.1 names the two places separately, "the Players' bench or penalty box"
Rule: "The first or second player to leave" is the NHL's and the IIHF's PLAYERS'-bench tier (NHL 70.3 and 70.6; IIHF 70.3 and 70.6) — out of the penalty box both books catch every player, and USA Hockey 629(a) catches every player off either bench
Rule: Hockey Canada 10.4(e) opens "A Game Misconduct penalty will be assessed to any player who leaves the players' or Penalty Benches while a fight is occurring on the ice"; its tiers — double minor plus game misconduct for the first off, game misconduct for a later player penalised — follow it
Rule: A penalised player who leaves the box during a stoppage and during an altercation takes a minor plus a game misconduct under the NHL (70.4, 70.6) and the IIHF (70.4), and 629(a)'s major plus game misconduct under USA Hockey
Rule: USA Hockey 629(a) treats the altercation as concluded once the referee enters the referee's crease or, with no penalties, signals a face-off location — until then, leaving the bench is the offence
Never: Leave the bench or the penalty box while an altercation is going on — not to help a teammate, not to change, and not even to skate to your own bench once your penalty has expired
```

**The instruction above is *hands down, gloves on, skate away*. There is one place on the ice where skating away is itself the major, and it is worth knowing exactly where.** If an altercation starts while you are on the players' bench or in the penalty box, this rule is not about what you do once you are on the ice. It is about the fact that you left the bench at all. **It is scoped to no division and no age** — unlike body checking, it binds you whatever category you play.

- **USA Hockey is the strictest of the four on the night, and it is the book most North American amateurs are under.** Rule 629(a): *"A major plus a game misconduct penalty shall be assessed to any player who leaves the players' bench or the penalty bench during an altercation or for the purpose of starting an altercation. These penalties are in addition to any other penalties that may be assessed during the incident."* ⚠️ **Price that properly, because "a major plus a game misconduct" is two separate bills and only one of them is yours.** A USA Hockey major is served on the ice — **Rule 403(a)**: the player *"shall be ruled off the ice for five minutes, or the designated major penalty time, during which time **no on-ice substitute shall be permitted**"* — so your team kills it a skater short while you are already in the dressing room, and 629(a)'s *"in addition to any other penalties"* means anything you did once you got there is added on top. *(Rule 403's own Note lets an affiliate shorten a major to three or four minutes in shorter periods, except for Adults; it does not let anyone shorten the ejection.)* ⚠️ **Two things players commonly believe about that rule are wrong, and USA Hockey's Casebook says so in terms.** The first is *"only the first man off gets it"* — **Rule 629, Situation 2** answers that the major plus game misconduct *"is assessed to any player leaving the bench during an altercation, not just the first player to do so."* The second is *"my penalty was up, so I was entitled to leave."*
- ⚠️ **That second one is the sharper of the two, because the player it penalises is the one who skated away.** **Casebook Rule 629, Situation 1** puts exactly that case: two players' coincident penalties expire, but they must wait for a stoppage — and the stoppage that comes is *"because of an altercation."* Both leave the box. One joins in; the other *"does not enter the altercation, but skates to their players' bench."* The ruling covers them both — *"Each player would be assessed a major plus game misconduct penalty"* — and the reason is stated flat: *"No player may leave the players' or penalty bench at any time during an altercation."* **The stoppage that released you was the fight, and that is the trap.** What ends it is written into 629(a) itself: the altercation is concluded *"when the referee enters the referee's crease or, in the absence of penalties, signals a face-off location."* Wait for that, and let the officials wave you out.
- **The IIHF answers that same fact pattern the same way, which is the version a British player needs.** Its Situation Handbook puts a player with *"0:45 to serve on the hooking minor"* leaving the penalty box during a fight who *"does not start another fight"* and *"just skates around the altercation"*. The ruling is *"an additional minor penalty plus a game misconduct"*, with the coach required to put a replacement in the box to serve both the remaining 0:45 and the new minor, leaving the team short-handed for 2:45 (Situation 70.1, on Rule 70.4). ⚠️ **Quoted as printed — and the printing is inconsistent.** The question names A5 as the player leaving the box; the answer penalises B5 and short-hands Team B. That mismatch is in the 2025/26 and 2026/27 handbooks alike. The ruling is unambiguous; only the team letters are not.
- **The prohibition is in all four books. What it costs, and who it catches, is not the same in any two of them.** The **NHL** and the **IIHF** bar the same act, in wording that is close but not identical. NHL 70.1: *"No player may leave the players' or penalty bench at any time during an altercation or for the purpose of starting an altercation."* The IIHF's 70.1 names the two places separately: *"No Player may leave the Players' bench or penalty box at any time during an altercation or for the purpose of starting an altercation."* In both books the automatic game misconduct that follows is written for the first or second player off — the NHL for *"[t]he player who was the first or second player to leave the players' (or penalty bench) during an altercation"* (70.3, restated at 70.6), the IIHF for *"the first or second Player to leave the Players' bench (or penalty box)"* (70.3 and 70.6). ⚠️ **So even in the two books the folklore comes from, it is the first *or second*, not the first** — and the NHL adds automatic suspensions of ten games for the first player off and five for the second (70.10). ⚠️ **That is not the whole of 70.10, and the part usually left off is the part that reaches a third player.** The same rule carries a separate clause for the penalty box alone: *"Any player who leaves the penalty bench illegally during an altercation and **is not the first player to do so**, shall be suspended automatically without pay for the next five (5) regular League and/or Play-off games."* **Not the second — any of them.** The IIHF writes no suspension tariff of its own here at all: its 70.10 is *"Disciplinary Measures"*, which refers the matter to the Proper Authorities under Rule 28, so a British player's exposure is discretionary rather than counted in games.
- ⚠️ **The IIHF-edition question does arise here — but not as a renumbering.** Rule 70 keeps all ten of its sub-rules, in the same order and under the same headings, in the 2025/26 (Version 1.1) and 2026/27 books, and the penalties themselves are identical. So unlike Rule 46 above, a rule number quoted here is safe in either edition. **What the newer book changes is what happens after the final buzzer.** Its 70.3, 70.4 and 70.6 each carry the same added sentence, which appears nowhere in the 2025/26 book: *"When a player has been penalized under this rule, the player's coach shall also be subject to potential supplementary discipline under Rule 28."* That sentence ends 70.3, and ends 70.4 bar a pointer to Rule 28 — but in 70.6 it sits mid-rule, with three further paragraphs after it, including the one that prices a penalised player leaving the box. In the same edition, 70.3's game misconduct now ends *"and shall be subject to potential supplementary discipline"*, 70.6 adds *"Those players shall also be subject to potential supplementary discipline"*, and a new **46.15** lists *"leaving the players' bench or penalty box"* among the acts that can draw it. **The penalty on the night is the same in both books. The exposure afterwards is wider in the current one, and it now reaches your coach.**
- **Hockey Canada bars it in general terms and then tiers the fight case — but read its opening sentence before its sub-clauses.** Rule 10.4 opens by allowing a player off the bench only *"for the purposes of a legal line change or at the end of a period"*, and off the penalty bench only *"upon the expiry of their penalty"*, so leaving is never simply permitted there either. Then **10.4(e)** begins: *"A Game Misconduct penalty will be assessed to any player who leaves the players' or Penalty Benches while a fight is occurring on the ice or for the purposes of starting a fight."* The tiers follow that sentence — a **double minor plus a game misconduct** for the first player off either bench (10.4(e)(i)), and a game misconduct for a later player who leaves during a fight *"and is subsequently assessed a penalty"* (10.4(e)(ii)), with a ceiling of five such game misconducts per team in a single stoppage (10.4(e)(iii)). ⚠️ **Whether that later-player tier narrows the opening sentence or simply prices one case of it is not settled anywhere in the book, so do not play the gap.** What Hockey Canada writes first is that leaving while a fight is on draws a game misconduct. What it will not assess for this offence is a **major**, which 10.4(b) rules out outright.
- **So the four books catch different sets of players, and the NHL and the IIHF stop at two only on the *players'* bench.** ⚠️ **Out of the penalty box those two books catch everybody, and that is the sentence to carry.** Both write the same paragraph twice, one word apart — the NHL's, at 70.4 and again at 70.6, is *"[a]ny penalized player leaving the **penalty bench** during a stoppage of play and during an altercation shall incur a minor penalty plus a game misconduct penalty"*, and the IIHF's, at its own 70.4 and 70.6 and identical in both editions, writes *"**penalty box**"* for the same place — with **no first-or-second limit in either**, and the NHL's 70.10 then adds a suspension in the same shape: *"Any player who leaves the penalty bench illegally during an altercation and **is not the first player to do so**, shall be suspended automatically without pay for the next five (5) regular League and/or Play-off games."* **Not the second — any of them.** So the third player out of the box is not somebody else's problem, and neither is the fourth. USA Hockey reaches every player who leaves during *any* altercation, at the top tier — a major plus a game misconduct — whether or not they do anything once they are out there. Hockey Canada's opening sentence also reaches every player who leaves, but only during a *fight*, and at a game misconduct. The NHL and the IIHF write their automatic game misconduct for the first or second player off. **Under none of the four is stepping onto the ice a neutral act while an altercation is going on.**
- **The penalty box is the limb worth memorising, because it catches ordinary players rather than fighters.** Under the **NHL** and the **IIHF**, *"[a]ny penalized player leaving the penalty bench during a stoppage of play and during an altercation shall incur a minor penalty plus a game misconduct penalty"* (NHL 70.4, restated at 70.6; IIHF 70.4, again writing *"penalty box"*). Under **USA Hockey** the same act is 629(a)'s major plus game misconduct. **Hockey Canada is harsher again for the player who was already serving time:** its Interpretation 6 to Rule 10.4 says that where the first player to leave a bench during a fight comes from the penalty bench, *"they will be assessed three minor penalties, plus a Game Misconduct penalty, plus any other penalties they may be assessed under the rules"* — the double minor and game misconduct for being first off the bench, plus a further minor for leaving before the penalty had expired. ⚠️ **That interpretation is printed under the heading *"Rule 10.4 (d)"*, as is Interpretation 5 before it — and 10.4(d) is the clause saying that no misconduct penalty may be assessed for leaving a bench, which is not what either interpretation is about. Both are quoted here under the number the book prints; what they interpret is 10.4(e).** **And a USA Hockey game misconduct does not end with tonight** — Rule 404(b) suspends you for *"the team's next game"* as well. The ladder is in [And what these fouls cost beyond tonight](#and-what-these-fouls-cost-beyond-tonight).
- **What the rule does not catch.** USA Hockey 629(a) protects a change that was already under way: *"Substitutions made prior to the altercation shall not be penalized under this rule provided the players so substituting do not enter the altercation."* So a line that changed before it started is not caught — as long as they stay out of it. Everything after that point is the offence.
- ⚠️ **And keep this rule well away from the one it can be confused with.** Everything above is about a bench emptying during an altercation, and it is entirely a question of the penalty sheet. **A player lying on the ice after a hit is a different situation with a different instruction, and nothing here bears on it** — there the answer is medical, and it is to leave them where they are, leave the helmet on and get help. See [If it happens anyway](#if-it-happens-anyway--a-suspected-neck-injury-on-the-ice).

### Helmets in an altercation — keep yours on, and never touch theirs

```facts
Rule: USA Hockey 615(c) is a match penalty "(for all age classifications)" for deliberately removing your own helmet/facemask "prior to an altercation", and a second one for removing an opponent's "prior to or during" one
Rule: USA Hockey's Casebook applies that match penalty to your own helmet removed during an altercation too (Rule 615, Situation 14), and adds "Fighting penalties do not have to be assessed when applying Rule 615(c)"
Rule: USA Hockey counts as an altercation "[a]ny physical interaction between two or more opposing players where at least one penalty assessed" (Casebook, Rule 615, Situation 21) — a post-whistle shove drawing one minor is enough
Rule: USA Hockey 615(c) also assesses a game misconduct with an express two-game suspension to a player "whose actions during an altercation causes the removal of an opponent's helmet and facemask"
Rule: USA Hockey 615(c) assesses a further game misconduct where your own helmet comes off because it was not worn as designed — Casebook Situation 19 measures a loose chin strap at more than one finger between strap and jaw
Rule: Hockey Canada makes it a Gross Misconduct — ordered to the dressing room and reported to the league (Rule 10.6(f), with 4.9(a)) — to intentionally remove your own or another player's helmet before or during a fight, or to undo either chinstrap so it comes off
Rule: The NHL and the IIHF are the lenient pair — a minor for unsportsmanlike conduct for removing your own helmet before a fight, and no penalty at all for helmets that come off in the course of it (NHL 46.6; IIHF 46.12 in 2025/26, 46.6 in 2026/27)
Rule: The roughing minor for removing an opponent's helmet is the NHL's and the IIHF's and is written for removal "during play" (NHL 51.1 and 51.2 via Rule 9.6; IIHF 51.1 and 51.2) — it is not the rule for a helmet that comes off in a fight
Never: Take your own helmet off, or reach for an opponent's, in an altercation. Gloves on and helmet on instead — skate away and let the officials sort it out
```

**This is one of the widest four-book divergences anywhere in this document, and under one of the books it does not need a fight to catch you.** Most players know the televised version: helmets come off in NHL fights constantly and nothing happens. That is a real rule, and it is one pair of books' rule only.

- **USA Hockey starts at a match penalty, and says so for every age group.** Rule 615(c) assesses one *"(for all age classifications)"* against *"any player who deliberately removes their helmet/facemask prior to an altercation"*, and a second against *"any player who deliberately removes their opponent's helmet/facemask prior to or during an altercation."* It then adds **a game misconduct carrying an express two-game suspension** for *"any player whose actions during an altercation causes the removal of an opponent's helmet and facemask"*, and a further game misconduct for *"any player who doesn't wear their helmet/facemask as designed and the helmet/facemask is removed during an altercation."* A match penalty is not a five-minute inconvenience — you are gone, and a hearing follows.
- ⚠️ **And USA Hockey's Casebook widens it twice, in the directions that reach ordinary players rather than fighters.** First, the printed clause reaches *your own* helmet only *"prior to"* an altercation; **Rule 615, Situation 14 applies the match penalty *during* one as well** — *"any time a player involved in that altercation deliberately removes their own helmet, a match penalty must be assessed"* — and states plainly that *"[f]ighting penalties do not have to be assessed when applying Rule 615(c)."* Second, the **Note applying to Situations 14-20** says *"an altercation does not need to be considered a fight for Rule 615(c) to be applied. This rule would apply in any altercation where at least one player will receive a penalty"*, and **Situation 21** defines an altercation as *"[a]ny physical interaction between two or more opposing players where at least one penalty assessed."* **So the trigger is one penalty, not one punch.** A post-whistle shove that gets somebody two minutes is an altercation, and pulling your own lid off in that moment is a match penalty on its own.
- **The rule reaches you even when you did nothing deliberate — which is why the chin strap matters.** Casebook **Situation 16** has a player whose chin strap was not properly fastened lose their helmet in the ordinary course of an altercation, with no deliberate act by anyone: *"A game misconduct penalty to Player A."* **Situation 12** answers the same way where it simply cannot be determined how the helmets came off. And **Situation 19** gives the measure: *"If the space between the player's lower jaw and the chin strap allows for more than one finger to be inserted into that space, then the chin strap is not being properly worn."* Do it up.
- **Hockey Canada files the same act under equipment rather than fighting, and still ends your night.** Its roughing and fighting rules, 7.9 and 7.10, say nothing about helmets; **Rule 10.6(f)** does, and the tier is a **Gross Misconduct** — under Rule 4.9(a) an order to the dressing room for the rest of the game and a report to the league, with an automatic suspension on top under 4.9(b) if it happens in the last ten minutes, in overtime or after the game. It reaches a player who *"intentionally removes their helmet before or during a fight OR undoes their chinstrap before or during a fight and that helmet subsequently comes off"*, and the same for *"another player's"*. **10.6(d) reaches the gesture on its own**: a misconduct for a player who *"undoes their chinstrap to incite or challenge an opponent but does not actually participate in a fight."* Where nobody acted deliberately, **Interpretation 15** splits it three ways — nothing for an incidental removal, a double minor under Rule 7.1 for grabbing a helmet without gaining an advantage, and a **match penalty** under 7.1(c)(iv) for grabbing one and using it *"to gain an advantage or to inflict punishment."*
- **The NHL and the IIHF are the pair that price it lowest, and they are the pair on television.** **NHL Rule 46.6** and **IIHF Rule 46.12** — renumbered **46.6** in the 2026/27 IIHF book — both read that no player may remove their own helmet before engaging in a fight, on pain of *"a minor penalty for unsportsmanlike conduct"*, and both then add the sentence that produces the folklore — the NHL's wording, which the IIHF carries in the same words bar its capital *P*: *"Helmets that come off in the course of and resulting from the altercation will not result in a penalty to either player."* **That sentence has no counterpart in USA Hockey's book**, where the same helmet coming off is a game misconduct for somebody. **Hockey Canada does reach the same result, but only for a genuinely incidental removal**: its Interpretation 15 to Rule 10.6(f) opens *"[i]f neither player intentionally removes a helmet or undoes a chinstrap prior to or during a fight but the helmets come off during the fight"*, and answers that where the referee judges the helmets came off incidentally, *"no further penalties should be assessed."* **A chin strap that was simply not fastened properly is the case the two North American books split on** — it is nowhere in that interpretation, and it is a game misconduct under USA Hockey 615(c). Which IIHF edition binds a British player is the same unresolved question as everywhere else in this section, but here it does not matter: the tier is a minor either way, and the 2026/27 text differs only in spelling it *"a two minute minor penalty"*.
- **And the roughing minor you may have heard of is a different rule about a different moment.** NHL 51.1 and 51.2 — and IIHF 51.1 and 51.2, identical in both editions — make it roughing, a minor, to intentionally remove *an opponent's* helmet **during play**, routed through each book's Rule 9.6. That is the open-ice snatch, not the scrum. **Do not carry it into an altercation, and do not carry it into USA Hockey or Hockey Canada at all**, whose roughing rules contain no helmet clause. The four-book comparison is in [Rules Primer](../foundation/rules_primer.md).
- ⚠️ **One thing this section is not about, and the two must not run together.** Everything above concerns a helmet that comes off in a scrum, and what it costs on the penalty sheet. A player lying on the ice after a hit is a different situation entirely, and there the instruction is medical rather than disciplinary: leave the helmet on, do not move them, get help. See [If it happens anyway](#if-it-happens-anyway--a-suspected-neck-injury-on-the-ice).


---

## Common Mistakes

- **Assuming your league's contact rules instead of checking them.** The same age group can be checking in one association and non-checking in the next town. **And getting it wrong is not capped at two minutes:** where checking is barred, USA Hockey 604 runs minor, major plus game misconduct, match — and Rule 604 is on Rule 411's aggressive-infractions list, so a third major in a season costs three further games. Hockey Canada 7.3 is the same shape, with the major mandatory on injury. And a checking team playing a non-checking team means nobody checks. **Under Hockey Canada, clearing U13 does not settle it either** — Rule 7.3 also applies in "any other divisions approved by a Member of Hockey Canada," and the playing rules do not say which those are. **In Britain this is not answerable from a rulebook at all** — neither the IIHF Rule Book nor the In-House Rules sets a body-checking age, and assuming Britain copies USA Hockey's 12U line or Hockey Canada's U13 line is a guess. **One adult book does answer it, and the answer is no:** the CARHA rule book has no checking division, Rule 49(a) makes a body check a minor or a major anywhere on the ice and a major where it injures, Rule 30(a) rules that major off the ice for the rest of the game with Rule 32(d) adding an automatic one-game suspension, and Rule 32(a) ejects you on three penalties of any kind — CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only. Ask your league.
- **Thinking "non-contact" means nobody touches you.** It does not — but the four books do not permit the same amount of it, so learn yours. **USA Hockey** is the widest: angling, physical engagement, standing your ground and *"simply maintaining established body position"* are all named as legal competitive contact, and it says the game *"can be very physical."* **IIHF Rule 101.1 — its women's-hockey rule, not its general one** — has two players in pursuit of the puck *"reasonably allowed to push and lean into each other provided that 'possession of the puck' remains the sole object of the two (2) Players"*; holding ice you have established is protected for everyone by the general Rule 56.1. **Hockey Canada is narrower** — Rule 7.3 defines legal body contact as *"incidental contact of two opposing players in pursuit of the puck on the ice in the same direction"*, its Interpretation 1 permits steering *"without actually touching"*, and **7.3(a)** writes a minor — **but not for anyone, and the scope is inside the same sentence**: *"A Minor penalty will be assessed to any player who, in the opinion of the Referee, intentionally body-checks, bumps, shoves, or pushes any opposing player, **in divisions of U13 and below, female hockey, and any other divisions approved by a Member of Hockey Canada**."* That is the same list of divisions the checking table above gives, so a Hockey Canada player above U13 in a division nobody has restricted is not reading their own rule there. **And a fourth book is narrower still** — CARHA's Rule 49(a) penalises a player who *"intentionally bodies, pushes, shoves, stands in front of an opponent for the purpose of making contact, and/or does not avert body contact with an opponent"*, which is a duty to get out of the way and not merely a ban on initiating one; what keeps a battle legal there is **49(c)**, under which *"[w]here, in the opinion of the Referee, accidental contact has taken place, no penalty shall be assessed"* — CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only. Players who expect a bubble get knocked off the puck all night; players who assume the widest book is theirs sit in the box.
- **Skating with your head down.** You cannot brace for a hit you cannot see, and a body that is surprised does not absorb contact. **And one book protects you less for it:** under **IIHF Rule 48.1** a square check with no upward motion on a puck carrier skating head down **toward the checker** is not penalised as an illegal check to the head. **That direction limb is part of the rule, so a head-down player hit from the side or the blind side is outside the carve-out**, and the checker is still obliged not to target your head. **No such exception was found in USA Hockey's Playing Rules, nor anywhere in Hockey Canada's rules, in any division** — Rule 7.6 and Rule 7.7 both, with their Interpretations. Carrying the puck head down is not the same as **ducking** a check, which has its own rules and its own penalty for the player who ducks.
- **Checking a player who has already passed or shot.** Common, and **not capped at two minutes**. NHL 56.2(iii) writes a minor for a deliberate check on *"an opponent, including the goalkeeper, who is not in possession of the puck"*, and USA Hockey 640(b) writes one too — written *"except Adult Male Classifications"*, where 640 Note 2 reaches it as roughing instead. Neither is the ceiling, and **Hockey Canada 8.3(b) reaches a major plus a game misconduct on *"the degree of violence of the impact"* with nobody hurt** — 8.3 also puts a measurable distance on it, an arm and stick length at the instant the puck is released, and **USA Hockey's Casebook writes the same measure** — though there it is one of four conditions rather than the whole test, so only under Hockey Canada does the gap decide it on its own. The NHL's window is *"rendered immediately following his loss of possession"* (56.1) and nothing wider; the IIHF adds a second condition on top of that — you must also still be inside an *"objectively reasonable window of opportunity"* (56.1) — and calls a late hit *"reckless endangerment"*, which 56.5 takes to a major plus a game misconduct with nobody hurt. **Under USA Hockey the window is the narrowest of the four**: 640(b) makes a minor of every *avoidable* check on a player who has released a shot or pass — the rule says they *"are not eligible to be body checked"*, and its Casebook allows one to be finished only where the checker made a prior effort to gain possession, was an arm's length plus stick length away when the puck was dumped, had the check already in the process of being delivered, and minimises the force — all four (Standard of Play, Situation 10). It rules lateness out in terms: *"It is not acceptable to skate one or two more strides after the puck is gone and then decide to deliver the check."* **And USA Hockey's own tier is written into Rule 640**: 640(g) is a major plus a game misconduct where an action under 640(b, c, d, e or f) *"recklessly endangers an opponent"*, and 640(h) a match penalty. Both reach the late hit only through (b), the sub-section excepting adult men, so whether they reach adult men's hockey the book does not say — but 602(a)'s match penalty for a player who *"recklessly endangers or attempts to injure any opposing player"* is tied to no named foul at all and does not depend on the answer. **And Rule 640 is on Rule 411(a)'s aggressive-infractions list**, so a 640(g) major counts on the season tally too — a third major costs three further games, a fourth five, and a fifth a suspension until a hearing. Check the carrier, or take the ice they need.
- **Finishing a check into a player who is facing the wall, or hitting anyone from behind.** There is **no minor-penalty option** for checking from behind in the NHL or IIHF (NHL Rule 43.2; IIHF Rule 43.2) — it is a major with an automatic game misconduct, anywhere on the ice. **Hockey Canada does have a minor for it, but never on its own:** Hockey Canada Rule 7.5(a) assesses a minor *and* a game misconduct together, so no book here lets you off with two minutes. **USA Hockey's floor is the lowest of the four and still not a bare minor** — 608(a) writes *"A minor plus a misconduct penalty, or a major plus a game misconduct penalty"*, so the lightest outcome there puts you in the box rather than out of the game. ⚠️ **A fifth book has no rung like that at all.** **CARHA Rule 53(a)** — CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only — writes its floor as *"[a] Minor penalty plus a Game Misconduct"*, so under CARHA checking from behind takes you out of the game even at its lightest, and 53(b) is a flat match penalty where the player driven from behind into the boards or goal net could not protect themselves. **The onus is stated in the two minor-hockey books, not in the two the sentence above is about** — USA Hockey Rule 608 (*"the onus is on the player delivering the check to not hit from behind"*) and Hockey Canada Rule 7.5 (*"in all circumstances the responsibility is on the player delivering the check to avoid"* contact with the back); neither NHL Rule 43 nor IIHF Rule 43 states one. And **"he turned at the last second" is a defence only under the NHL and the IIHF, and only for a deliberate turn** — NHL Rule 43.1 waives the penalty for a player who *"intentionally turns his body to create contact with his back"*, and IIHF Rule 43.1 for one who *"intentionally turns their back towards an opponent and puts themself in a vulnerable position"*, adding that other penalties may still be assessed. **USA Hockey and Hockey Canada write no such waiver** — nor does USA Hockey's separate Casebook, whose Rule 608 situations attach no exception for a player who turned — and Hockey Canada's Interpretation 2 to Rule 7.5(a) puts that exact question — what happens when a player about to be checked turns and creates contact with the back — and answers that the penalty goes to the checker, because *"the responsibility is always on the player delivering the check **to avoid making contact with the back**."* **Read the object, because it is what the duty is about** — the book states a specific duty about the back, not strict liability for every check; [Rules Primer](../foundation/rules_primer.md) sets out how far an onus clause does and does not go. Take the puck, take the ice, or angle them off it.
- **Assuming the four worst fouls are scoped to checking leagues like body checking is.** They are not. **Checking from behind, head contact, charging and boarding bind every division, yours included** — Hockey Canada scopes only 7.3 and USA Hockey only 604; boarding (7.2, 603), charging (7.4, 607) and checking from behind (7.5, 608) carry no division clause, and Hockey Canada's head-contact rule binds *"at all times"*.
- **Treating boarding as a foul that stops at two minutes — or as something you can only do with your body.** ⚠️ **Under USA Hockey it is also a stick foul:** Rule 603's Note reaches a player who *"pushes, trips or body checks"* an opponent dangerously into the boards, and its Casebook at Rule 603, Situation 3 says that where a trip sends an opponent into the end boards, *"[e]ven though a tripping penalty may also be appropriate, the boarding call is preferred to draw attention to the more aggressive infraction."* The other three books define boarding by the check alone (NHL 41.1, IIHF 41.1, Hockey Canada 7.2). **The minor is the base tier in three books** — NHL 41.2 leaves it to the referee *"based on the degree of violence of the impact with the boards"* and IIHF 41.2 does the same on the degree of *"force and violence"*, and Hockey Canada 7.2(a) writes one — **but none of the four caps it there.** **USA Hockey 603(a) writes no bare minor at all** — it reads *"A minor plus a misconduct or major plus game misconduct penalty shall be assessed for boarding an opponent"*; **Hockey Canada 7.2(b)** reaches a major plus a game misconduct *"At the discretion of the Referee, based on the degree of violence of the impact"* **with nobody hurt**, and makes that same pair *mandatory* where a board that would otherwise be a minor injures; and under the IIHF book — the British one — **41.4 reaches a major plus a game misconduct with nobody hurt at all**. **Three of the four reach a match penalty** — NHL 41.4, USA Hockey 603(c), Hockey Canada 7.2(c) — and the IIHF is the exception because its book writes none.
- **Treating "the onus is on the checker" as a rule of strict liability — in either direction.** As the checker, it does not mean every collision with a turned player is automatically yours — **in three of the four boarding rules**: NHL Rule 41.1 states the onus and then says the circumstances, including whether the opponent made himself vulnerable immediately before the check and whether the check was unavoidable, "can be considered," and that "this balance must be considered by the Referees when applying this rule." **IIHF Rule 41.1 — the British reader's — and Hockey Canada Rule 7.2 write the same however-clause**, and Hockey Canada's carries no division scope. **USA Hockey Rule 603 is the one that does not: it states the onus and stops.** **Do not carry that qualification across to checking from behind.** The NHL's checking-from-behind rule (43) states no onus at all, and the books that do state one for that offence — USA Hockey 608, and Hockey Canada's, which says "in all circumstances" — attach no such balancing clause. As the player being hit, it does not mean turning your back buys you a power play: NHL and IIHF Rule 43.1 both waive the checking-from-behind penalty when the turn was intentional — outright in the NHL's case, while the IIHF adds that other penalties may still be assessed. Neither point changes what you should do. Give a turned player the ice, and never turn into a hit. But a player who thinks the rule is absolute is surprised by the call either way.
- **Turning your back to the boards when contact is coming.** It feels protective. It gives the checker your unprotected spine, puts your head first into the boards, and makes the contact a hit from behind — which USA Hockey warns could inflict a severe head or neck injury even when the hit is light. **Get skates parallel to the wall and take it on the forearm and hip** — that is the target orientation, and **head up with your chin off your chest** goes with it however you meet the wall. Squaring your chest to the boards is the Case 3 fallback for when you cannot turn, not something to aim for.
- **Arriving at the boards perpendicular, or on the point of your shoulder.** Go in on an angle and get your skates parallel; USA Hockey names the shoulder tip specifically as an injury mechanism. If you are stuck facing the wall, get your gloves and stick up and spread out on the glass rather than taking it on one arm.
- **Angling at the player instead of at the ice.** If you skate at the body, a good carrier changes the puck to their far side and goes around you. Skate at the space.
- **Angling flat across the ice.** Without momentum back toward your own net, one pass leaves you facing the wrong way.
- **Committing your feet on a poke check.** A missed poke with your weight forward means you are out of the play.
- **Never learning the stick lift.** Lower-risk than a poke or a check when the lift is taken up rather than sideways and below the bottom hand, and most amateurs have simply never been shown it.
- **Taking the lift on an opponent bent low over the puck as if it were any other lift.** Their face is down at stick height, and a lift that misses and catches it is high-sticking **however low your own hands were** — the reference is the struck player's waist, not yours (NHL Rule 60.3; IIHF Rule 60.3 in the 2026/27 book; IIHF Situation Handbook 2026/27, Situation 60.13). **And it is not capped at the double minor:** IIHF 60.4 and USA Hockey 621(b) both reach a major plus a game misconduct for recklessly endangering an opponent, with nobody injured. **And a fifth book starts above the double minor rather than below it** — CARHA Rule 62(b), in CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only, is a **major** for contact above an opponent's shoulders whether the stick got there accidentally or not and whether or not anyone is injured, with no minor rung above the shoulders and no windup carve-out anywhere in the book. ⚠️ **And a major there is usually the end of your night:** its Note adds a **game misconduct** where the high stick was intentional, or where an accidental one injures, and only an accidental one that injures nobody leaves you a major alone. Take that one under the shaft, or leave it alone.
- **Cross-checking at the net front to create space.** An enforcement emphasis officials are specifically told to watch for — but not, as the folklore has it, the most-called penalty there. In 400 tracked games it ranks **seventh** in the net-front box at 5.7% of calls, essentially its league-wide rate (see §9). The stick fouls that really concentrate there are hooking and slashing. Tie the stick instead. And don't rely on the "blade on the ice" folklore — that isn't in the NHL, IIHF or Hockey Canada definition. **Nor is a cross-check capped at two minutes in any of the four books, and no major there needs an injury** — NHL 59.3 turns on *"the severity of the contact"* with an automatic game misconduct at 59.5 and a match penalty at 59.4; IIHF 59.3 reaches a major plus an automatic game misconduct for reckless endangerment; USA Hockey 609(b) and 609(c) reach the same two tiers; Hockey Canada 9.2(b) has both limbs: the referee may assess a major plus a game misconduct *"based on the degree of violence of impact"* with nobody hurt, and the same pair *"will be assessed"* where the cross-check injures, with a match penalty at 9.2(c) — and its Interpretation 2 to 9.2(b) sends a cross-check above the shoulders out to Rule 7.6 Head Contact as a major plus game misconduct that *"must be assessed"*.
- **Clearing the net front by moving a body across your own goalmouth.** Whichever way is easier is not the safety question. That path has a steel goal frame at one edge and your own goaltender at the other, and the player travelling it is going sideways or backwards and cannot see either of them — and USA Hockey writes the goal post into the head-first spinal-injury mechanism alongside the boards, at a speed it says can be walking pace. **Take them toward the corner instead.** That direction is coaching caution drawn from the hazard rather than a rule anyone writes; what the rules reach is the worst version of it, and none of them at two minutes — a major plus a game misconduct under **USA Hockey 608(b)** where a check from behind *"causes them to go head first into the boards or goal frame"*, a **match penalty** under **Hockey Canada 7.5(c)** for the same hit *"into the boards or goal frame, in such a way that the player is unable to protect or defend themselves"*, and no minor option for checking from behind at all in either the NHL's book or the IIHF's (**43.2** in both: *"[t]here is no provision for a minor penalty for checking from behind"*). **And the hazard is not scoped to checking leagues even though the walk-out is** — a legal box-out moves a body along the same path.
- **Standing up in a board battle.** The lower player usually wins — not always, and a big enough strength gap still wins, but your base is the part you control and standing tall throws it away.
- **Forgetting your feet exist.** In a real battle your stick is tied up and your skates are free. Kick the puck out — at the puck, never at a player: NHL 49.3 makes kicking or attempting to kick an opponent a match penalty whether or not an injury occurs, USA Hockey 627(b) and Hockey Canada 7.1(c)(iii) reach a match penalty too, and under the IIHF — which writes none — the referee may assess a major, with the game misconduct automatic once they do. **No book here makes it a two-minute mistake.** And if you cannot see the puck, do not swing at where you think it is.
