- **Putting a leg behind an opponent in a battle.** It is the shortest distance there is from a legal box-out to the most heavily punished foul in the book. **No book here makes slew-footing a two-minute mistake:** a mandatory match penalty under NHL 52.2 with no lesser tier, a major plus an automatic game misconduct under IIHF 52.2, and a major plus a game misconduct at the floor under USA Hockey 639(b) — whose Casebook says at Rule 639, Situation 5 that *"[a] minor penalty is NOT an option for slew footing."* **Hockey Canada's double minor (8.8(a)) is a floor, not a ceiling** — 8.8(c) reaches a match penalty on the degree of violence and mandatorily where it injures. Keep the leg in front of you.
- **Pinning the player rather than the puck in a corner or a board battle.** The contact is legal because the *puck* is what you are going for; make the *player* the object of it and it stops being legal in any book. **USA Hockey Rule 604, Note 1** counts *"physically forcing the opponent off the puck and with no effort to legally play the puck"* as an illegal body check — a minor under **604(c)** at 12U and below, in all girls'/women's and all non-check adult classifications, with **604(d)** a major plus a game misconduct for reckless endangerment and **604(e)** a match penalty — and **Hockey Canada 7.3(a)** writes the same minor for a player who *"intentionally body-checks, bumps, shoves, or pushes any opposing player"* in the divisions Rule 7.3 names. **And in women's hockey the pin itself is barred, not merely the push:** IIHF Rule 101.1 does not allow two or more players competing for possession to use the boards *"to make contact with an opponent to eliminate her from the play, push her into the boards, or pin her along the boards"* — a minor, or a major (5') plus an automatic game misconduct, which In-House Rule 101 turns into an ejection *"in all cases"* in Britain. Seal, and take the puck.
- **Second man diving into the pile.** Two teammates tangled with one opponent while a third opponent takes the puck.
- **Blocking a shot on a 2-on-1.** Your job is the pass. Let the goalie have the shooter.
- **Blocking a shot from a screening position.** A deflection off a body the goalie could not see is a goal.
- **Turning sideways or lifting a leg to block.** Exposes the unpadded parts of you and blinds you to the puck.
- **Winding up from across the ice to deliver a check.** Both **USA Hockey** and **Hockey Canada** put a stride count in writing, and **they are not the same count** — USA Hockey (Standard of Play — Body Checking, and Rule 607) penalises taking *"more than two strides"*, while Hockey Canada (Rule 7.4) defines charging as building up speed by taking *"two or more strides"*. So the two strides that are your ceiling under USA Hockey are already the offence under Hockey Canada. **IIHF Rule 42.1 — the British test** — names the same act without a count, penalising a player who *"builds up speed by taking multiple strides immediately prior to making contact."* The NHL's Rule 42.1 sets no number at all and judges charging on "distance traveled" instead. Anything more than a couple of strides of build-up is charging in any book. And for an **open-ice** check USA Hockey's own coaching manual is tighter still — one stride, or you do not have the gap. **And charging is not capped at a minor in any book:** NHL 42.2/42.3/42.4, USA Hockey 607(a) with no bare minor and 607(e) a match, Hockey Canada 7.4(a)/(b)/(c) — the IIHF again stopping at a major plus a game misconduct.
- **Assuming a hit is safe because the player was facing you and had not turned.** Facing you does not make contact with their head legal, and there is no clean version of that hit to find: **IIHF Rule 48.1** — *"There is no clean check to the head or neck… This rule supersedes all similar actions regarding hits to the head and neck."* **USA Hockey Rule 620** goes wider than the NHL's main-point-of-contact test, reaching any contact to the head, face or neck with any part of the body, equipment or stick, and its Standard of Play puts the onus on the checker *"regardless as to size differential, to not make contact in the head/neck area of the opponent."* A shoulder that arrives from the front and finds a head is still the offence. **The books split three ways on the player whose head is low when the check arrives, and one of the three penalises *you*.** **The IIHF writes the narrow version, and its trigger is carrying the puck with your head down.** IIHF Rule 48.1 provides that a player who bodychecks an opponent *"who is skating with the puck with their head down in the direction of the Player and does not use an upward motion or drive their body up into the opponent, shall not be penalized for an 'illegal check to the head'."* Even under the IIHF book the checker is still obliged not to target your head; what that book declines to penalise is only the square hit with no upward motion, on a player who has the puck and is skating **toward** the checker — so a head-down skater hit from the side or the blind side, or hit without the puck, is outside that sentence altogether. The IIHF book attaches no penalty of its own to the player whose head was down. **Hockey Canada goes further in both directions, and on a different trigger — ducking to avoid the check rather than carrying the puck head down.** **The checker's amnesty is junior and senior only; Rule 8.7's crouching prohibition carries no division scope**, so the ducker is exposed in every division. Hockey Canada's Interpretation 3 to Rule 7.7(a) reads: *"In Junior and Senior divisions, if a player ducks to avoid a check and, as a result, is hit in the head area with an otherwise legal check, this will not be considered Head Contact and no penalty will be assessed. The referee may assess a penalty to the player who ducks to avoid a check under Rule 8.7 — Clipping, if appropriate."* Hockey Canada Rule 8.7 states that *"Players may not crouch down to avoid being bodychecked"*, and Hockey Canada Rule 8.7(a) makes that a Minor penalty and 8.7(b) and 8.7(c) reach a major plus a game misconduct and a match penalty — and because Rule 8.7 carries no division scope, ducking out of a check can put **you** in the box in **any** Hockey Canada division. ⚠️ **What changes with the division is the checker's exposure, not the ducker's.** In Hockey Canada junior and senior, Interpretation 3 to Rule 7.7(a) tells the referee that a check which catches a ducking opponent's head *"will not be considered Head Contact and no penalty will be assessed"* — the checker skates away. Below junior and senior that interpretation does not apply and Rule 7.6 governs — *"Regardless of the circumstances, players must be penalized for contact with an opponent's head, face, or neck at all times."* So in Hockey Canada minor and female hockey the ducker is still exposed under Rule 8.7 **and the checker must be penalised too**. **That Hockey Canada interpretation draws its own line, and it is the part to hold on to:** it *"does not apply to a player who is in a vulnerable position as the result of a legitimate hockey play (e.g. shooting, making or receiving a pass, etc.)"* — a head that is low because you are shooting or taking a pass is not a duck, and under Hockey Canada the checker still answers for it. **No such exception was found in USA Hockey's Playing Rules, nor anywhere in Hockey Canada's rules, in any division — Rule 7.6 and Rule 7.7 both, with their Interpretations.** USA Hockey Rule 620's Note puts the onus on the checker *"to avoid placing a vulnerable or defenseless opponent in danger of potential injury"*, and USA Hockey defines vulnerable or defenceless as *"unaware, unprepared, or unsuspecting of an impending hit"* — which is what a head-down carrier is. The Hockey Canada Rule 7.6 preamble — the head-contact rule that governs minor and female hockey, quoted earlier in this bullet — is flatter still, penalising head contact *"regardless of the circumstances"*; and Hockey Canada's ducking interpretation is confined by its own words to *"Junior and Senior divisions"*, so a minor or female player under Hockey Canada has no equivalent. *(That absence is a search of the USA Hockey Official Playing Rules, of the separate USA Hockey Rules and Casebook, and of the Hockey Canada Playing Rules. The Casebook writes no such exception either, and its four Rule 620 situations run the other way — the only head contact they excuse is accidental contact between two players getting up after a fall (Situation 2). **Its Rule 607, Situation 2 goes further and points at the checker**, though read both halves of it: an opponent *"skating up the ice with their head down"* is there the *"vulnerable or defenseless"* player, and the check on them is charging **because** the checker *"traveled a great distance at full speed for the purpose of punishing"* them — it is the distance and the speed that make the call, not the head-down position on its own. No separate Hockey Canada casebook is held here, so the Hockey Canada half rests on its Playing Rules and the Interpretations printed in them.)* So under USA Hockey a square hit that finds a bent-over head is still your foul, at minimum a minor plus a misconduct (USA Hockey Rule 620(a)) and a mandatory major plus game misconduct where the contact is careless or reckless (USA Hockey Rule 620(b)) — **and where the force was excessive and meant to intimidate or punish, its Casebook makes the match penalty mandatory rather than optional, in the rule's "may" against the Casebook's "must" (Rule 620, Situation 4).** Do not plan on being the exception in any book, in either direction. Note which way Hockey Canada's exception points, and how far down it reaches. Keep your head up: under Hockey Canada, ducking is the version that can cost *you* the penalty. **And the penalty is not the reason not to duck.** A chin tucked to the chest removes the neck's natural curve and straightens the cervical spine, which is how a head-first collision produces a fracture — that is anatomy, not a rule, so it holds whether or not your book penalises the duck. The clipping rules on ducking are covered [above](#do-not-turn-your-back-to-an-oncoming-checker-near-the-boards).
- **Checking a player who is already in a battle with a teammate.** **USA Hockey defines them as defenceless** in so many words — *"when two or more players are physically engaged for control of the puck along the boards, they are considered to be vulnerable or defenseless"* — and says the check "must be penalized". **The IIHF book has no board-battle clause but reaches the same hit by another route:** its glossary makes a player who has neither possession nor preparation for the hit *"vulnerable"*, and *"a bodycheck to an opponent who is vulnerable is automatically considered reckless even if that bodycheck would be considered legal to a non-vulnerable opponent"* — and reckless is the trigger for the major-and-game-misconduct tier. So under the British book it is not a free hit either. Only the **NHL** rulebook leaves it purely to boarding or roughing at the referee's judgement.
- **Retaliating after the whistle.** You cannot count on the officials having seen the first one, and USA Hockey rewards *not* retaliating explicitly (Rule 615(a)). Take the hit and skate away.
- **Coming off the bench, or out of the penalty box, because it has kicked off.** **Two beliefs get players ejected here, and both are wrong under USA Hockey.** *"Only the first man off gets it"* — its Casebook says the major plus game misconduct goes to *"any player leaving the bench during an altercation, not just the first player to do so"* (Rule 629, Situation 2). And *"my penalty was up, so I was entitled to go"* — **Situation 1 penalises the player who left the box and deliberately skated away from the fight to his own bench**, because the stoppage that released him was the altercation itself. **The prohibition is in all four books and the price is different in all four:** the NHL's and the IIHF's automatic game misconduct is for the first *or second* player off (NHL 70.3 and 70.6; IIHF 70.3), Hockey Canada 10.4(e) gives the first a double minor plus a game misconduct and a later player a game misconduct only if they are penalised themselves, and USA Hockey 629(a) reaches everyone who leaves — a major **plus** a game misconduct, so your team kills five minutes short-handed with you already gone (Rule 403(a)). ⚠️ **And "first or second" is the players'-bench tier only — out of the penalty box it saves nobody.** NHL 70.4 and IIHF 70.4, each restated at 70.6, put a minor plus a game misconduct on *any* penalised player who leaves the box during a stoppage and during an altercation, and NHL 70.10 suspends for five games any player off the penalty bench who *"is not the first player to do so"*. **The third man out of the box is caught in every one of the four books.** Stay put until the referee enters the crease or signals a face-off — [Leaving the bench or the box](#leaving-the-bench-or-the-box-during-an-altercation).
- **Taking your own helmet off when it gets heated — or reaching for theirs.** The televised version of this is harmless: NHL Rule 46.6 and IIHF Rule 46.12 (46.6 in the 2026/27 book) put a minor for unsportsmanlike conduct on removing your own helmet before a fight and expressly nothing on *"[h]elmets that come off in the course of and resulting from the altercation."* **Under USA Hockey the same act is a match penalty, *"(for all age classifications)"*, and it does not need a fight** — Rule 615(c), applied by its Casebook to your own helmet *during* an altercation as well as before one, in *"any altercation where at least one player will receive a penalty."* **Hockey Canada makes it a Gross Misconduct** under Rule 10.6(f). **And a chin strap loose enough for more than one finger is a game misconduct of its own if the helmet then comes off.** Do it up, keep your hands down, and skate away — [Helmets in an altercation](#helmets-in-an-altercation--keep-yours-on-and-never-touch-theirs).
- **Sending a player back out after a head knock because they were not knocked out — or once they say the headache has gone.** Loss of consciousness is not required for a concussion and is not typical of one, and CRT6 bars a return to any activity risking head contact, a fall or a collision until the player has been assessed medically, *"even if the symptoms resolve"*. Suspected concussion means off the ice, no return that day, **and a medical professional before they play again.**
- **Letting them drive home, or sending them home on their own.** CRT6 says a player with a suspected concussion should not be sent home by themselves — *"[t]hey need to be with a responsible adult"* — and should not drive *"until cleared to do so by a healthcare professional"*. In adult hockey this is the instruction that gets skipped, because it happens after everyone has left the rink.
- **Helping up a player who may have hurt their neck.** The most common and most damaging mistake made after a spinal impact. Do not move them and do not let them move. Call for medical help, check airway and breathing, reassure them, keep them warm and immobile — head and neck especially — and leave the helmet on. See [the on-ice spinal-injury response above](#if-it-happens-anyway--a-suspected-neck-injury-on-the-ice).

## Check yourself

*Answer each question before you go on — a real attempt, not a guess. Then go
and check, because the checking is the part that does the work. Producing an
answer and getting it wrong is worth more than recognising a right one, but only
when the right answer follows: the section named with each question, and the Key
Takeaways after them, are where you get it. Where the answer lives in another
document, that is named too.*

1. What is the difference between body contact and body checking, which of the two
   is legal in every league at every age, and what does "non-check" therefore not
   mean? *(§1. Which League Are You Actually In?)*

2. You play in Britain and want to know the age at which body checking becomes
   legal. What do the IIHF Rule Book and the In-House Rules say about it, and
   where does the answer actually live? *(§If you play in Britain)*

3. You are closing on a puck carrier heading for the wall. Name the four things
   you are managing in the angle, and say why you must not cross over in the last
   few strides. *(§2. Angling — the four things you are managing)*

4. Contact is coming and you are near the boards. Turning your back feels like
   protecting yourself — why is it the opposite, and what are the three ranked
   cases you should be aiming for instead, best first? *(§6. Do not turn your back
   to an oncoming checker near the boards)*

5. You want to take away a net-front player's tip and rebound. Why is a
   cross-check the wrong tool even with your blade on the ice — and where exactly
   on their stick must a lift be taken instead, and why there? *(§The cross-check
   problem, and §3. The stick lift)*

6. A teammate is already tangled with an opponent along the wall and you arrive
   third. Why is that hit not available to you? Give the route each of the two
   books takes to get there. *(§Boarding, and the player who cannot see you coming)*

7. A player is down after a hit and may have hurt their neck. What is the most
   common and most damaging thing bystanders do, and what are your actual steps in
   order? *(§If it happens anyway — a suspected neck injury on the ice)*

8. Your league does not permit checking and you still want to defend properly.
   What does Rule 56.1 entitle you to do with your body, and what are the two
   things that turn all of it into a penalty? *(§4. Body Position as Defence)*

9. "The onus is on the checker." Which rulebooks actually state that onus for
   checking from behind, and which well-known one does not state it at all? Then
   name the one thing the sentence does *not* mean for the player delivering the
   hit, and the one thing it does not mean for the player receiving it — and say
   why neither changes what you should do.
   *(§Checking from behind, and what the onus does and does not mean, and §6. Do not turn your back to an
   oncoming checker near the boards)*

10. Your penalty has just expired, play stops because a fight has broken out,
    and you step out of the box and skate to your own bench without going near
    it. What does USA Hockey assess you, and what has to happen before leaving
    stops being the offence? Then say how the answer differs in the other three
    books. *(§Leaving the bench or the box during an altercation)*

---

## Key Takeaways

1. **Find out whether your league permits body checking before you play a shift, and get the answer in writing.** Body *contact* is legal everywhere; body *checking* is not — and where it is barred, a check is **not** a two-minute mistake: under both books that bar it the ladder runs minor, major plus a game misconduct, and match penalty. Age alone does not settle it, because Hockey Canada also bars checking in any other division a Member has approved and does not publish the list. **One adult book settles it in print, and the answer is no** — the CARHA rule book has no checking division at all, its Rule 49(a) makes a body check a minor or a major anywhere on the ice and a major where it injures, and Rule 30(a) rules that major off the ice for the rest of the game; that binds CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only. **In Britain you cannot look up an age** — neither the IIHF Rule Book nor the In-House Rules states a body-checking age, so do not assume Britain copies the North American lines. **Women's hockey is the exception, and it is published:** it runs IIHF Rule 101.1, a restricted form of bodychecking, with Adult from the 14th birthday under In-House Rule 100.1. **And in British women's hockey a major is never just five minutes, at any age** — In-House Rule 101 adds an automatic game misconduct and ejection to any major *"in all cases"*, adult play included. Every division and every rule number is in [Which League Are You Actually In?](#1-which-league-are-you-actually-in), the penalty ladder in [Delivering a Legal Body Check](#5-delivering-a-legal-body-check), and the British detail in [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md).
2. **Angling is legal in every league, and it is how good defenders take the puck without hitting anyone.** Steer opponents with your route, not your body: take away the middle, keep your momentum heading back toward your own net, close under control, and arrive stick-on-puck.
3. **Stick on the puck is legal; stick on their hands or body is a penalty — and the stick lift is the best application of it.** Take the lift **up rather than sideways, and below their bottom hand**: at or near the hands the IIHF's Rule 55.1 reaches it on the location alone — subject to an unresolved carve-out in that same rule, below — and USA Hockey and Hockey Canada reach it where it stops the opponent playing the puck. Low is the answer under all four either way. **The NHL is not silent on the hands either, and the slashing route is not the NHL's alone** — its hooking rule writes no hands sentence, but Rule 61.1 makes a forceful chop on or near them **slashing** unless it is an attempt to play the puck, which a clean lift for the puck is; IIHF 61.1 is that sentence word for word and USA Hockey 634 a shortened form of it with no *"attempt to play the puck"* let-out at all. **And a chop is never the cheaper option:** it can reach a **major plus a game misconduct with nobody injured** in all four books (NHL 61.3 with 61.5, IIHF 61.3, USA Hockey 634(b), Hockey Canada 9.3(b)), where a hooking major needs an injury under the NHL (55.3) and Hockey Canada (8.2(b)) but not under the IIHF (55.3) or USA Hockey (623(b)), whose hooking rules reach that same tier for recklessly endangering an opponent. Taken low it costs far less penalty risk than a poke check or a body check, **but not none**. **And one lift is worth passing on:** against an opponent bent low over the puck their face is down at stick height, and a lift that misses and catches it is high-sticking however low your own hands were — the reference is the waist of the player you hit, not yours (NHL 60.3; IIHF 60.3 in the 2026/27 book; IIHF Situation Handbook 2026/27, Situation 60.13) — and it is not capped at the double minor either (IIHF 60.4; USA Hockey 621(b)) — **and a fifth book on disk starts above it**, CARHA Rule 62(b) making contact above an opponent's shoulders a **major** whether accidental or not and whether or not anyone is injured, with a **game misconduct** on top where it was intentional or where an accidental one injures, in CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only. Take that one under the shaft, or leave it alone. Two things people get wrong here are book-specific rather than laws of hockey: *"stick on their stick is legal"* holds in only **two of the four** rulebooks, and the folklore that a blade on the ice makes a two-handed shaft shove legal is **one book's wording, absent from the other three** — and a cross-check is not a two-minute foul in any of the four books either. The rule numbers and the divergences are in [Stick Checks](#3-stick-checks--legal-effective-and-under-used), and what actually gets called is in [What actually gets called](#what-actually-gets-called).
4. **You are entitled to the ice you are standing on** (NHL Rule 56.1). You can block a path, stand your ground, box out and lengthen an opponent's route to the puck — as long as you do not use your stick to make yourself wider or your free hand to hold, pull, tug or grab. **And unless your league permits full body checking, only as long as you hold your position rather than drive them off theirs:** walking an opponent out at the net front is a push. Where checking is barred it is a minor under USA Hockey Rule 604(c) and Hockey Canada Rule 7.3(a) — the latter inside the scope Rule 7.3 sets for itself, *"divisions of U13 and below, in female hockey, and any other divisions approved by a Member of Hockey Canada"* — **and neither of those minors is the ceiling**: USA Hockey 604(d) reaches a major plus a game misconduct for recklessly endangering an opponent and 604(e) a match penalty, while Hockey Canada 7.3(b) reaches the same pair on *"the degree of violence of the impact"* with nobody hurt, mandatorily where the check injures, and 7.3(c) a match penalty. **In women's hockey, where checking is restricted rather than barred, it is an illegal hit under IIHF Rule 101.1** — *"any move by a Player to step or glide into an opposing Player will be assessed at least a minor penalty (2') for an 'illegal hit'"*, with a major (5') plus an automatic game misconduct above it.
5. **A legal check is trunk on trunk, above their knees and at or below their shoulders, on a player who has the puck, to win the puck.** **Only two of the four books state that envelope in those words** — the NHL defines no legal check at all, and Hockey Canada works by directional force instead. **Play the envelope anyway:** it is the stricter standard, and a check that satisfies it is legal in all four. Add two habits to it. Start with **your stick blade below the knees** — that is one book's written enforcement standard rather than a universal rule, but a stick that is down means hands, elbows and shoulder all arrive where they should. And **never leave your feet**: jumping into a check is charging in its own right, however short the approach. The wind-up counts vary by book — two strides written, "multiple" unnumbered, or no figure at all — and are in [Delivering a Legal Body Check: Technique](#delivering-a-legal-body-check-technique).
6. **Never hit a player from behind, near the head, into the boards, with speed built up to punish, or after they have already passed or shot.** Checking from behind, head contact, boarding and charging **bind every division, including yours if you play non-check.** The body-checking rules are the ones scoped to a division — and where head contact is split by division too, as it is under Hockey Canada, both halves are covered and nobody is exempt. **Checking from behind is the one that is never just two minutes** — no book here lets you off with a bare minor for it. **Under CARHA it does not leave you on the ice at all** — Rule 53(a)'s floor is a minor plus a *game* misconduct, not the ordinary misconduct USA Hockey's 608(a) attaches, which you serve and come back from (CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only). ⚠️ **And two of the four fouls are wider than they look under USA Hockey.** Boarding there is not only a hit: Rule 603's Note reaches a player who *"pushes, trips or body checks"* an opponent dangerously into the boards, and its Casebook prefers the boarding call over tripping where a trip sends somebody into the end wall. And where a check used excessive force to intimidate or punish and contacted the head, its Casebook turns 620(c)'s *"may also be assessed"* into *"must be assessed as a match penalty"*. **Boarding, charging and head contact do start at a minor in most books, and none of the four caps them there:** a major, a game misconduct or a match penalty sits above, on violence, injury and which book you are under. All four of these fouls reach a **match penalty in three of the four books** — **the IIHF writes no match penalty at all**, so a British reader's ceiling is a major plus a game misconduct — automatic for checking from behind and head contact (43.3, 48.3), at the referee's discretion for boarding and charging (41.4, 42.4). **The onus is on you**, and where a book does soften it, that clause is written for boarding and may not exist in the rule you are actually judged under — so play it as though it were absolute, because a spinal injury is not undone by a favourable call. **And the cost need not end tonight:** all four books let a game misconduct reach the next game or beyond. The rule numbers and the floors are spread across the last four sections of §5 — head contact and charging in [The four that cause the injuries](#the-four-that-cause-the-injuries), boarding in [Boarding, and the player who cannot see you coming](#boarding-and-the-player-who-cannot-see-you-coming), checking from behind and the onus in [Checking from behind](#checking-from-behind-and-what-the-onus-does-and-does-not-mean), and what a game misconduct costs beyond tonight in [And what these fouls cost beyond tonight](#and-what-these-fouls-cost-beyond-tonight).
7. **Taking a hit near the wall: head up, chin off your chest, never your back to the boards, never duck.** Go in on an angle. If contact is coming, get your **skates parallel to the wall** and take it on your **forearm and hip** — not the point of your shoulder. If you are already facing the wall and cannot turn, get your gloves and stick up and spread out on the glass; that is damage limitation, not the position you were aiming for. **The two prohibitions exist for two different injuries.** Turning your back makes the contact a hit from behind, which USA Hockey warns can cause severe head or neck injury *even from a light hit*. Ducking straightens the cervical spine, and it is the **injury** from that head-down, head-first collision with the boards or goal post that USA Hockey says *"can occur at walking speed"* — there is no speed slow enough to make it safe. Crouching to avoid a check is itself penalised, and how far that reaches depends on your book: see [Receiving a Hit Safely](#6-receiving-a-hit-safely).
8. **If a player may have hurt their head or neck, nobody moves and somebody phones.** Do not help them up. Call for immediate medical help, observe the ABCs, reassure them, keep them warm, and instruct and immobilise them so they do not move — head and neck especially. Leave the helmet on. Assume a possible spinal injury in every head injury: neck pain or tenderness is itself an ambulance. Full response in [If it happens anyway](#if-it-happens-anyway--a-suspected-neck-injury-on-the-ice). **And the commoner case — they get up.** A suspected concussion means off the ice, no return that day, **and a medical professional before they play again**, whether or not they were knocked out; do not let the player decide. **Symptoms clearing up is not the all-clear** — CRT6 bars a return to any activity risking head contact, a fall or a collision until the player has been assessed medically, *"even if the symptoms resolve"*. **And somebody drives them and somebody stays with them:** CRT6 says a player with a suspected concussion should not be sent home by themselves and should not drive until a healthcare professional clears them.
9. **Battles are usually won by the lower player, not the stronger one, and by the second man staying out of the pile.** Wide base, bent knees, body between opponent and puck, wall on the far side — though leverage does not always beat strength, and a large enough strength gap wins anyway. Use your skates to kick the puck free when your stick is tied up — **at the puck, never at a player** — a match penalty under the NHL book *whether or not an injury occurs*, a match penalty under USA Hockey and Hockey Canada too, and under the IIHF — Britain's book, which writes no match penalty — a major at the referee's discretion with an automatic game misconduct on top. And if you cannot see the puck, do not swing at where you think it is. In the corner, first man engages and stops the **puck** — pin the puck, not the player, because the puck being the object of the contact is what keeps it legal (USA Hockey Rule 604, Note 1), and IIHF Rule 101.1 bars pinning an opponent along the boards in women's hockey outright — and second man reads where it squirts. **And never put a leg behind them:** slew-footing is a mandatory match penalty under NHL 52.2 with no lesser tier, an ejection at the floor in three books of four, and Hockey Canada's double minor (8.8(a)) is a floor rather than a ceiling because 8.8(c) reaches a match penalty. At the net front, control sticks before you get physical — **and when you do move a body there, move it toward the corner and not across your own goalmouth**, because that path has a steel goal post at one edge and your goaltender at the other and it is the player being moved who meets the post. **Head up and chin off your chest is the instruction at both ends of it**: USA Hockey puts the goal post in the head-first spinal-injury mechanism alongside the boards, at what it says can be walking pace. The corner direction is coaching caution rather than a rule, but the worst version of it is priced and never at two minutes — USA Hockey 608(b) a major plus a game misconduct, Hockey Canada 7.5(c) a match penalty, and neither the NHL nor the IIHF writing any minor for checking from behind at all (43.2 in both). **Whose corner it is in your own end is a coaching choice** — find out which system you play. Detail in [Board Battles](#7-board-battles) and [Whose corner is it?](#whose-corner-is-it).
10. **Block shots close, on your feet, hands tucked, head out of the lane — and do not block the shot on a 2-on-1 or from in front of your own goalie's eyes.** How much your team blocks at all is a coaching choice, so ask your goalie what they want. Most amateur penalties, meanwhile, are late, frustrated or lazy rather than dirty: if you lost position, the only legal way to get it back is with your feet. Skate more, reach less. **And if it does boil over, your gloves stay on and so does your helmet.** Deliberately removing your own helmet, or an opponent's, in an altercation is a **match penalty at every age under USA Hockey** (Rule 615(c)) and a **Gross Misconduct under Hockey Canada** (Rule 10.6(f)) — and under USA Hockey's Casebook it needs no punch, only an altercation in which somebody takes a penalty. The NHL and the IIHF price the same act at a minor and penalise nothing for a helmet that comes off in the fight itself, which is exactly why it is worth knowing that they are not your book. **And if it kicks off while you are on the bench or in the box, stay there.** All four books bar leaving during an altercation, and under **USA Hockey 629(a)** it is a major plus a game misconduct — for every player who leaves rather than only the first, and for the player who steps out on an expired penalty and skates away from the fight just the same (Casebook Rule 629, Situations 1 and 2). **The NHL's and the IIHF's "first or second player off" is their *players'-bench* tier and nothing more** — out of the **penalty box** both of those books put a minor plus a game misconduct on *any* penalised player who leaves during a stoppage and during an altercation (70.4, restated at 70.6), and the NHL adds a five-game suspension to every one of them past the first (70.10). **There is no book in which the third man out of the box is clear.** Wait until the referee enters the crease or signals a face-off.

---

*Sources — retrieved 27 July 2026:*

*Rulebooks: [NHL Official Rules 2025-2026 (PDF)](https://media.d3.nhle.com/image/private/t_document/prd/slwjuaqwmuvj5bkplixo.pdf) — Rule 41 Boarding, 42 Charging, 43 Checking from Behind, 44 Clipping, 45 Elbowing, **46 Fighting** (46.2's aggressor definition and tier; 46.11's instigator tier — *"an instigating minor penalty, a major penalty for fighting and a ten-minute misconduct"* — with its escalation to a game misconduct for a second instigator penalty in the same game and a third in one regular season; 46.14's major; and 46.16's third-man game misconduct, including its exception, *"except when a match penalty is being imposed in the original altercation"*, all read 31 August 2026), 48 Illegal Check to the Head, **49 Kicking** (49.1's definition, 49.2's four kicked-puck clarifiers, and 49.3's match penalty), 51 Roughing, **52 Slew-footing** (52.1's two-limbed definition, ending on a player made to *"fall violently to the ice"*, and 52.2's flat, mandatory match penalty with no tier beneath it), 55 Hooking (with **55.3**, whose major requires that the player *"injures an opponent"*, and 55.4), 56 Interference, **57 Tripping** (57.1's definition — a stick, knee, foot, arm, hand or elbow placed *"in such a manner that causes his opponent to trip or fall"* — and 57.3's puck-first carve-out, which converts the penalty shot to a minor and does not remove the minor), **59 Cross-checking** (59.1's definition; 59.2 and 59.3, whose minor and major both turn on *"the severity of the contact"* and not on injury; 59.4's match penalty where the player *"attempted to or deliberately injured his opponent by cross-checking"*; and 59.5, *"When a major penalty is assessed for cross-checking, an automatic game misconduct penalty shall be imposed"*, all read 30 August 2026), 60 High-sticking, 61 Slashing (with **61.3**, whose major turns on *"the severity of the contact"* and not on injury, and 61.5), 63.2(i) Delaying the Game (freezing the puck), **69 Interference on the Goalkeeper** (69.1, the premise that position inside or outside the crease does not by itself decide the goal, and 69.3 on contact inside the crease), **70 Leaving the Bench** — 70.1's flat bar, *"No player may leave the players' or penalty bench at any time during an altercation or for the purpose of starting an altercation"*; 70.3 and 70.6, whose game misconduct is written for *"the first or second player to leave"*; 70.4 and 70.6's minor plus game misconduct for *"[a]ny penalized player leaving the penalty bench during a stoppage of play and during an altercation"*; and 70.10's automatic ten-game and five-game suspensions for the first and second player off, all read 1 September 2026 in the 2025-2026 edition and found word for word identical in the 2024-2025 one · [NHL Official Rules 2024-2025 (PDF)](https://media.d3.nhle.com/image/private/t_document/prd/yikcdsxofkmgsrhjl3di.pdf) — **Rule 60 High-sticking only**, read against the 2025-2026 text on 31 August 2026, which is what dates the high-sticking rewrite stated above. Its **60.1** reads *"A 'high stick' is one which is carried above the height of the opponent's shoulders"* and its **60.3** *"When a player carries or holds any part of his stick above the shoulders and makes contact with his opponent's neck, face or head so that injury results"* — so the waist floor is absent from both clauses in the 2024-2025 edition and present in both in the 2025-2026 one, and the 2025/26 IIHF wording quoted above is word for word the NHL's superseded 60.1. **60.2 is identical in the two editions.** No other rule was compared across them, and no NHL edition earlier than 2024-2025 was consulted, so *when* before 2025-26 the older wording had stood is not established here. The NHL book carries no summary of rule changes and dates no rule · [USA Hockey 2025-29 Official Playing Rules](https://cdn2.sportngin.com/attachments/document/945a-3442848/2025-29_USAH_Playing_Rules.pdf) — Declaration of Player Safety glossary (Body Checking, Competitive Contact, Angling) and its **Late Avoidable Body Check** paragraph, which is where *"[t]he concept of 'finishing the check' is an unacceptable action as it is one that is meant to intimidate or punish the opponent with no intent to gain possession of the puck"* is written — the book's own **Glossary** repeats it under *"Late Body Check"* in the wider form, *"no intent, or possibility, to gain possession"*; **Rule 603 Boarding**, whose Note states the onus flat — *"The onus is on the player delivering the check to avoid placing a vulnerable or defenseless opponent in danger"*, with no balancing sentence after it, unlike NHL 41.1, IIHF 41.1 and Hockey Canada 7.2, read 31 August 2026, and **read again in full on 1 September 2026** for two clauses not previously carried: the Note's **first** paragraph, which defines boarding as the action where a player *"pushes, trips or body checks an opponent causing them to go dangerously into the boards"* — the only one of the four books to name a trip, against *"checks or pushes"* in NHL 41.1, IIHF 41.1 and Hockey Canada 7.2 — and the sentence immediately after 603(a), *"'Rolling' an opponent along the boards where they are attempting to go through too small an opening is not considered boarding"*, which likewise has no counterpart in the other three books; **Rule 602(a) Attempt to Injure or Recklessly Endanger an Opponent** (*"A match penalty shall be assessed to any player or team official who recklessly endangers or attempts to injure any opposing player or team official"* — tied to no named foul at all); **Rule 640 Unnecessary Roughness (Roughing)** — **640(b)**, the late-hit minor written *"(except Adult Male Classifications)"*; **640 Note 2**, which directs a roughing penalty for *"an avoidable body check to an opponent after the whistle or who is no longer in control of the puck"* with no classification exception; and **640(g)** and **640(h)**, the major plus game misconduct and the match penalty for reckless endangerment, both of which reach a late hit only through sub-section (b), while (d) and (e) carry no classification limit; **Rule 629 Leaving the Players' Bench or Penalty Bench** — 629(a) read in full on 1 September 2026, including the proviso that *"[s]ubstitutions made prior to the altercation shall not be penalized under this rule provided the players so substituting do not enter the altercation"* and the clause concluding an altercation *"when the referee enters the referee's crease or, in the absence of penalties, signals a face-off location"*; **Rule 404(b)**, whose game misconduct suspends the player for *"the team's next game"*; **Rule 411 Progressive Suspensions** — 411(a) read in full on 31 August 2026, including its three-, four- and five-major rungs and the seventeen-rule aggressive-infractions list, which **runs across a page break and is complete only across both pages**, its last two entries being Rule 639 and Rule 640; **Rule 615 Fighting** — 615(a), whose third paragraph reads *"A minor, double minor or major plus game misconduct penalty, at the discretion of the Referee, shall be assessed to any player who, having been struck, continues the altercation by retaliating. A player who does not retaliate after being struck shall not be assessed a penalty for fighting under this rule"* — note the protection is written *"under this rule"* and so does not reach roughing or misconduct; **Rule 617(c)(1)**, which disallows a goal where *"an attacking player kicks the puck directly into the goal or the kicked puck deflects off any player, including goalkeeper, prior to entering the goal"*; **Rule 627 Kicking Opponent or Puck** — 627(a) the push-off, 627(b) the match penalty, 627(c) the kicked-puck goal, which writes no stick-versus-body distinction; all read 30 August 2026. Rule 604 Body Checking, 608 Checking from Behind, **609 Cross-Checking** (the Note's *"with no portion of the stick on the ice"* wording, and 609(b) and 609(c) — the major plus game misconduct that *"shall be assessed"* and the match penalty that *"may also be assessed"*, both for recklessly endangering an opponent by cross-checking, both read 30 August 2026), 610(a) Delaying the Game (deliberately freezing the puck along the boards), 620 Head Contact, **621 High Sticks** (621(b), the major plus game misconduct that *"shall be assessed to any player who recklessly endangers an opponent as a result of high sticking"*, and 621(c), the match penalty available for the same, both read 1 September 2026; the 621 Note measures against *"the normal height of the opponent's shoulders"*, and a de-hyphenated whole-book search of the playing rules **and** the Casebook on 1 September 2026 finds *"normal"* defined in neither), **639 Tripping/Clipping/Leg Checking/Slew Footing** (Note 4's definition; 639(a), which writes the minor for the family *"except slew footing"*; 639(b), whose second sentence reads *"The minimum penalty to be assessed for slew footing is a major plus game misconduct penalty"*; and 639(c), the match penalty for reckless endangerment), 623 Hooking (with **623(b)** and **623(c)**, the major plus game misconduct that *"shall be assessed"* for recklessly endangering an opponent by hooking, and the match penalty above it), 625(b) (the position bar on a goal with an attacking player in the goal crease, and the push-in relief in its third paragraph — "if the attacking player has been physically interfered with by the actions of a defending player that causes them to be in the goal crease, play shall not be stopped and any legal goal scored shall be allowed" — read 31 August 2026; the book was read in full for a screening or goaltender-vision provision and has none), 634 Slashing (its Note — *"Any forceful chop with the stick on an opponent's body or opponent's stick, on or near the opponent's hands, shall be considered slashing"* — carrying no *"attempt to play the puck"* let-out, with **634(b)** and **634(c)** matching 623(b) and (c)) · [USA Hockey Standard of Play — Body Checking 2025-29](https://cdn1.sportngin.com/attachments/document/ef49-3383370/USAH_Playing_Rules_SOP_Body_Checking_2025-29.pdf) — its **CROSS-CHECKING** heading, read 30 August 2026, whose two worked examples are the net-front shove *"in a dangerous manner to create space"* and the same shove on a player *"protecting the puck along the boards"*; the playing rule at 609 carries none. Also its **ROUGHING**, **HEAD CONTACT** and **CHARGING** headings, quoted above · [USA Hockey Official Rules and Casebook 2025-29 (PDF)](https://cdn3.sportngin.com/attachments/document/4f1a-3442850/2025-29_USAH_Playing_Rules___Casebook.pdf) — **Standard of Play, Situation 10**, on when a body check may still be finished after the puck has gone: a prior effort to gain possession, *"an arm's length plus stick length"* at the instant the puck was dumped, the check already *"in the process of being delivered"*, and minimised force, with *"one or two more strides after the puck is gone"* ruled out in terms — corroborated at **Rule 640, Situation 3** and **Standard of Play, Situation 23**, which state the same test as avoidability and give *"finishing the check"* *"no place in youth hockey"*; and **Standard of Play, Situation 2** with **Rule 623, Situation 1**, which permit the stick lift *"at any time provided they do not impede their progress"* after Situation 1 has ruled that hooking an opponent's stick is itself a penalty. Also **Standard of Play, Situations 5, 6 and 7**, which are where USA Hockey writes its free-arm permission — absent from the playing rules — and its conditions; **Rule 620, Situations 1–4** and **Rule 607, Situation 2**, read for any exception excusing a check on a player whose head was low, of which they have none — and **read again on 1 September 2026 for their mandatory limbs**, which the playing rule does not carry: **Situation 4**, *"Any check using excessive force for the purpose of intimidating or punishing the opponent that makes direct contact with the head area of the opponent must be assessed as a match penalty"*, and **Situation 3**, *"Any time the Referee deems the contact to the head area to be intentional or a reckless act, a major plus game misconduct or match penalty must be assessed"*, both saying *must* where 620(c) says a match penalty *"may also be assessed"*; **Rule 608, Situation 1**, read for any exception for a player who turned into the check, of which it has none; **Rule 621, Situations 1–15** — the Casebook prints fifteen, and Situations 4–15 are all 621(d) high-sticking-the-puck cases — read in full on 1 September 2026 for a windup or follow-through carve-out and for anything addressing a crouching opponent, of which they have none, and where Situation 2 rules that contact is not required at all and that *"[i]t is not a requirement that a stick make contact above shoulder height for high sticking to be called"*; **Rule 603, Situations 1 and 2** with **Standard of Play, Situation 19**, read 1 September 2026 — Situation 2 restates the onus scoped *"[f]or USA Hockey youth games"*, asks *"was the check reckless and/or what was the vulnerability of the player being checked?"*, and runs the force-and-preparedness example both ways; **Standard of Play, Situation 19 prints the same weighing a second time**, asking instead *"was the check necessary and/or what was the vulnerability of the player being checked?"* and conditioning its no-penalty example on a checker who *"clearly attempted to gain possession of the puck"*, a condition Situation 2 does not carry; **Rule 603, Situation 3**, read 1 September 2026, which answers *"Yes"* to whether boarding may be called where a trailing player *"trips the opponent and causes them to lose control and crash into the end boards"*, and adds that *"[e]ven though a tripping penalty may also be appropriate, the boarding call is preferred to draw attention to the more aggressive infraction"*; **Rule 629, Situations 1 and 2**, read 1 September 2026 — Situation 1 assessing a major plus game misconduct to each of two players who left the penalty box on expired penalties at a stoppage caused by an altercation, including the one who *"does not enter the altercation, but skates to their players' bench"*, on the ground that *"[n]o player may leave the players' or penalty bench at any time during an altercation"*, and Situation 2 stating that the penalty is assessed *"to any player leaving the bench during an altercation, not just the first player to do so"*; **Rule 639, Situation 5**, which names leg checking, clipping and slew footing as the tripping actions calling for a more severe penalty, states *"A minor penalty is NOT an option for slew footing"*, and closes, after a further sentence on clipping, *"Any time one of these actions has been deemed to recklessly endanger the opponent, a match penalty must be considered"*; **Rule 627, Situations 4 and 5**, read 30 August 2026, which put the kicked-puck deflection directly — *"The puck is kicked by an attacking player, deflects off their stick and enters their opponent's goal. Should the goal be allowed?"* — and answer *"No. Rule Reference 627(c)"*, the same answer following for a deflection off a teammate; and **Rule 625, Situations 1 to 17**, read for a screening or goaltender-vision provision, of which they have none; and **Rule 621, Situations 1 to 15**, read in full on 1 September 2026 — **Situation 2** answers *"No."* to *"Must contact with an opponent occur in order for the Referee to assess a penalty for high sticking?"*, adds that *"[i]t is not a requirement that a stick make contact above shoulder height for high sticking to be called"*, and requires the call *"[w]henever a player raises their stick above their shoulders in an attempt to intimidate an opponent or is careless in their actions"*, all of which the playing rule's 621 Note does not say; **Situation 1** routes a stick to the head *"during the process of delivering a check"* to Rule 620 Head Contact instead; **Situation 3** confirms the 621(b) and 621(c) tiers on a player who recklessly endangers an opponent with a stick while falling; and **Situations 4 to 15** are all 621(d), high-sticking the puck, and bear on none of the above. Not read in full · [Hockey Canada Playing Rules 2026-2028 (PDF)](https://cdn.hockeycanada.ca/hockey-canada/Hockey-Programs/Officiating/Downloads/2026-28-hc-rulebook-e.pdf) — Rule 7.3 Body-Checking and Interpretation 1 (with 7.3(b)'s two limbs and 7.3(c)), 7.4 Charging, 7.5 Checking from Behind, **7.2 Boarding** (7.2(b)'s two limbs and 7.2(c)), **7.6 and 7.7 Head Contact** with Interpretations 1–2 to 7.6 and 1–3 to 7.7(a) — 7.6(a)'s minor for accidental contact to the head, face or neck in minor and female hockey requires no injury, and 7.6(b) and 7.7(b) each run to three paragraphs and only one of each turns on an injury — and **8.7 Clipping** (8.7(b)'s two limbs and 8.7(c)), all five read 30 August 2026, 8.2 Hooking and its Interpretation 1 to 8.2(a) (the stick lift, allowed for a play on the puck, a hooking penalty where it contacts the hands), 8.2(b) (the major and game misconduct, which requires that the player *"injures an opponent"*) and 8.2(c) (*"No Match penalty may be assessed for a hooking infraction"*), **8.8 Slew-Footing** — its preamble reaching a leg or foot used to *"knock, drag, or sweep"* an opponent's feet away, 8.8(a) the double minor, 8.8(b) and 8.8(d)–(e) barring the major, misconduct and game misconduct, 8.8(c) the match penalty *"based on the degree of violence of impact (including the impact with the ice, goal, or boards)"* and mandatory on injury, and Interpretation 1 to 8.8(a) on the offender who is *"in motion"* — **9.2 Cross-Checking** — 9.2(a) the minor, 9.2(b) the discretionary major plus game misconduct *"based on the degree of violence of impact"* and the mandatory one for a player *"who injures an opponent by a cross-checking infraction that would otherwise call for a Minor penalty"*, 9.2(c) the match penalty, 9.2(e) requiring the game misconduct with any such major, and **Interpretation 2 to Rule 9.2(b)**, which sends a cross-check *"above the normal height of the shoulders"* out of Rule 9.2 to Rule 7.6 Head Contact as a major plus game misconduct that *"must be assessed"*, all read 30 August 2026 — 9.3 Slashing — which enumerates no part of the opponent, its major and game misconduct at 9.3(b) turning on *"the degree of violence of the impact"* and its match penalty at 9.3(c) — **9.5 High-Sticking – Junior/Senior** — whose preamble and 9.5(a) both measure against the *"normal height of the shoulder[s]"*, a phrase the book defines exactly once and elsewhere, at **Rule 6.9(a) Note 1** (*"The 'normal' height of the shoulders refers to when a player is standing fully upright"*), under high-sticking **the puck**, with nothing in the book saying that definition travels to 9.5 — and **Rule 6.9(c)**, read at `hc_layout.txt` on 1 September 2026, which routes a player who high-sticks or attempts to high-stick the puck and in doing so contacts an opponent to **Rule 7.6 Head Contact**, in minor and female divisions only. ⚠️ **That clause is paraphrased rather than quoted here because the book prints *"In Minor of Female divisions"* — *of* for *or* — and quoting it would reproduce the book's typo while correcting it inside quotation marks would be a fabricated quotation; a whole-book search on 1 September 2026 returns that Note as the only definition of the word — with **Interpretation 1 to 9.5(a)** (the minor where a follow-through after high-sticking the puck hits an opponent above the shoulder with no injury) and **Interpretation 2 to 9.5(a)**, whose items i–vi treat attempted restraining fouls as accidental high-sticks — item ii the attempted stick lift causing injury (a double minor) and item vi a *"pitchfork action in lifting the stick"* causing injury (a major plus game misconduct under 7.7) — read 1 September 2026, and 4.8(b)(i), the Game Ejection at three minors for *"stick infractions"*, defined there as Rules 9.1 to 9.5 and so excluding hooking, with **Interpretation 2 to Rule 4.8(b)** on the double minor counting as one infraction and Interpretation 1 to Rule 9.1(a) confirming the point for butt-end hooking — all three read 31 August 2026 *(citation-resolution note: Interpretation 1 to Rule 9.1(a) points the reader at "Rule 4.8 (a) – Game Ejection & Game Misconduct Penalties", and the Game Ejection provision is 4.8(**b**); 4.8(a) is the Game Misconduct. This document cites the body lettering rather than reproducing the book's pointer)*, 8.5 Interference with the Goaltender (its crease bar and the carve-out that follows it, under which a goal scored by a teammate outside the crease "WILL BE ALLOWED" if the crease player stays passive; the book's only two references to a goaltender's view both reach a player in the crease), **6.6(c) and 6.10 Kicking the Puck with Interpretation 1 to Rules 6.10(b) and 6.10(c)**, read 30 August 2026 — 6.10(b) states that *"no goal can be scored as the result of a kicking motion by an attacking player"* and extends that to a deflection *"off any other player or object"*, and Interpretation 1 answers the teammate case for *"skate, body or stick"* alike with *"no goal"*, and **7.1(c)(iii) Attempt to Injure**, the match penalty for *"Attempting to kick or deliberately kicking an opponent"*, and **Rule 10.4 Leaving the Players' or Penalty Bench**, read 1 September 2026 — 10.4(b) barring a Major penalty for it outright, and 10.4(e) triggering on a *fight* rather than an altercation, with (e)(i) assessing *"a double Minor penalty and a Game Misconduct penalty"* to *"[t]he first player of either team to leave their players' or Penalty Bench"* and (e)(ii) reaching a later player only where they are *"subsequently assessed a penalty"* *(the clauses quoted in this document, and Interpretation 1 to each of the three rules, are word-for-word unchanged from the superseded 2024-2026 edition. **The Rule 8.2(b), 8.2(c), 9.2, 9.3 and 4.8(b)(i) clauses are an exception to that statement: the 2024-2026 edition is not held here and those five were not compared across editions**. Rule 7.4 did change in one respect that this document does not rely on: the blindside-hit definition moved out of a Note under 7.4(a) into a new Interpretation 2, and gained the sentences "The 90° angle of approach will be determined based on the orientation of the player's body prior to receiving the hit. The direction of travel of the player receiving the hit is not a key consideration in determining if a 'blindside' hit has occurred." The old Interpretation 2 to 7.4(b) on goaltender contact is renumbered Interpretation 3.)* · [IIHF Official Rulebook 2025/26 (Version 1.1, July 2025)](https://blob.iihf.com/iihf-media/iihfmvc/media/contentimages/3_the_iihf/2025-26_iihf_rulebook_22122025-v1.pdf) — **Rule 24.8** (the penalty-shot puck-first carve-out, in the NHL's words, which likewise leaves the tripping minor standing), **41 Boarding** — 41.1 states the responsibility on the checker and then the same *"However, in determining whether such contact could have been avoided…"* balancing sentence the NHL writes, closing *"This balance must be considered by the Referees when applying this rule."*, read 31 August 2026 — 42 Charging, 43 Checking from Behind, 44 Clipping, **46 Fighting** (46.1, 46.3 Instigator / Initiator, 46.4 Aggressor, 46.6 Defender – Unwilling Combatant and 46.7 Third Player In, all read 30 August 2026 — and see the 2026/27 entry below, which renumbers every one of them), 48 Illegal Check to the Head or Neck, **49 Kicking** (49.2, whose clauses (I) to (IV) reproduce the NHL's kicked-puck clarifiers word for word, and 49.3), **52 Slew-footing** (52.1 in the NHL's words, and 52.2, headed "Major Penalty and Game Misconduct Penalty", on a "shall, at their discretion, assess" frame rather than the "may assess" of its neighbours, with no lesser tier beneath it and no match tier above it because the IIHF book writes none), 55 Hooking (including **55.1**, whose second sentence — *"If the stick goes against the opponent's hands / or near the opponent's hands, it shall be penalized as 'hooking'"* — has no counterpart in NHL 55.1, and is present unchanged in the 2026/27 edition; and **55.3**, whose major and automatic game misconduct turn on reckless endangerment rather than injury; both read 31 August 2026), 56 Interference, **59 Cross-checking** (59.1's definition and 59.3, headed "Major Penalty and Game Misconduct Penalty", whose major plus automatic game misconduct turns on recklessly endangering an opponent rather than on injury, read 30 August 2026), **61 Slashing** (61.1 reproducing the NHL's hands sentence word for word, and 61.3 pairing the major with an automatic game misconduct for reckless endangerment), 100 Women's Hockey Age Categories, 101 Women's Hockey Specific Playing Rules, 200–202 Men's Junior Hockey, and the Definitions appendix entries for "Body Checking", "Body Contact" and "Vulnerable Position". **Rule 70 Leaving the Players' Bench or Penalty Box was read in full on 1 September 2026 in this edition and in the 2026/27 one, and the two are substantively identical on every clause quoted in this document** — 70.1's flat bar, 70.3's game misconduct for *"[t]he Player who was the first or second Player to leave the Players' bench (or penalty box) during an altercation"*, and 70.4's minor plus game misconduct for *"[a]ny penalized Player leaving the penalty box during a stoppage of play and during an altercation"*. The 2026/27 text adds potential supplementary discipline at 70.3 and exposes the coach to it as well; neither changes any of the three. *(All cited rules were read in the 2025/26 text, which is the edition used as the baseline throughout this guide and the foundation adopted by England Ice Hockey, the SIHA and the BUIHA. Rules 42, 43, 44, 46, 48, 55 and 56 were compared against the May 2025 v1.0 text and are substantively identical; Rules 100, 101 and 200–202 were not compared across the two versions. Rule 101.1 was read in full against the statement of it in §1, and the wording there is correct.)* · [IIHF Official Rulebook 2026/27 (Version 1.0, June 2026)](https://blob.iihf.com/iihf-media/iihfmvc/media/downloads/rule%20book/2026-27_iihf_rule_book.pdf) — **Rule 60 High-sticking**, read in full against the 2025/26 text: **60.1** carries the NHL's *"provided their shoulders are at waist level or higher"* sentence word for word where the 2025/26 book tests a stick *carried* above the shoulders, **60.3** gains the parenthesis *"(above the height of the opponent's waist)"*, and **60.2** is identical in both. **60.4 is identical in both as well** — the major and *"automatic game misconduct penalty"* the referee may assess where a Player *"recklessly endangers their opponent by 'high-sticking'"* — compared 1 September 2026, so that escalation does not turn on which edition a reader is under. The 60.1 windup and follow-through carve-out, including the bent-over centre at a face-off, is identical in both — and that carve-out is word for word the NHL's. **Rule 41.1 Boarding was compared across the two editions on 1 September 2026 and is unchanged**, ending on the same *"This balance must be considered by the Referees when applying this rule."* Also **Rule 46 Fighting**, read in full against the 2025/26 text on 30 August 2026 and **renumbered throughout** — instigator 46.3 to **46.10**, aggressor 46.4 to **46.2**, third player in 46.7 to **46.14**, and 46.6 changed from *Defender – Unwilling Combatant* to *Helmets*. Two changes of substance: **46.14** replaces 46.7's blanket exception with a waiver conditional on an aggressor penalty in the original altercation **and** the referee's judgment that the third player in was *"intervening to stop the ongoing altercation with an 'unwilling combatant' and not to start a secondary altercation"*; and **46.6's retaliation sentence is gone**, the unwilling-combatant idea surviving only as a note under 46.2, 46.8, 46.10 and 46.12 waiving *"the automatic game misconduct penalty"* at the referee's discretion. The instigator and aggressor tiers are unchanged. A de-hyphenated, whitespace-flattened whole-book search puts *"few punches"* at 2 occurrences in the 2025/26 text and **0** in the 2026/27 one. Also **Rule 49 Kicking** and **Rule 59 Cross-checking**, each compared across the two editions and word-for-word identical. *(Rules 46, 49, 59 and 60 were compared across the two editions; every other IIHF rule cited in this document is cited from the 2025/26 text above and was not re-checked against 2026/27.)* · [IIHF Situation Handbook 2026/27 (Version 1.0, June 2026)](https://blob.iihf.com/iihf-media/iihfmvc/media/downloads/officiating%20files/situation%20handbook/2026-27_iihf_situation_handbook.pdf) — **Situation 60.13**, on a stick-lift attempt that catches a bent-over opponent in the face while the striker's own stick is *"only slightly above the height of their waist"*, and the Foreword's *"in case of any contradictions, the valid IIHF Rulebook is always the basis."* Situation 60.13 has no counterpart in the 2025/26 Handbook, whose Rule 60 situations end at 60.12 · [IHUK In-House Rules 2026-27, Revision 1 (published 25/07/26)](https://englandicehockey.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/08/IHUK-In-House-Rules-2026-2027.pdf) — the Introduction, which adopts *"the latest IIHF Official Rule Book (Version 1.1, published July 2025)"* — that being the 2025/26 book. *(Its **Section 6 – Physical Fouls** was read in full on 1 September 2026 and amends only fighting; a search of the document for "board" returns nothing, so no boarding amendment was found in it. Its **Section 8 – Stick Fouls** amends Rule 60 in one respect only — *"60.3 – On-ice video review is not available in EIH or SIHA competition"* — so the double minor stands but the referee's video-review option in 60.3 does not, in those competitions.)* *(The Rule 100.1, 101, 200 and 201 clauses quoted in §1 are word-for-word identical in this edition and the 2025-26 one; the junior birth-year bands shift by a season and are not stated here.)* · [IHUK In-House Rules 2025-26, Revision 1 (published 27/10/25)](https://englandicehockey.com/wp-content/uploads/2025/11/IHUK-In-House-Rules-25-26-R1.pdf) — Introduction (adoption of the IIHF book), Section 6 Physical Fouls (fighting), Section 11 Women's Ice Hockey (Rules 100.1, 101, 102), Section 12 Junior Ice Hockey (Rules 200, 201). Full amendment list in [UK and England Rules](../foundation/uk_rules.md) · [PWHL Rulebook 2025-26](https://assets.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltebdb4296e05d53db/bltb8440fe08a5e6dc5/69178cd0a582d7db321ad107/2025-2026_PWHL_Rulebook.pdf) — Rule 52 Body Checking · [CARHA Hockey Official Rule Book, printed 2020 (PDF)](https://carhahockey.ca/wp-content/uploads/2024/01/RuleBk-2020-interactive-1.pdf) — **Rule 49 Boarding and Body Contact**, read in full by line range on 2 September 2026, all four clauses (a) to (d) with the Note to (b), together with its two **Rule 49 case situations** in the book's Case Situations section (a bodycheck at centre ice, *"A Minor or Major Penalty – at the discretion of the Referee"*; a bodycheck into the boards, *"A double Minor penalty… should a player be injured as a result of the incident a Major penalty… should the incident be of a serious nature the Referee may impose a Match penalty"*), and the book's referee's-signal page, which prints one signal headed *"BOARDING AND BODY CHECKING"*. **Rules 30 (Major Penalties), 32 (Game Ejection/Game Misconduct Penalties) and 34 (Match Penalties)** were read the same day for what those tiers cost. **Verification note:** the book was searched for any checking division or contact classification — *body check*, *bodycheck*, *body contact*, *non-contact*, *checking*, *division*, *junior*, *minor hockey*, *age category* — and it has none. Every hit is an offence, a definition or a signal rather than a classification: Rule 49, Rule 53 (Checking from Behind), Rule 54 (Cross-Checking), Rule 71(b) (a body check on an official), the Note to Rule 86 on tripping, the Glossary entries for *Checking From Behind* and *Cross-Checking*, the referee's-signal page and the case situations. The only *division* in the book is the tournament kind, in its tie-breaking and substitution rules. The adult scope is the book's own front matter — its Mission Statement is *"dedicated to servicing the adult recreational and oldtimers' hockey community in Canada"* — rather than a scoping rule inside the playing rules, and the book contains no junior or minor division anywhere in it. **Rule 62 High Sticks**, read in full on 2 September 2026: 62(a)'s minor for a player who *"checks an opponent or intimidates an opponent while carrying their stick above the waist height of their opponent"*; **62(b)**, *"[a] player who accidentally or intentionally contacts an opponent above the normal height of the opponent's shoulders with a high stick shall be assessed a Major penalty, whether or not injury results"*, with its Note adding a game misconduct where the contact was intentional, or accidental and injuring; and 62(c)'s match penalty. 62(d), the fourth and last clause, is a different subject — a stick on the **puck** above your own shoulders, which stops play for a face-off and carries no penalty at all. **Rule 50 Head Contact** was read the same day and is written for contact *"with any part of the player's body or equipment other than a stick"*, its opening Note reading *"If a stick is used on contact this infraction falls under Rule 62 – High Sticks"* — so there is no route back down to a minor for a stick above the shoulders. **Rule 50's own tiers**, read the same day, are 50(a)'s Minor for a player who *"accidentally contacts an opponent in the head, face or neck"*, 50(b)'s Double Minor or Major plus Game Misconduct where the contact is intentional, and 50(c)'s Match penalty for a deliberate attempt to injure. **Rule 52 Charging and Rule 53 Checking from Behind** were read in full on 2 September 2026 for §5: 52(a)'s Minor or Major *"to any player who runs or jumps into, or charges an opponent"*, its mandatory Major and Game Misconduct *"[i]f injury results"*, and its Note *"If more than two steps or strides are taken, it shall be considered a charge"*, with 52(b)'s Major and Game Misconduct for charging a goalkeeper in the crease; and 53(a)'s floor of *"[a] Minor penalty plus a Game Misconduct"*, its mandatory Major plus Game Misconduct *"[i]f a player is injured"*, its *"[a] Match penalty could also be assessed under this rule"*, and **53(b)**'s Match penalty with the Note instructing referees *"not to substitute other penalties when a player is checked from behind in any manner. This rule must be strictly enforced."* ⚠️ **A concept search of the flattened, de-hyphenated book on 2 September 2026 found no windup or follow-through carve-out** — *windup*, *wind-up*, *wind up*, *shooting motion*, *backswing*, *back swing*, *follow through*, *follow-through*, *accidental*, *unintentional* and *inadvertent* were each searched; the single *"wind up"* is the glossary's slap-shot definition, and *"accidental"* appears in Rule 62 only to withhold the game misconduct, never to withhold the major. ⚠️ **Citation-resolution note, because this one is likely to be re-found and read as a contradiction:** a search of `carha.txt` for *waist* hits **62(a)**, and that is **not** a waist floor on the contact rule and does not falsify the statement above that the waist floor sits in the NHL book and the IIHF 2026/27 one and nowhere else. 62(a) is a carry threshold for a different offence — checking or intimidating while carrying the stick above the opponent's waist, no contact required. ⚠️ **CARHA binds CARHA-affiliated adult leagues only** and is not a general rec-hockey standard; it is cited here because it is harsher than any of the four on this one point and because many of this document's readers play adult recreational hockey. Nothing else in this document rests on it, and it has not been read in full*

*Helmets in an altercation — all four books, read 1 September 2026: [NHL Official Rules 2025-2026 (PDF)](https://media.d3.nhle.com/image/private/t_document/prd/slwjuaqwmuvj5bkplixo.pdf) — **Rule 46.6 Helmets** and **Rule 9.6**, with **51.1 and 51.2** · [IIHF Official Rulebook 2025/26 (Version 1.1)](https://blob.iihf.com/iihf-media/iihfmvc/media/contentimages/3_the_iihf/2025-26_iihf_rulebook_22122025-v1.pdf) — **Rule 46.12 Helmets**, with **51.1 and 51.2** · [IIHF Official Rulebook 2026/27 (Version 1.0, June 2026)](https://blob.iihf.com/iihf-media/iihfmvc/media/downloads/rule%20book/2026-27_iihf_rule_book.pdf) — **Rule 46.6 Helmets**, the same clause renumbered, differing only in reading *"a two minute minor penalty"* where the 2025/26 text reads *"a minor penalty"*; **Rule 51 is word for word identical in the two editions**, compared 1 September 2026 · [USA Hockey 2025-29 Official Playing Rules](https://cdn2.sportngin.com/attachments/document/945a-3442848/2025-29_USAH_Playing_Rules.pdf) — **Rule 615(c)**, read in full: two match penalties, the game misconduct with the two-game suspension, and the game misconduct for a helmet not worn as designed. **Rule 640 Unnecessary Roughness was searched for a helmet clause and has none** · [USA Hockey Official Rules and Casebook 2025-29 (PDF)](https://cdn3.sportngin.com/attachments/document/4f1a-3442850/2025-29_USAH_Playing_Rules___Casebook.pdf) — **Rule 615, Situations 12 to 21** and the **Note applying to Situations 14-20**, read in full. **The Casebook's printed Rule 615 runs (a) to (f) exactly as the Playing Rules volume does and its situations cite the same letters**, so the one-letter offset that affects Rule 610 does not affect 615 — checked 1 September 2026. Nothing here rests on the book's Summary of Penalties, whose two 615(c) entries disagree with each other (*"Match Penalty Option"* against *"Match Penalty (only)"*) · [Hockey Canada Playing Rules 2026-2028 (PDF)](https://cdn.hockeycanada.ca/hockey-canada/Hockey-Programs/Officiating/Downloads/2026-28-hc-rulebook-e.pdf) — **Rule 10.6 Illegal Equipment** (10.6(d), 10.6(f)(iii) and (iv), and **Interpretation 15 to Rule 10.6(f)(iii)(iv)**), with **Rule 4.9(a) and 4.9(b)** for what a Gross Misconduct is. **Rules 7.9 Roughing and 7.10 Fighting were read for a helmet clause and have none.** The book prints that interpretation's heading as *"Interpretation 15 Rule 10.6 (f)(iii)(iv)"*; its first ruling is quoted above in paraphrase because the extracted text reads *"came of"* rather than *"came off"*, and no clean copy was available to settle whether that is the book's typo or the extraction's.*

*Boards safety — the three-case rule in §6 is quoted verbatim from: [USA Hockey — Introduction To Body Contact (Coaching Education Program, PDF)](https://cdn4.sportngin.com/attachments/document/55c1-2114751/IntroductiontoBodyContact.pdf) — "Contact Confidence": A. Checked Into The Boards, B. Checked Along The Boards, C. Checked Away From The Boards; and the Heads Up Hockey appendix: "Angling In", "Hitting the Boards", "Taking a Check" · [USA Hockey — Heads Up Hockey Program Guide (PDF)](https://cdn4.sportngin.com/attachments/document/0138/6193/HUH_Program_Guide_FINAL.pdf) — "How spinal injuries happen in hockey" (cervical flexion straightening the curve; "it can occur at walking speed"), citing Charles H. Tator et al., *Spinal Injuries Due To Hockey*, Canadian Journal of Neurological Sciences 11:34–41; Session 5, "Giving A Check" (the quote sits under its "Checking from behind" subheading, which is not the session's title), for *"Even a light hit from behind could inflict a severe head or neck injury"*, carried in the same words by *Introduction To Body Contact* §5 "Checking From Behind"; and "Identifying and dealing with a spinal injury", carried in condensed and near-identical form in the *Introduction To Body Contact* Heads Up Hockey appendix — that appendix has no such heading, and its wording differs in small ways ("get help" for "your immediate role is to get help", "arms or legs" for "arms and/or legs", "arms and legs" for "arms or legs"). The quotations in §6 follow the Program Guide. Both PDFs refetched and every string quoted here located in the extracted text on 31 July 2026. **Note the division of labour between the two quotes:** the walking-speed finding is USA Hockey's statement about *"the head being slightly flexed (head down) while making head-on contact with the boards or goal post"* — a head-down, head-first collision — and the guide does **not** attach it to a player turning their back. The hazard of the turned back is that it makes the contact a hit from behind, which is the sentence quoted for it. The walking-speed sentence appears in the Program Guide only; it is not in *Introduction To Body Contact*. **Note:** the same guide's Grade 1/2/3 concussion table (Colorado Medical Society, 1991) is obsolete and is deliberately not used anywhere in this guide — for concussion, follow CRT6 and the Amsterdam consensus via [Conditioning and Recovery](../off-the-ice/conditioning_and_recovery.md). · The body-play progression in §5 is from *Introduction To Body Contact*, "A Misunderstanding of Body Checking" (skating → angling → stick checks → contact confidence → body blocking → take out/rub out → solid body checks), and the one-stride open-ice constraint from its "Shoulder Check – open ice contact" entry **The goal-post material in §9 rests on the same two documents, which name the post beside the boards throughout.** The Program Guide's "How spinal injuries happen in hockey" carries it in both quoted sentences — the mechanism, *"when a player hits the boards or a goal post head on"*, and the finding, *"head-on contact with the boards or goal post"* — and its Session 3 is headed *"Good ways and bad ways to have a close encounter with a goal post, the boards, or another player."* The one-line instruction *"Hit the boards or goal posts with an arm, a leg, or anything but your head first"* is printed in *Introduction To Body Contact* under "Basic Principles of Heads Up Hockey" as well as by Look Up Line, and §9 attributes it to the former. The Program Guide separately lists *"[a]voiding goal posts while driving to the net"*, but prints it as one of the answers it expects players to give when asked what safe hockey means rather than as an instruction of its own, so nothing here rests on it. All strings located in the extracted text on 1 September 2026. · [Look Up Line — Heads Up, Don't Duck](https://lookupline.org/heads-up-dont-duck/) · [Hockey Equipment Certification Council — Heads Up, Don't Duck!](http://hecc.org/headsup.html)*

*Concussion and the on-ice spinal-injury response: [Concussion Recognition Tool 6 (CRT6), Echemendia RJ et al., Br J Sports Med 2023;57:692-694 (doi:10.1136/bjsports-2023-107021)](https://bjsm.bmj.com/content/57/11/692) — the source of the three "Remember" box instructions quoted in the spinal-injury section: "Do not attempt to move the athlete (other than required for airway support) unless trained to do so", "Do not remove helmet (if present) or other equipment", and "Assume a possible spinal cord injury in all cases of head injury". ⚠️ **Do not try to search the published two-page PDF for these — it is not a scan, and that is what makes it deceptive.** It is an InDesign-produced file carrying a real, embedded text layer that holds none of the tool's own words: a text extraction returns about 1.5 KB of BMJ stamp and page furniture and nothing else, so a search for *helmet* comes back empty while the instruction sits plainly printed on the page. All three quotations above were located by rendering the pages and reading them by eye, 31 August 2026. Developed by the Concussion in Sport Group, and its own "Supported by" line names the International Olympic Committee, FEI, FIA, FIFA, the IIHF and World Rugby. The USA Hockey material cited above carries no helmet instruction of any kind — searched flattened and de-hyphenated across both coaching manuals, the Playing Rules and the Casebook. [Conditioning and Recovery](../off-the-ice/conditioning_and_recovery.md) owns the ten red flags and the hours afterwards.*
