*Coaching and technique: [USA Hockey — Winning in Transition: Angling, Gaps and Creating Turnovers in Today's Game](https://www.usahockey.com/news_article/show/988313-winning-in-transition-angling-gaps-and-creating-turnovers-in-today-s-game) (Seth Appert) · [USA Hockey — Body Contact Should Be Taught at Every Level](https://www.usahockey.com/news_article/show/904873-body-contact-should-be-taught-at-every-level) (Guy Gosselin) · [USA Hockey — Body Contact: The (Sometimes) Forgotten Element of Skill Development](https://www.usahockey.com/news_article/show/453192-body-contact-the-sometimes-forgotten-element-of-skill-development) (Ken Klee) · [USA Hockey — Clinic Teaches Players and Coaches How to Use Body Contact Safely and Effectively](https://www.usahockey.com/news_article/show/873887-clinic-teaches-players-and-coaches-how-to-use-body-contact-safely-and-effectively) (Dan Jablonic; "Heads Up, Don't Duck") · [USA Hockey — Michigan Clinics Emphasize the Skills of Proper Body-Checking](https://www.usahockey.com/news_article/show/881483-michigan-clinics-emphasize-the-skills-of-proper-body-checking) (Bob Mancini) · [USA Hockey — Net-Front Presence with Alabama-Huntsville's Mike Corbett](https://www.usahockey.com/news_article/show/775914-net-front-presence-with-alabama-huntsville-s-mike-corbett) · [USA Hockey — Winning Battles Essential to Winning Games](https://www.usahockey.com/news_article/show/494762-winning-battles-essential-to-winning-games) (Michele Amidon) · [USA Hockey — Puck Protection: Tips for Adults](https://www.usahockey.com/news_article/show/1168288-puck-protection-tips-for-adults) (Blaine Buterbaugh) · [How To Hockey — Tips to Safely Block a Shot](https://howtohockey.com/tips-to-safely-block-a-shot-in-hockey/) · [How To Hockey — Playing the Angles in Defense](https://howtohockey.com/playing-the-angles-in-defense/) · [How To Hockey — How to Hit Properly](https://howtohockey.com/how-to-hit-properly/) · [CoachThem — How to Defend With Angling (Danny Heath)](https://coachthem.com/blog/how-to-defend-with-angling-danny-heath) (the don't-cross-over-as-you-close point in §2)*

*Penalty-location data: [NHL public play-by-play API](https://api-web.nhle.com/v1/gamecenter/2024020001/play-by-play) — `details.descKey`, `details.xCoord`, `details.yCoord` for every penalty; the §9 figures aggregate game IDs 2024020001–2024020400 (2,967 penalties, 265 inside the net-front box — the box being 10 to 25 feet out from the goal line, i.e. 64 ≤ |x| ≤ 79, and within 10 feet either side of centre, |y| ≤ 10, in the API's own foot-based rink coordinates). Coordinates are as logged by officials and are approximate, and 400 games is not a full season.*

*Conditioning against penalty minutes — the §12 figures: [Haugen T, Hopkins W, Breitschädel F, Paulsen G, Solberg P — "Fitness Tests and Match Performance in a Male Ice Hockey National League", Int J Sports Physiol Perform, 2021;16(9):1303–1310 (PMID 33662926, doi:10.1123/ijspp.2020-0644)](https://vuir.vu.edu.au/44542/1/Fitness_tests%20_and_match_performance_in_a_male_ice_hockey_national_league.pdf). ⚠️ **The link is the free accepted manuscript in Victoria University's research repository, not the published article** — Human Kinetics puts the version of record behind a subscription. The record and the abstract were read from the Europe PMC REST API on 2 September 2026, and the abstract was confirmed the same day in the rendered text of the Human Kinetics article page the DOI resolves to. **The correlations below are Table 1 of the accepted manuscript**, extracted with pdftotext on 2 September 2026; the version of record's full text sits behind that subscription and has not been read here. Penalty minutes per game against each test measure, all players 2008–2017, goalies excluded: age 0.25, body mass index 0.16, squat 0.14, bench press 0.23, 40-metre sprint −0.04, countermovement jump 0.05, hanging sit-ups 0.07, chin-ups 0.00, box jumps −0.09, 3000-metre run −0.01. The paper's own thresholds put anything under ±0.10 at trivial, and it gives 90% compatibility limits of about ±0.11 for the game-against-test correlations, so every endurance value above is inside the noise. ⚠️ **Three limits on how far this can be pushed.** The penalty-minute correlations appear only in Table 1 and are not discussed anywhere in the text; the study's own conclusion is about predicting match performance, not about penalties. The paper's Methods paragraph lists what was downloaded from the Norwegian Ice-Hockey Association's site as games played, goals, assists, points, plus-minus and national-team selection — **penalty minutes is named in the abstract and in Table 1 but omitted from that enumeration.** And the design is between-player and season-level, so it cannot speak to within-shift fatigue, which is the mechanism §12 describes. The search behind the negative, run against Europe PMC on 2 September 2026 with field-qualified phrases rather than the loose default parser, and with every hit read: `ABSTRACT:"penalty minutes" AND ABSTRACT:"fitness"` returns this paper alone; `TITLE_ABS:"penalties" AND TITLE_ABS:"fatigue" AND TITLE_ABS:"hockey"` returns nothing; `TITLE_ABS:"penalty minutes" AND TITLE_ABS:"ice hockey"` returns nine, of which the rest are about aggression, personality, jerseys, facial metrics or injury risk rather than conditioning. **The nearest adjacent finding, carried here only to bound the negative and not relied on anywhere in the text:** [Acquin JM, Giguère-Lemieux E, Deschamps A, Glaude-Roy J, Brunelle JF, Fait P, Corbin-Berrigan LA — "Baseline Neurotracker performance does not correlate to overall season sport performance in university varsity athletes", Front Sports Act Living, 2025;7:1608463 (PMID 41234217, doi:10.3389/fspor.2025.1608463)](https://europepmc.org/articles/PMC12605341) found that in its 34 male hockey players, a pre-season multiple-object-tracking score correlated negatively with **total penalty minutes at r = −0.375 and games played at r = −0.381**, both p < 0.05, and with nothing else it measured; its conclusion is that the tool has no use as a performance predictor. ⚠️ **Read that one against its own tables, because its abstract does not match them — and it does not match them in two places, not one.** The abstract reports a significant *penalty minutes per game* correlation of −0.39; the paper's Table 3 gives that figure as **−0.28 at p = 0.11, which is not significant** — the two significant columns are games played and total penalty minutes, and games played is the one the abstract omits. **The abstract also disagrees about *total* penalty minutes**, where it gives −0.40, the Results text gives −0.375 and Table 3 gives −0.38. The last two are one value at two precisions; the abstract's is neither of them. **The figures quoted above are the Results text's, not the abstract's.** ⚠️ And the mechanism the paper offers for the penalty finding is **not its own**: it credits it to Poltavski and Biberdorf, its reference 16, which has not been read here. This is a rested perceptual-cognitive baseline in 34 players, not conditioning and not fatigue, so it is carried here only to bound the negative above and nothing in this guide rests on it.*

*Leverage and stance (nearest quantification, non-hockey): [Green A, Coopoo Y, Tee J, McKinon W — "A review of the biomechanical determinants of rugby scrummaging performance", S Afr J Sports Med, 2019;31(1) (PMID 36817990)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36817990/) — **open-access full text read via Europe PMC (PMC9924573) on 28 July 2026**; note that the hip/knee/ankle correlations quoted in §5 are the review's reference [13], **not the review's own data** · [Wu WL, Chang JJ, Wu JH, et al. — "An investigation of rugby scrummaging posture and individual maximum pushing force", J Strength Cond Res, 2007;21(1):251–258](https://doi.org/10.1519/00124278-200702000-00045) — **the actual source of r=−0.47 / −0.51 / −0.70** · [Green A, Kerr S, Dafkin C, Olivier B, McKinon W — "A lower body height and wider foot stance are positively associated with the generation of individual scrummaging forces in rugby", Int J Perform Anal Sport, 2017;17(1–2):177–189, doi:10.1080/24748668.2017.1309094](https://doi.org/10.1080/24748668.2017.1309094)*

*Fall biomechanics (cited only to bound the locked-arm claim, not as hockey evidence): [Chou PH, Chou YL, Lin CJ, et al. — "Effect of elbow flexion on upper extremity impact forces during a fall", Clinical Biomechanics, 2001;16(10):888–894 (PMID 11733126)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/11733126/) · [Morrissette C, Park PJ, Lehman RA, Popkin CA — "Cervical Spine Injuries in the Ice Hockey Player", Global Spine Journal, 2021;11(8):1299–1306 (PMID 33203240)](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33203240/)*

*Unverified or single-source, flagged inline: the warning that bracing a locked, straight arm against the boards breaks wrists and collarbones appears in no USA Hockey, HECC or Look Up Line document, and USA Hockey positively teaches spreading your stick and gloves onto the glass; it is retained in §6 as labelled coaching craft. The nearest evidence is fall biomechanics rather than hockey: Chou and colleagues measured elbow mediolateral shear 68% higher in extension than flexion during forward falls onto the hand, but found no axial-force difference, and no study has applied that to board collisions. **On being hit from behind**, USA Hockey does publish a receiving-player body position for being checked while facing the boards, and its *Heads Up Hockey* guide tells a player who "can't skate through a check" to keep "arms up, knees bent and, of course, their heads up." No published treatment gives a technique premised on genuinely zero warning; beyond that the hazard is addressed through the checker's obligations and the victim's awareness. **The strong form of the cross-checking claim is not supported by the call data:** over 400 games of the 2024-25 season, cross-checking is the **seventh** most-called penalty at the net front rather than the first, and at **1.08×** it is called there at essentially its league-wide rate rather than being concentrated there at all; the penalties that genuinely concentrate in that box are hooking (2.10×) and slashing (1.76×). Those figures and the full method are set out in §9. USA Hockey's Standard of Play naming the net-front cross-check as its leading example remains an enforcement emphasis, which is how it is presented. The "over 70 per cent of goals scored within two stick-lengths of the net" figure is a single coach's account of one season at one NCAA programme, not a league-wide statistic — and the same play-by-play feed contradicts it at NHL level: over 400 games of 2024-25 the true share within two stick-lengths is **26.8%**, with 70% reached only at about thirty feet. It is retained in §9 as a quotation with the contradiction stated beside it, rather than deleted, and is not offered here as a statistic. The USA Hockey interview was refetched on 28 August 2026 and the quoted sentence located in the rendered page; **the two claims §9 used to open with are not Corbett's.** He says nothing about penalties anywhere in that interview, and he describes his own team's season rather than where goals are scored in general. **"Most penalties are taken" at the net front was also contradicted by this document's own call data** — 265 of 2,967 penalties fall inside the net-front box — so it is not stated any more; the box's real signature is hooking and slashing, which is set out in the table. The scale point is separate: the crease front is a sub-area of the slot, and the claim that the **slot** takes most goals belongs to [Shooting](shooting.md), with the Sportlogiq limits that document carries. Hockey Canada's checking-skills coaching resources (as distinct from its rulebook) are not publicly available, so all Hockey Canada claims here are sourced to the Playing Rules — and **Rule 7.4 does carry the two-stride charging test** in both the 2024-2026 and 2026-2028 editions. NHL rule numbering and wording here are taken from the NHL Official Rules 2025-2026. **The British body-checking age is stated here as unknown, deliberately.** The IIHF Rule Book 2025/26 was searched in full for any age or category restriction on body checking and contains none outside women's Rule 101.1; its junior section (Rules 200–202) covers age eligibility, facial protection and one penalty rule and says nothing about contact. The In-House Rules 2025-26 Rev 1 were read in full and contain no reference to body checking, body contact or non-contact play in any section. This guide therefore does **not** state a British threshold and does **not** assume the USA Hockey 12U or Hockey Canada U13 line transfers; the answer lives in individual competition regulations and Rule Bulletins, which have not been obtained. The **EIHL Casebook** has not been read either, and nothing here describes Elite League interpretation. **On the net-front lift in §9, no penalty is asserted and the caution is coaching caution.** A whitespace-flattened whole-book search of the USA Hockey Playing Rules, the USA Hockey Casebook and the Hockey Canada Playing Rules on 1 September 2026 returns no clause about extending the body upward or outward: the only *"outward"* in either book is the knee, under kneeing (USA Hockey Rule 628, Hockey Canada Rule 7.8), and Hockey Canada's two instances of *"upwards"* are a slash and a cross-check deflecting into the head. **The NHL is the fourth book and it does write one**, at **Rule 48.1(i)**, read 1 September 2026 — a hit is judged on, among other things, *"whether the player attempted to hit squarely through the opponent's body and the head was not 'picked' as a result of poor timing, poor angle of approach, or unnecessary extension of the body upward or outward"* — which is the clause §5 quotes. **IIHF Rule 48.1(III)** is its counterpart, word for word identical in the 2025/26 (Version 1.1) and 2026/27 editions, compared 1 September 2026. ⚠️ **Neither reaches a box-out**: both sit inside the illegal check to the head or neck, the NHL's opening on *"[a] hit resulting in contact with an opponent's head"* and the IIHF's chapeau confining its list to *"when a Player checks an opponent"*. So the negative that matters is the narrow one — **no book here writes a penalty for the lift on its own** — and not a claim that only one book names the extension. **The direction to walk a net-front opponent toward the corner rather than across the goalmouth is likewise coaching caution drawn from the sourced hazard, not a rule any book writes.** What the books do write is the goal frame beside the boards in checking from behind (USA Hockey 608(b), Hockey Canada 7.5(c), IIHF 43.1) and in charging (NHL 42.1, IIHF 42.1, Hockey Canada 7.4, in identical words; USA Hockey's Rule 607 Note names neither), all read 1 September 2026 — with **NHL 43.2** and **IIHF 43.2** both read the same day for the no-minor sentence, the NHL's *"[t]here is no provision for a minor penalty for checking from behind"* and the IIHF's *"[t]his infraction is considered serious and dangerous, therefore there is no option to award a minor penalty for 'Checking from Behind'"*, the extracted IIHF text hyphenating the last word across a line break. **No injury rate for net-front or goal-post collisions is given anywhere in this document, because no study giving one was found in this corpus or its sources.***
